[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 0.6 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"88cb0e","wall_time_seconds":0.3776136,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":19020,"output":"\\r\\nFILE training-camp\\\\translation\\\\Take a Guess at Words You Dont Know • LO.md\\r\\n---\\r\\ntitle: \\"Take a Guess at Words You Dont Know • LO\\"\\r\\nsource: \\"[url]\\r\\nauthor:\\r\\npublished:\\r\\ncreated: 2026-08-29\\r\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\r\\ntags:\\r\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\r\\n---\\r\\n## Take a Guess at Words You Don\'t Know\\r\\n0:14\\r\\n/\\r\\n6:58\\r\\n- 0:00Take a guess at words you don\'t know. This is so important because I\'m going to outline for you a problem that I see a lot when people go through translating and they get to a word that they do not know.\\r\\n- 0:11When people are reading and they see a word they don\'t know, very often what they will do is just glaze over that word and keep on going. And they will then allow the not knowing of that one word to morph into an ever-expanding black hole that surrounds the surrounding ideas.\\r\\n- 0:31And now there\'s a big black hole in the middle of the sentence and the stuff on the edge of the word you don\'t know is now being encompassed by it as like, oh, well, I just don\'t know what that means.\\r\\n- 0:39I\'m, and I don\'t know that word, so I\'m not going to know what it means. And what they want to do is keep on reading, just blowing past this word they don\'t know, and hoping that later on the rest of the text will help me understand what that word meant.\\r\\n- 0:53So let me tell you why this doesn\'t work and why it\'s harmful. Because when you allow that word you don\'t know to permeate and become this big black hole that like, oh, I didn\'t know that word, so now I don\'t know anything really surrounding it either, there tends to be an avoidant mentality\\r\\n- 1:09that accompanies that. That people generally feel uncomfortable acknowledging that there is a word they don\'t know in the moment and so they don\'t really want to sit in that kind of icky, uncomfortable feeling and so they want to move forward. They want to get away from it.\\r\\n- 1:23And they tell themselves this little white lie of, oh, I\'m keeping on going because I don\'t have time to know what this means and reading more, maybe it\'ll help me know what it means.\\r\\n- 1:32The truth of the matter is you do not actually go back to that word and figure out what it means.\\r\\n- 1:36You just continue on and try and pretend that it isn\'t there. And the later paragraphs or whatever it is, they do not actually tell you what the word meant and you shouldn\'t pretend that actually going on is really in service of knowing what the word meant because it isn\'t\\r\\n- 1:54actually true. That then there is this opening in the passage where, and of course this could be a stimulus too, so even though I\'m saying passage, this also applies to LR, but there\'s this opening and then if an answer choice is playing on anything associated with that opening, it\'s\\r\\n- 2:10a big gap that can potentially create wrong answers. So let me tell you what to do instead. If you do get to a word that you do not know, I want that to instead of creating an avoidant mentality, I want it to create a mentality of attraction, curiosity, fixation,\\r\\n- 2:24that when you see that word, you\'re like, hmm, I\'m not sure about that. OK, you\'re allowed to read like to the end of the sentence, the end of the thought for sure and then but when you get to the end of that sentence, I want you to go back and I want you to take an educated guess on what that\\r\\n- 2:37word means. You cannot leave it open as just a blank. This is the biggest mistake that people make. And so, OK, I\'ve read the sentence, I go and I take a guess.\\r\\n- 2:46Now, how often would you surmise that that guess that people take is actually correct? Because I will tell you in my observations of many, many students over many, many years, it\'s right probably like 90 plus percent of the time.\\r\\n- 3:00When people take a guess, they\'re almost always correct. And of course, if they are correct and they run with that guess, wonderful.\\r\\n- 3:09If they\'re incorrect and they run with their guess, OK, they have an inaccuracy in terms of what this word meant. Maybe it\'s a lot bit off, but they are much more likely to find success with the wrong understanding of a single word, but the correct\\r\\n- 3:22understanding of surrounding words and no avoidance of the information in that overall sentence. They\'re much, much better off in terms of their ability to get a question right. That may follow that is either based or not based on that information.\\r\\n- 3:34And so it is better, even if you guess wrong, to buy into your guess and use it moving forward because it\'s not enough to simply take a guess at the word you don\'t know. You then have to use that guess as though it is right and lock in on that meaning and use it going forward in your answering\\r\\n- 3:54of the question. I know this might be challenging, but challenge yourself to do it because it actually really does yield return. But this also leans into our idea of we want to create a curiosity about things we don\'t understand rather than avoidance, right?\\r\\n- 4:07Curiosity not avoidance. So one more thing about these words you don\'t know and how you are going to use them after the section is over.\\r\\n- 4:13So be sure that when you\'re in section and you come across a word you don\'t know like this that you highlight it.\\r\\n- 4:19And then at the end of the section, I want you to go back and harvest all of those highlighted words into your\\r\\n- 4:25vocab tracker so you can build your customized vocab list and actually start to drill these words. I know that this is like the really unsexy part of LSAT prep where it\'s like, oh, well, Ellen, you know, who\'s even going to say that that word\'s going to come up again?\\r\\n- 4:38Yeah, but who\'s going to say it doesn\'t? And I don\'t think it\'s really that big of a deal to harvest a word and put it in a vocab tracker and drill those words.\\r\\n- 4:48It\'s going to help you in law school. It\'s going to help you in your general life to expand your vocabulary overall.\\r\\n- 4:52Like you get the benefit of just having a better lexicon that aids you in your thinking, writing, reading all throughout your life.\\r\\n- 5:00Like this is all just value add. So even if the word doesn\'t come up on the LSAT, you are better served for knowing it.\\r\\n- 5:05But also, like we want to generally approach this with the mindset of expansiveness, you know, that we don\'t want to do the least\\r\\n- 5:13we want to do the most. And so the fact that there even is a chance that this word would come up on a future exam is enough to cover that base\\r\\n- 5:19because it\'s not that expensive to go and put the word in a vocab tracker and then go on to drill that vocab. And of course, I have other videos about all the idiosyncrasies of how to learn vocab with AI, not with AI, all that different kind of stuff.\\r\\n- 5:31And so definitely avail yourself of those videos as well. I just want to point out that when you don\'t know what a word means, that is a flag telling you this is a place where I can be better.\\r\\n- 5:40I need to study this. And there are so few parts of the LSAT that really like directly benefit from pure memorization.\\r\\n- 5:48You know, there\'s things like conditional indicators, premise, Conclusion indicators, question stems, all that kind of stuff. And that\'s like wonderful. And you should memorize that stuff.\\r\\n- 5:53But all of the vocab that you might not initially understand, that is an opportunity for you as well to just through pure memorizing the kind of stuff you would do on a normal exam to actually uplift your score, which is really fantastic.\\r\\n- 6:05And also, you don\'t have to get this exact word again in order for it to raise your score next time,\\r\\n- 6:12because if you learn this one word that you didn\'t know really well, you can then break it down into its component roots and start to memorize similarities and differences of let\'s say this word is presuppose\\r\\n- 6:22and like you maybe you didn\'t you didn\'t know pre exist either. But presuppose, you know, is to suppose something before it happens.\\r\\n- 6:30And now you know that pre is a prefix that means before and then you see pre exist next time. You\'re like, OK, I know what exists means.\\r\\n- 6:37I know pre exist is not that word of a word. So don\'t come for me.\\r\\n- 6:41But you can break those words apart and actually get a lot of benefit out of them, even on associated root words as well. And\\r\\n- 6:49so I hope that this helps. Be patient with yourself. It is OK to expand your vocab in this process.\\r\\n- 6:54And I look forward to your happy guessing. All right. See you soon.\\r\\n##### Day 4\\r\\n### Take Notes\\r\\n#\\r\\n\\r\\nFILE training-camp\\\\translation\\\\Translate Prove You Were Listening • LO.md\\r\\n---\\r\\ntitle: \\"Translate Prove You Were Listening • LO\\"\\r\\nsource: \\"[url]\\r\\nauthor:\\r\\npublished:\\r\\ncreated: 2026-08-27\\r\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\r\\ntags:\\r\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\r\\n---\\r\\n## Translate: Prove You Were Listening\\r\\n0:10\\r\\n/\\r\\n6:17\\r\\n- 0:00Translation, right? This is the bread and butter of the whole thing. And so, alright, how do you translate? Well, we\'ve just gotten out of our read stage, right?\\r\\n- 0:10We\'ve just read, we\'ve beautifully intoned. Okay, great. And now one key thing, you are not going to read during your Translation stage, right?\\r\\n- 0:18Because in the read stage, you\'re only reading. In the Translation stage, you\'re only translating. You will not be rereading during this stage.\\r\\n- 0:24And so in order to keep yourself honest of actually, like, really not rereading, we\'re going to need you to either close your eyes or look away from the stimulus while you\'re translating, just so that we can build some level of accountability to not rereading during the Translation process.\\r\\n- 0:38And so, like, why is it so important to not reread? Because if you\'re rereading during the Translation process, it\'s kind of like one way street, but two way cars.\\r\\n- 0:46Things get jammed. And essentially, you aren\'t translating. You aren\'t really using your short term memory to engage with what you had just read.\\r\\n- 0:53Instead, you\'re just really reading words again and kind of reading out loud again. But it\'s all jammed and messed up.\\r\\n- 1:00And so it messes up the Translation stage. And it also makes you translate in their exact words way more often than is optimal.\\r\\n- 1:05Because in the Translation stage, you really want to translate in your own words. You want to tell your own story.\\r\\n- 1:11And that means that you are allowed to say the ideas out of order. You\'re allowed to say them in a more casual way.\\r\\n- 1:17You\'re allowed to laugh while you\'re doing it. It\'s meant to be your own imbibed version of the text.\\r\\n- 1:25And, you know, I love this one saying, I think one of my students told me this once, that their definition of intelligence is the ability to say complex things in a simple way.\\r\\n- 1:34And I was like, oh, you\'re so smart. But I really think that is the essence of the Translation process as well.\\r\\n- 1:42Because when you\'re translating text on the else, that very often that text is going to be quite gnarly, snarly, or early. You know, it\'s going to be all messed up.\\r\\n- 1:50There\'s going to be a lot of kind of fancy words in it. And your job is to dress it down to size a little bit, because when you translate, you\'re saying it in your own words, in your own language, like you\'re telling a story to a friend.\\r\\n- 2:02And that probably is not going to necessarily use all the exact same fancy sentence structures, all the exact same five dollar words, because you\'re making it your own.\\r\\n- 2:12You\'re taking a complex set of complex text and you\'re making it simple. But one really key thing in making it simple, that\'s not the same thing as cutting out information. Because you really need to make sure that you have all of the conceptual details of the text present in your Translation.\\r\\n- 2:31And what does that mean? It means that we need to remove your filter. We we don\'t just translate the gist.\\r\\n- 2:37We don\'t just translate the summary. We don\'t just translate the important parts. All of that stuff is impossible nonsense, because here here\'s the big secret.\\r\\n- 2:46It\'s impossible to know what the important parts of a piece of text are before you\'ve read it. Right. And really, that read stages when you\'re bringing the information in.\\r\\n- 2:55And so if you\'re going to filter stuff out, like it would have to be, you know, not really coming in the door when it\'s first coming in.\\r\\n- 3:02And then in the Translation stage, the reason you wouldn\'t translate it is because it\'s not there to translate. You want to translate all of the information that you have in your brain.\\r\\n- 3:09You don\'t want to efficientize it and, you know, get rid of various stuff because that\'s not a Translation. That\'s something else.\\r\\n- 3:17And the LSAT is optimized for Translation. It\'s not optimized for gist, for summary, for getting rid of the list, for only the important parts, yada, yada, yada down the line.\\r\\n- 3:26That\'s not what Translation is. And, you know, I don\'t think that doing it will be very successful that way.\\r\\n- 3:32And so I don\'t want you to do it that way. But it\'s kind of like in our therapist analogy, you know, if you\'re a therapist and you\'re proving that you are active, listening to your patient, your patients is speaking to you. And you\'re listening, of course, that\'s like a read stage.\\r\\n- 3:44And then you say back to the patient, OK, what I\'m hearing from you is da da da da da da. And if you cut out a bunch of what that patient said, you know, if they said, oh, the the floral arrangements of my wedding are giving me such trouble.\\r\\n- 3:58I don\'t know where that came from. And you just go, OK, so the arrangements for your wedding are giving you trouble and you cut out that adjective.\\r\\n- 4:06Do you think your patients really going to think you were listening to them? They won\'t. Right. And I like in Translation to that moment when you\'re in a job interview and you have to say back, you know, you have to have some kind of like, OK, so here\'s what you want me to do.\\r\\n- 4:21You want me to do that and say all of the stuff that you were just asked to do. All right. And now I\'m going to do it for you.\\r\\n- 4:26You say you\'re doing some kind of job test. I know that you need to show an exhaustive understanding of the information.\\r\\n- 4:34And one more thing about understanding very often, people will not translate the entirety of what they read. They\'ll cut out the adjectives, they\'ll cut out the details, all that different kind of stuff.\\r\\n- 4:44And they will defend this by saying, oh, but I understood it. And and that\'s wonderful. And, you know, I respect you and that great.\\r\\n- 4:53But if you understand it, then we need to ask, OK, is that enough? And I would say it\'s not because understanding is necessary for everything and sufficient for nothing.\\r\\n- 5:06If you cannot use your understanding to actually go and take ideas apart, to explain ideas, to work with ideas, unpack them, look inside them, shine a spotlight around them.\\r\\n- 5:16If you can\'t actually do that with the ideas that you\'re understanding. Well, the understanding is nice and everything, but it kind of dies on the vine. Right.\\r\\n- 5:26I mean, it doesn\'t really get us to the place that we\'re trying to go. And so even if you do understand it, we want to move that understanding into an action stage\\r\\n- 5:36because that action stage of actually translating it, proving that you really do have all of the information that\'s what gets us closer to getting questions right.\\r\\n- 5:45Because if you really understand it, OK, great. Are you retaining it, though, because retention is really key to have that information that you understood so well still present with you so you can interrelated with the answer choices to know which answer is right and know which answer is wrong.\\r\\n- 6:00And so I want you, as you\'re hearing my Translation demos, to hear how I am not cutting out information\\r\\n- 6:08that is really, really, really, really, really important. All right. I love you. I hope this was helpful. And let\'s get to quipping.\\r\\n##### Day 2\\r\\n### Ellen\'s Notes\\r\\nAnd for those who justly complain on Reddit that I already have too many acronyms in *The Loophole* 😘\\r\\n### Take Notes\\r\\n#\\r\\n\\r\\nFILE training-camp\\\\translation\\\\What If I Cant Think of Anything to Say • LO.md\\r\\n---\\r\\ntitle: \\"What If I Cant Think of Anything to Say • LO\\"\\r\\nsource: \\"[url]\\r\\nauthor:\\r\\npublished:\\r\\ncreated: 2026-08-29\\r\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\r\\ntags:\\r\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\r\\n---\\r\\n## What If I Can\'t Think of Anything to Say?\\r\\nThe moment that it\'s no longer an option to opt out\\r\\n0:05\\r\\n/\\r\\n5:32\\r\\n- 0:00What if I can\'t think of anything to say? Another classic quipping objection. You might be wondering why so many of these videos in the quipping module are about like, I don\'t want to quip.\\r\\n- 0:10And the reason why, it\'s not that I\'m, you know, like personally against you, it\'s just I\'ve had a lot, I have a lot of baggage.\\r\\n- 0:15I\'ve spent the past, I don\'t know, what is it? Six years, five years hearing people tell me they don\'t want to quip\\r\\n- 0:23and, you know, giving various objections and it always comes down to like an interpersonal interaction between me and the student to finally convince them to quip.\\r\\n- 0:32And so I\'m trying to replicate all of those interpersonal interactions, all of my best pleas, all of my like most passionate entreaties.\\r\\n- 0:41I\'m trying to give them all to you right now in video form to replicate that style of interpersonal engagement and, you know, overcome the objections that you may have to this Methodology so that you will come with me on this journey,\\r\\n- 0:54because I will tell you, after I overcome people\'s objections, which like I said, it\'s usually, you know, a conversation that might take a minute or two,\\r\\n- 1:01then we really do head into a much brighter future. I even, I had one of these moments on Monday with someone where she wanted to quip,\\r\\n- 1:09she was, you know, fine and everything. It was just that she was like, I just don\'t know what to say, which is, you know, what leads us to this video.\\r\\n- 1:15This is something I\'ve heard a lot of times over the years, what if I can\'t think of anything to say?\\r\\n- 1:19And I\'ll tell you the classic reasons why people think that and how you can get over it. And so the first one is like, oh, I just can\'t think of anything.\\r\\n- 1:26You can. You can. There are a couple of reasons why you think you can\'t. One, you have some kind of internal time clock in your head for how long you wait before it\'s not worth it anymore.\\r\\n- 1:40Because I guarantee you, if I said to you, you will not get up from this chair until you say something.\\r\\n- 1:46I don\'t care if you sleep in this chair, you will not get up until you say something back to this piece of text that you just read.\\r\\n- 1:53You have to have some sort of reaction to it. The moment that it\'s no longer an option to opt out because you can\'t, you\'ll find a way.\\r\\n- 2:01Trust me, you will. The other reason why people tend to feel like, oh, I can\'t think of anything is they are amending it with I can\'t think of anything worthwhile to say.\\r\\n- 2:11So it\'s really like, I can\'t think of anything in the amount of time I think is reasonable, or I can\'t think of anything worthwhile.\\r\\n- 2:18And that part of worthwhile, I really wanna dive into because I don\'t want you to pre-vet your thoughts for are they good enough before you have the thought. Because the thought itself, and by thought in this case, that would be a quip on a piece of text that you just translated,\\r\\n- 2:37by pre-vetting your thoughts, and this is beyond just the LSAT, before you actually express the thought or allow the thought to see the light of day, breathe, you are limiting your own greatness.\\r\\n- 2:48You are limiting your own creativity. And when people say I\'m just not a creative person, it\'s probably because they are engaging in this sort of self-editing, self-vetting, being like a real Aaron Burr of a situation, say less, smile more, that we wanna say more. And smiling\\r\\n- 3:05is good, but we wanna say more. And generally, there are no wrong answers in improv. And like I\'ve said in some of my previous videos on quipping, quipping is based on that improv mindset of you get a prompt and you just go, you just say it. There are no wrong answers. It\'s a yes and Methodology.\\r\\n- 3:20And so when you\'re in that situation and you\'re like, oh, is this a good enough quip? Is this an okay quip? Cut that nonsense out. Just say the thing and then do it again better next time. You know, like if you say a really non-ideal quip, is the world gonna end?\\r\\n- 3:39Am I even gonna know you did it? I won\'t, because unfortunately, we may not have a real conversation, although I would love to have a real conversation with you. But you are going to be able to go to the next sentence. And as long as you\'re pushing yourself to do better, what I care a lot more about\\r\\n- 3:54is getting good solid reps in where you are trying your absolute best. Because remember, like I said in the previous video, there\'s no such thing as enough, there\'s only your best. And I believe that your best includes actually quipping each sentence with something substantive.\\r\\n- 4:07If you are worried, oh, this isn\'t worthwhile, this isn\'t useful, like whatever information, you are not a good judge of what is useful before you have done the rest of the question.\\r\\n- 4:16You do not know what will or won\'t be useful. And I would claim also that regardless of the substance of that quip that you might not think is very useful, just the habit of actually accessing your subconscious reaction to information creates usefulness by itself.\\r\\n- 4:31Simply engaging in the habit of not questioning yourself before you have thoughts and actually just doing, rather than thinking, that by itself creates usefulness.\\r\\n- 4:40This goes to the idea of letting the eye disappear, which is something I talk about in a different video that I\'ll link to down here. But again, one little hack you could do if you are worried about usefulness is ask a question of the piece of text you just read.\\r\\n- 4:53And then after you\'ve asked that piece of text a question, answer the question yourself. Don\'t just pose questions, because very often that can lead to just rephrasing and putting a question mark at the end of it.\\r\\n- 5:03But instead, if you ask a question, that\'s a great way to get your mind kickstarted, then answer that question immediately after.\\r\\n- 5:11I hope that this helped. (you are not broken)\\r\\nor they will just not have any sub vocalization at all.\\r\\n1:16\\r\\n/\\r\\n6:29\\r\\n- 0:00What is reading and what should it be? What are you really doing?\\r\\n- 0:18And what are your intentions when you are actually doing that act of reading? Like what do you really want to have happen?\\r\\n- 0:25Because very often and when people read they are not necessarily reading to get all the nuances of every single thing that is on the page right\\r\\n- 0:35because there are lots of different intentions that somebody might have when they\'re reading But very very often what you will find is what people call reading is more like some of the following\\r\\n- 0:46I listed out just a couple of habits that I have observed over the years that people do when they read\\r\\n- 0:50they will take on Multiple sentences all at once that they\'re just kind of like looking at them overall. They\'ll scan for proper nouns.\\r\\n- 0:57They\'ll omit connector words They\'ll get the gist. They\'ll omit the details. They\'ll combine the lists into those things They\'ll say okay, you know, and this is something about coins or this is something about boats.\\r\\n- 1:08This is something about light bulbs they\'ll Kind of say the words in their head But it will be in a monotone or they will just not have any sub vocalization at all Vocalization\\r\\n- 1:18means the voice in your head and they will often skip words that they don\'t want to pronounce And so if a word is long, they will just skip it.\\r\\n- 1:27They won\'t actually even really read it They\'ll skip names. They\'ll skip dates. They\'ll skip numbers. Oh, I heart.\\r\\n##### Day 2\\r\\n### Take Notes\\r\\n#\\r\\n\\r\\nFILE training-camp\\\\translation\\\\Whats Rtq • LO.md\\r\\n---\\r\\ntitle: \\"Whats Rtq • LO\\"\\r\\nsource: \\"[url]\\r\\nauthor:\\r\\npublished:\\r\\ncreated: 2026-08-24\\r\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\r\\ntags:\\r\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\r\\n---\\r\\n## What\'s RTQ?\\r\\nIt is a process, an operation\\r\\n0:32\\r\\n/\\r\\n4:31\\r\\n- 0:00What is RTQ? I mean, I have been waiting so long to be able to tell you this. I\'m so excited. OK,\\r\\n- 0:07so RTQ stands for read, translate, quip. And it\'s actually an acronym that one of my students came up with that I now look back and I\'m like, you know, they were right.\\r\\n- 0:17That\'s a that\'s a good way to encapsulate it, because RTQ is basically a unit. You read and you translate and you quip on every piece of text that you enCounter throughout a stimulus, an answer choice, a question to them, a passage, whatever it might be. It is a process, an operation that you perform\\r\\n- 0:34on chunks of text as you go. And so this is something you would be doing instead of writing down the conceptual information in your Translation like we have in the Loophole book.\\r\\n- 0:43It\'s totally fine to start with that writing down of conceptual information as a practice. You begin when you\'re reading a Loophole book.\\r\\n- 0:49It\'s not harmful. It\'s good. It\'s kind of a good like warm up to be able to get us into the RTQ process.\\r\\n- 0:56And the RTQ process is just a little bit more actively engaging throughout the stimulus as opposed to saving it all until the end, which is, you know, interesting because it allows us to do more things.\\r\\n- 1:05And so let me explain just the basics of how it works, and then we\'ll have in-depth videos that go through each step for you next.\\r\\n- 1:11And so RTQ, essentially, let\'s say a sentence is a nice full idea chunk. And that that doesn\'t necessarily mean that you would stop to RTQ at the end of a sentence only. Maybe you have like a comma and you want to have a sentence.\\r\\n- 1:25Maybe it\'s actually, you know, more like breaking it into thirds of a sentence. Typically, I tell people don\'t RTQ combining sentences unless you\'re, you know, very advanced and you feel very, very confident that you absolutely will retain all the conceptual details of everything you\'ve read.\\r\\n- 1:42Because usually when someone combines two sentences, especially when they\'re in the beginner stages, the first sentence\'s details will often be lost. And we don\'t want that to happen.\\r\\n- 1:51And so I often say, try and figure out where you want to break in your read to RTQ. By thinking of texts like this coffee cup, noticing\\r\\n- 1:57all my videos, I always have a coffee cup as a prop. It\'s because they\'re like five on this desk.\\r\\n- 2:02So as you\'re reading, you want to think of words as filling the coffee cup. And the words should go until about here, not here. When you stop to translate,\\r\\n- 2:13meaning you don\'t want to be retaking on so much text at a time that the words are starting to get lost and overflow.\\r\\n- 2:19You want to be a little bit more conservative in your break strategy of like I read, read, read, read, read until I\'m to about here in terms of the amount of words I\'ve taken it. And I\'m like, OK, I know I\'ve got this. I know it\'s not going to spill. I can stop to RTQ.\\r\\n- 2:33And so that would be when you stop, like I said, rounding down instead of rounding up, because if you round up, the chances of lost information are much higher than if you round down.\\r\\n- 2:42Some exceptions being, you know, if you really don\'t have a full idea of what\'s happened yet, you know, that you don\'t have any complete thought. OK, you can take on a little more text. But like I said, be really careful about going beyond one sentence at a time, especially to start.\\r\\n- 2:56But that read stage of the RTQ process, that\'s the first stage. You want to make sure that you\'re fully focusing on your read while you\'re doing it, that you\'re not mixing in other steps, especially in the beginning.\\r\\n- 3:08You want to have full focus on just taking in kind of like a one way street on a road that\'s like we\'re just bringing information into the brain in that read stage. And then the Translation stage is when we say back to ourselves the conceptual information, what we just read. And we\'re going\\r\\n- 3:24to say it in our own words, like we\'re telling a story to a friend. And then in our quip stage, this is the new stuff. This is when we react to what we just read.\\r\\n- 3:34We show curiosity, we show skepticism, we have an independent thought about what we just read, bringing ourselves into an active alignment with the author of the stimulus, having a little bit of a back and forth.\\r\\n- 3:45And that quip stage is super, super, super important to do after every every read read and translate stage, because it keeps you really conceptually alive throughout the stimulus, really active so that you\'re taking the question apart. And\\r\\n- 4:01essentially the quips are where you\'re getting the question right. And so that\'s why it\'s really important because you\'re actively taking the stimulus apart.\\r\\n- 4:08This is when you\'re essentially doing your analytical thinking to enable you to do the work to eventually arrive at the correct answer to the question.\\r\\n- 4:15It\'s kind of like your margin notes on the stimulus as you are going. And so that\'s the basics of RTQ.\\r\\n- 4:22I hope that that helped. We\'re just going to go into a lot more of the details on every individual stage in these next couple of videos.\\r\\n- 4:28So definitely stay tuned. All right. I\'ll see you soon.\\r\\n##### Day 2\\r\\n### Ellen\'s Notes\\r\\nAnd this one is for those who justly complain on Reddit that I already have too many acronyms in *The Loophole* 😘\\r\\n### Take Notes\\r\\n#\\r\\n\\r\\nFILE training-camp\\\\translation\\\\Whats the Translation Standard Were Trying to Hit • LO.md\\r\\n---\\r\\ntitle: \\"Whats the Translation Standard Were Trying to Hit • LO\\"\\r\\nsource: \\"[url]\\r\\nauthor:\\r\\npublished:\\r\\ncreated: 2026-08-29\\r\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\r\\ntags:\\r\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\r\\n---\\r\\n## What\'s the Translation Standard We\'re Trying to Hit?\\r\\n1:23\\r\\n/\\r\\n2:36\\r\\n- 0:00What is the Translation standard we\'re trying to hit? You know, what does good look like? What is the Elysium at the end of the rainbow here?\\r\\n- 0:07It is that when you read and translate a stimulus, you have a 100% conceptual awareness, 100% conceptual retention of all of the information that is presented to you in that stimulus.\\r\\n- 0:19Now, that does not mean that you are literally reciting every word of the stimulus in the exact order it\'s given absolutely verbatim.\\r\\n- 0:27You\'re totally free to translate into your own words using your own language and also to take things out of order.\\r\\n- 0:35But when I say translate things in your own words, I don\'t mean take away the details. What I mean is save the details in your own words.\\r\\n- 0:44And maybe that\'s fewer words because you\'re able to conceptualize the information in a more efficient way, but it actually covers all of the information.\\r\\n- 0:52If that\'s so, that\'s totally fine. But I don\'t want you to take efficiency in your Translation as an excuse to leave information out\\r\\n- 0:59because you need all the concepts in the stimulus to be retained and presented within your Translation. And you wanna retain your entire Translation as you\'re going into the answer choice because you need that information,\\r\\n- 1:10that information interrelated with the information that\'s given to you in the answer choices is how you\'ll know what is correct and what is incorrect.\\r\\n- 1:17But the standard isn\'t just conceptual retention and conceptual awareness, it\'s also the way in which that conceptual awareness is gained.\\r\\n- 1:25Eventually what you want is for Translation and quipping to feel like breathing, feel like water, feel like something that you cannot turn off.\\r\\n- 1:33It\'s just when you read, you are naturally translating without having to volitionally tell yourself that you have to translate. And without having to say, okay, I need to remember this, look for this, remember that, you just do remember it. That is the goal of mastery that we\'re\\r\\n- 1:51hoping to achieve here. When I am going through and reading something, the reason why I didn\'t know Translation existed for so long is because I did not know that I was actually doing all of this.\\r\\n- 2:00It was just that at the end of reading a stimulus, I\'d of course be able to tell you everything that happened because I thought that was just the way people read.\\r\\n- 2:08That\'s what we want for you is that as you\'re going through, all of these things are naturally happening in a subconscious way that form a great process that at the end of the stimulus, you\'re like, yeah, of course I know what it said.\\r\\n- 2:18How could I not? Of course I know what it said. How could I not? Isn\'t that just such a gift of entitlement?\\r\\n- 2:24Of course I know what it said. How could I not? That\'s the standard that we\'re going for at the end of every stimulus in logical reasoning at the end of every passage as well.\\r\\n- 2:35All right, I\'ll see you soon.\\r\\n##### Day 5\\r\\n### Take Notes\\r\\n#\\r\\n\\r\\nFILE training-camp\\\\translation\\\\Why I Expanded Translation Beyond the Loophole Book • LO.md\\r\\n---\\r\\ntitle: \\"Why I Expanded Translation Beyond the Loophole Book • LO\\"\\r\\nsource: \\"[url]\\r\\nauthor:\\r\\npublished:\\r\\ncreated: 2026-08-24\\r\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\r\\ntags:\\r\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\r\\n---\\r\\n## Why I Expanded Translation Beyond the Loophole Book\\r\\nwith even more tutoring students\\r\\n1:25\\r\\n/\\r\\n6:53\\r\\n- 0:00Why did I expand Translation beyond what you read in the Loophole book? Well, because I\'ve been doing a lot of stuff since the Loophole came out.\\r\\n- 0:07I\'ve probably taught the LSAT for over 10,000 hours. And like I actually did the math. It\'s not probably. I have.\\r\\n- 0:14Since the Loophole was published. The Loophole was published at the very end of 2018. And so it hasn\'t actually been that long. I just love what I do.\\r\\n- 0:23I do LSAT a lot all the time, seven days a week. But when the Loophole book was published, it was in 2018. And I discovered Translation overall in around 2015. So I had about three years of working on Translation to finalize the version that you see in the Loophole book, which I still\\r\\n- 0:42endorse. You know, this is not me saying that don\'t do the Translation just in the Loophole book.\\r\\n- 0:47It definitely still has a place in curriculum that I will get to. But I want you to know just that this Methodology of Translation, it has been evolving for literally 10 years at this point.\\r\\n- 0:57And what you see in the Loophole book is the Loophole Translation Method about three years in ish. We\'re in 2025 now, but it\'s beginning of 2025. So that\'s six and a half or whatever years in.\\r\\n- 1:10And we\'ve had a lot of evolution since then. My tutoring students up until the Loophole book were published were absolute wonderful advocates who helped me understand how Translation worked and got me to the place where I could come up with what\'s in the Loophole book.\\r\\n- 1:22It works and it\'s great. But I have been working since with even more tutoring students to get a more expanded universe of what Translation can mean.\\r\\n- 1:30And so the Translation Drill that you do in the Loophole book where you write down the Translations on each stimuli,\\r\\n- 1:36it\'s a wonderful drill to have really high levels of accountability for your own self checks between your self, like writing something down and the actual Translation of the stimulus.\\r\\n- 1:47Because it\'s a written product, it enables you to actually check really well whether you got everything in the stimulus.\\r\\n- 1:54It lets you know that you\'re right. And so part of the reason why that was the version of Translation that went into the Loophole book\\r\\n- 2:00is because I knew that I wasn\'t going to necessarily be able to have a conversation, have more of a like human interaction with every single person who Read The Loophole book.\\r\\n- 2:10Even though through the book, I feel like I am. But I wasn\'t going to be able to have a back and forth conversation to be able to tell how good their Translations were.\\r\\n- 2:19So I needed a process that was going to be able to be checkable by the person doing it. And that\'s a lot of why that written process exists.\\r\\n- 2:27I mean, it also was just the first thing I tried and it worked well enough that I liked it.\\r\\n- 2:32But then after the Loophole book came out, I got a lot more tutoring students and I was you know, there were times in 2019 and 2020 where I was tutoring like. 60 to 70 hours a week, maybe even over 70 hours a week.\\r\\n- 2:47There were times I was pulling like 14 hour days, like not infrequently. I was not somebody who really knew how to run a business.\\r\\n- 2:55I didn\'t really know what I was doing at all. But in that time period of 2019 and 2020, I enCountered some of the pitfalls of Translation when it was written.\\r\\n- 3:06Once you got past to it, like a more advanced level, because as I wanted to up level people even further, I was noticing that there were ways that when people would do it out loud with me in session instead of writing. If they didn\'t have the more expanded universe that you\'re going to\\r\\n- 3:24learn about here, which is intonation and quipping and all that different kind of stuff. What I would notice is sometimes they would say the words or write the words, but I could tell they didn\'t really know what the words signify.\\r\\n- 3:36They didn\'t really understand the deeper meaning and deeper connections between the ideas that the words entailed.\\r\\n- 3:43And so I began experimenting with lots of different things and seeing what worked across all of the wonderful body of tutoring students that I had at the time.\\r\\n- 3:52And like I said, I was tutoring a lot until I had a lot of students that I could very rapidly iterate and test what I was working on in a way that I couldn\'t do before the Loophole book came out.\\r\\n- 4:03And, you know, I had way, way fewer students just because, you know, some chicken palo alto who loved the LSAT. Like people didn\'t really know who I was at all.\\r\\n- 4:10But then all of a sudden, after the Loophole came out, you know, I had way more people who wanted to study with me\\r\\n- 4:14and way more people were really like down and wanted to be the test subject. And so I was able to figure out intonation and quipping in 2019.\\r\\n- 4:25And since then, I have been iterating on that, what I call the RTQ process. It stands for read, translate, quip.\\r\\n- 4:33I\'ve been iterating on the RTQ process since 2019, really dialing it in to make sure that it works the way it should work as a next phase of the Translation process that\'s in the Loophole book.\\r\\n- 4:45Because here in, you know, Loophole Online land, I actually, you know, I feel like I can have a little bit more of a full relationship with you all where there are more resources to be able to actually like see how good you are in a way that the disconnected, you know, feeling of a book where\\r\\n- 5:02it\'s, you know, it\'s not video. It\'s not it doesn\'t it doesn\'t feel as alive, you know, even though I do endeavor to make the Loophole book be as alive as it possibly can.\\r\\n- 5:10But in this online platform, I\'m a little bit closer to you. So I feel like I can give you a process that it\'s not as, you know, easily checkable. Maybe now in the world of AI, where you can have AI transcribe your Translations for you, you actually can check your Translations a little bit easier\\r\\n- 5:27than you could have maybe 2018. But in this world, I feel I finally have like the resources and the team and the platform to bring you what I have always wanted to bring you for the past six years.\\r\\n- 5:41You do not know how important doing this has been to me to finally, finally be able to share with my Loophole book audience that I love so much. The things that I\'ve been working on with so, so few people\\r\\n- 5:58and, you know, you have to work with few people to really understand whether what you\'re doing works because you need that really in-depth ability to see, you know, you have to study and you have to adjust and you have to dial it in to like make sure that it works. Because if\\r\\n- 6:12it doesn\'t work, you know, it\'s trash. Get rid of it. But I am convinced because I\'ve had six years to make sure that this worked and it does work. And I have wanted to give it to you for so long. And it\'s been really, really hard to get this\\r\\n- 6:25whole thing up and running. But it is now and it\'s going to be yours finally. And so thank you for being here with me and for actually, you know, listening to half this cockeyed theories I have about all this stuff of, you know, helping you learn how to really retain and work with information.\\r\\n- 6:45And prove to you that you\'re a lot smarter than you really think you are. All right. I love you. Of course, I get emotional this video. All right.\\r\\n- 6:52I\'ll see you soon.\\r\\n##### Day 2\\r\\n### Take Notes\\r\\n#\\r\\n\\r\\nFILE training-camp\\\\translation\\\\Why Its Good to Be Hypersensitive • LO.md\\r\\n---\\r\\ntitle: \\"Why Its Good to Be Hypersensitive • LO\\"\\r\\nsource: \\"[url]\\r\\nauthor:\\r\\npublished:\\r\\ncreated: 2026-08-29\\r\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\r\\ntags:\\r\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\r\\n---\\r\\n## Why It\'s Good to Be Hypersensitive\\r\\n0:06\\r\\n/\\r\\n5:44\\r\\n- 0:00why it\'s good to be hypersensitive. So this is coming from someone who is hypersensitive. And so absolutely no shade. Being hypersensitive is super, super useful in the LSAT.\\r\\n- 0:10The reason why it\'s good is because it allows you to avert all kinds of bad consequences later on in a question or a section.\\r\\n- 0:16Those bad consequences being wrong CLIRs, wrong answers, confusion, like time panic because you don\'t know the answer, all different kinds of stuff like that.\\r\\n- 0:26And so what do I mean, though, when I say hypersensitive? Mostly what I mean is if you are reading something and there\'s like a word in what you\'re reading, you notice it and you find significance in that word that you\'re reading.\\r\\n- 0:38People who are less sensitive are more likely to overlook certain words because they don\'t think they really matter that much.\\r\\n- 0:45They don\'t think that they\'re very important. Like, I honestly can\'t tell you how many times I\'ve written in someone\'s tutoring notes, pay attention to the adjectives and adverbs in a stimulus or in an answer choice.\\r\\n- 0:56And this isn\'t because adjectives and adverbs are more important than other words in the stimulus. Like I say, every word in the stimulus is the most important word in the stimulus.\\r\\n- 1:03The reason why I write that so much is because people who are less sensitive tend to overlook adjectives and adverbs.\\r\\n- 1:10And so everything being equal, that will be something that I correct more often simply because it\'s more likely to be overlooked.\\r\\n- 1:17And the people who write the exam know that people who are less sensitive to individual words will tend to overlook an adjective or an adverb typically more often than they\'re going to overlook a noun or a verb.\\r\\n- 1:28And so they\'ll often pack a ton of meaning, a ton of significance, a ton of influence over the rest of the stimulus into that adjective or adverb.\\r\\n- 1:37And that will then cast a large shadow over the rest of the question that if you didn\'t get that adjective or adverb, getting the correct answer is going to be a much, much harder venture for you.\\r\\n- 1:48In fact, this morning in tutoring, I wrote this note and it was because the person I was with, they did not see the word recent in recent findings.\\r\\n- 1:59And there was another time duration early in the stimulus that they just auto applied to the next sentence instead of actually seeing the word recent and applying it to what the recent was talking about.\\r\\n- 2:09And this is just the kind of very innocent sort of LSAT mistake that people will often write off as like, oh, just a misread.\\r\\n- 2:16Oh, I just need to pay closer attention and all of that different kind of stuff. And they\'ll act like these sorts of mistakes aren\'t like that big of a deal. But these sorts of mistakes are actually like probably the majority of LSAT mistakes.\\r\\n- 2:28And so any sorts of actions that we take to lessen our propensity toward those mistakes is really going to be in our benefit. Right.\\r\\n- 2:36Because obviously, like if a given cause makes up the majority of mistakes, well, then you would want to go and make an impact on that set of mistakes just because there are so many. I don\'t\\r\\n- 2:48want you to when you see a mistake like this, like, oh, I just didn\'t see recent. Oh, whatever. No, it\'s not whatever. It\'s I need to be more sensitive to what is going on in the stimulus.\\r\\n- 3:00I need to not write off little things that I don\'t think matter just to go faster or because, you know, I just want the gist or whatever else. I need to really focus and be super sensitive to what I\'m doing.\\r\\n- 3:09Very often, I will describe reading as like it\'s like a tactile sense and like Powerful words. They feel like cold metal, like super solid, Provable words.\\r\\n- 3:18They feel like they\'re flowing like water. But it\'s almost like when you\'re reading, it\'s like Morse code or Braille a little bit where you can just you can feel the ups and downs of what you\'re reading.\\r\\n- 3:27This also goes with your intonation. I know I sound unhinged when I talk about stuff like this, but that sensitivity of feeling those words in a deeper way, being super invested in what you\'re doing when you\'re doing it, it is the biggest effortless hack there is. And I know it might sound silly right\\r\\n- 3:47now, but if you can try and invest more in your intonation and actually making every word pop just a little bit, you\'ll get closer and closer to feeling those words more directly and not having to think about how much adjectives matter.\\r\\n- 4:01Because I don\'t want you sitting on the actual day of the test and being like, oh, Ellen told me I need to pay attention to the adjectives.\\r\\n- 4:06So like, here I am. Look at the adjective. That\'s not what I want at all. I want you to uplift your general level of like care and obsession with every little word so that those adjectives are naturally retained.\\r\\n- 4:17But one more story from this morning, the same student that I was with this morning, she had cut out a full dependent clause,\\r\\n- 4:25what you what you might call an optional element from the first chapter of the Loophole. It was just it was like consequently and then words in a comma and then next part of sentence.\\r\\n- 4:35And this dependent clause or optional element, it was specifying the group that the Conclusion was supposed to apply to. And she completely left it out of her Translation and completely left the information out of her CLIR.\\r\\n- 4:47And then the CLIR was off because that information was actually pretty operative in terms of how you should be CLIRing the stimulus and how you would then go into the answer choice and what answer is right.\\r\\n- 4:56And when I brought it up to her, she\'s like, oh, I just didn\'t think that was very important. And I bring this up to show you that you don\'t actually know what is important when you first meet a set of text.\\r\\n- 5:07When you\'re first looking at the first letter of a dependent clause, you cannot know whether or not that is going to be operative in a later stage of the question or not.\\r\\n- 5:17And so it pays to be sensitive to that information regardless of what you might be future projecting in terms of its importance, because you will be wrong as often as you\'re right in terms of your ability to predict that sort of behavior of the text.\\r\\n- 5:32And so you want to just increase your overall level of sensitivity to the point where you can feel those words in your soul, too. All right.\\r\\n- 5:40I love you. I hope you don\'t think I\'m too much of a weirdo and I will see you soon.\\r\\n##### Day 5\\r\\n### Take Notes\\r\\n#\\r\\n\\r\\nFILE training-camp\\\\translation\\\\Why Outside Knowledge Is Not the Devil • LO.md\\r\\n---\\r\\ntitle: \\"Why Outside Knowledge Is Not the Devil • LO\\"\\r\\nsource: \\"[url]\\r\\nauthor:\\r\\npublished:\\r\\ncreated: 2026-08-29\\r\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\r\\ntags:\\r\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\r\\n---\\r\\n## Why Outside Knowledge is Not the Devil\\r\\nand they, they maybe have pearls\\r\\n4:43\\r\\n/\\r\\n7:52\\r\\n- 0:00That\'s right. Outside knowledge isn\'t the devil. I know you want to come for me. It\'s okay. Outside knowledge is not the devil that people make it out to be in LSAT prep.\\r\\n- 0:11So I want to tell you why and I want to tell you why people say it and why you shouldn\'t care.\\r\\n- 0:15It is not the devil because it is unavoidable. When you read a word on the LSAT in logical reasoning or in reading comp, you have no choice but to know the definition of that word and to know the general context in which words like that appear.\\r\\n- 0:32For instance, if you read the word cigarette, how do you know what a cigarette is? You know, because of outside knowledge, right?\\r\\n- 0:40And when you read the word cigarette, I would say it\'s generally accepted to know that these are entities that people smoke and there\'s nicotine involved and there\'s health consequences perhaps associated and stuff like that.\\r\\n- 0:53Like when we call this common knowledge about cigarettes, right? And a lot of times, I think when we talk about outside knowledge on the LSAT, we act like you\'re not allowed to know that cigarettes are often associated with health consequences, that that\'s like some kind of weird imaginary veil\\r\\n- 1:10you have to place over your understanding. But that\'s impossible. That\'s not like a real thing that a person can do.\\r\\n- 1:17When you read the word cigarettes, you\'re going to have to use your outside knowledge to Fill In the definition of that word.\\r\\n- 1:24And at some point you might be like, oh, well, okay, well, I can know what cigarettes is, but, you know, I shouldn\'t assume that the cigarettes are what we smoke through the mouth and we shouldn\'t assume that there are perhaps health consequences associated with those cigarettes and\\r\\n- 1:37all that different kind of stuff. But I would say you kind of don\'t have a choice because all of those associations are essentially intrinsic to the concept of a cigarette.\\r\\n- 1:46And if you are narrowing yourself down in what you allow the definitions of words to be as you read them on the LSAT, so far that you\'re like, well, is it a smoking cigarette?\\r\\n- 1:57I mean, you\'re essentially playing a losing game. You know, like there is a level of outside knowledge that is simply necessary to read words and perform analytical operations on this test\\r\\n- 2:08to question logical relationships. You have to have some level of knowledge of what the concepts being explored are. And I find very often that this aversion to outside knowledge because of previous cautions that people have heard in the preposphere makes people very afraid to quit because they\'re\\r\\n- 2:24like, well, I\'m not allowed to say anything outside the text and all of this kind of stuff. And it\'s like, girl, you\'re allowed to have thoughts and beliefs and use your beautiful brain.\\r\\n- 2:35You know, like this is, I think, one of the ways that accidentally in prep we make smart people feel worse than they actually should is we tell people they\'re not allowed to use all of the wonderful associations that they\'ve made throughout their life between different sorts of ideas.\\r\\n- 2:49And I just, you know, I complain about being people being skeptical of me, but I actually am the ultimate not follow the rules type of person.\\r\\n- 2:56And when I was prepping, I didn\'t really care what anyone said about outside knowledge. I was like, well, I\'m going to read it the way I read it\\r\\n- 3:02and I\'m going to think about it the way I think about it. So whatever. And I just would go through and question whatever I wanted to question using whatever knowledge I wanted to use, because\\r\\n- 3:09I was like, girl, bye. I mean, like, what am I going to do? How am I going to know what kind of knowledge is good knowledge and bad knowledge?\\r\\n- 3:15And this becomes like some kind of random epistemological question that we\'re juggling in the middle of taking a 35 minute L.R. section of like, oh, is this knowledge common enough of what the definition of a cigarette is\\r\\n- 3:26or is this knowledge too esoteric? And all of that is a question that doesn\'t have an answer. There is no line we can draw in the sand of this kind of knowledge is good\\r\\n- 3:35and that kind of knowledge is bad. And it would be an impossible question to answer even if it did have an answer in the context of a 35 minute section.\\r\\n- 3:42And so why am I telling you all of this? I\'m telling you all of this because I want you to start using your beautiful brain.\\r\\n- 3:47I want you to actually bring in all the beautiful knowledge and context of the world that you have as a thinking, breathing human who is existing in the world and start to use your common sense from real life, because your common sense is what informs reasonable logical relationships versus non reasonable\\r\\n- 4:04logical relationships. And I want you to start getting questions right on that basis. Now, let me tell you why this is all come to be. And this is like the whole guarding against outside knowledge thing is such a prevalent prescription in LSAT prep.\\r\\n- 4:18Well, people tell you to be afraid of outside knowledge because the LSAT is not a knowledge test. The LSAT is not grading\\r\\n- 4:25you on how much you know about oysters coming into the test. Right. It\'s not as though you should have been studying up on what waters they like to live in or whatever. But the LSAT does expect you that when you read oysters, you know, you know, these are sea creatures that\\r\\n- 4:40they live in, you know, water and they maybe have pearls and they\'re bivalves. Maybe not bivalves. That was probably a little bit of high school bio talking.\\r\\n- 4:47But that they expect you to know those sorts of general things, but they\'re not testing you on how many species of oyster are there.\\r\\n- 4:55You don\'t have to know that as some kind of preexisting fact. But of course, you have to be attuned if the stimulus brings you that information.\\r\\n- 5:00So, you know, bully for all of us, but you need the context of an oyster to apply that how many species there may be information that may come to you in the stimulus.\\r\\n- 5:08So since the test is not devoted to testing your preexisting knowledge of oysters and whatnot, people are like, don\'t bring that in.\\r\\n- 5:15And I can only guess that this is like some kind of lowest common denominator piece of advice of like, don\'t choose an answer because, like, you know, in real life, there\'s this many species of oyster or my mom really likes the color blue. So blue is the best.\\r\\n- 5:29And I pick B because it says blue or something like I don\'t really know, because in all my years of teaching and this is thousands and thousands of hours of one on one else that prep, I can probably count on one hand the number of times I\'ve ever seen anyone miss a question due to misusing outside knowledge.\\r\\n- 5:46And 100 percent of those times were because someone was an expert in the field they were doing the question on, as in somebody had a Ph.D. In like, what was it like? Neuro engineering or something\\r\\n- 5:59was something with the brain and machines and they were doing an R.C. passage that was something related to the brain.\\r\\n- 6:05And so when people have that kind of outside expertise, it\'s very challenging for them to understand what is common knowledge and what is not.\\r\\n- 6:13If you are not coming into this as some kind of world expert in a topic that you are about to get questions on. Hey, congrats.\\r\\n- 6:20But when you get questions related to, say, your major or things like that, remember, you\'re not being expected here to, like, reinvent what supply and demand should best be calculated as.\\r\\n- 6:34You\'re just expected to be able to navigate concepts of supply and demand using basic tools. But you\'re not going to have to do any sort of unreal calculations or anything like that.\\r\\n- 6:44That\'s totally fine. And so I don\'t want you to be disempowered in any way, thinking that, like, oh, because outside knowledge is good, I have to, like, become an expert in all these various fields.\\r\\n- 6:55No. But as you probably can see from looking at the curriculum, we do want to boost your amount of common knowledge that you\'re bringing to the exam.\\r\\n- 7:03For instance, if you\'ve ever heard of trade secrets before you do R.C., you probably have a leg up. But ever hearing of trade secrets and I\'m going to write a treatise on trade secrets are two very different things.\\r\\n- 7:13We want you more on the former side of the line rather than the latter. And so I hope that this helps that you do not have to be afraid of bringing your real experience, your real knowledge and like how you exist in the world and think of relationships as reasonable.\\r\\n- 7:28You don\'t have to be afraid of bringing that into the exam. This is a demon that doesn\'t really exist. It\'s kind of like a paper tiger that\'s been threatening you all along.\\r\\n- 7:37And if you do get a question wrong because of outside knowledge, shoot me an email. Now there, I am really putting my money where my mouth is with this, right?\\r\\n- 7:46Because am I about to have my inbox blow up? I don\'t think I will. All right. I\'ll see you soon.\\r\\n##### Day 5\\r\\n### Take Notes\\r\\n#\\r\\n"}'}]

It\'s conditioned on this if condition\\r\\n- 2:24and that\'s a certain state of affairs, meaning the economic policy is not changing, that that didn\'t happen, meaning we know from what the economist told us that the economic policies actually did change, meaning that if condition was dormant and so I know that this is a tough one to\\r\\n- 2:40translate. When we\'re engaging with these conditional statements and we\'re saying essentially that an if condition didn\'t get activated, we\'re not saying that anything is false. Ah, where one\'s hatred of abstraction comes home to roost, right? People very often hate abstraction on the LSAT. They complain about it all the time.\\r\\n- 0:10And Method, it\'s really the most abstract task you\'re going to be asked to do on the entire LSAT. Of course, very similar to Argument Part, but since you have to do the whole stimulus, I would say that it\'s, you know, it\'s a bigger, bigger task than Argument Part to a certain extent. And flaw\\r\\n- 0:24has more guardrails. You know, there are like, there\'s more clues on what is bad in reasoning. You can memorize lists,\\r\\n- 0:30you know, there\'s there\'s more to go on with Method. Like, yeah, you could maybe memorize like every single form of reasoning that\'s ever been given on the LSAT.\\r\\n- 0:38I don\'t personally find that to be super valuable because it\'s just too much and there isn\'t enough return on investment to do it.\\r\\n- 0:43You instead have to really read the room of the stimulus, understand on a deep level what the author was trying to do.\\r\\n- 0:52I really like Method because it kind of plays to that sense of empathy that you have to have for the author of\\r\\n- 0:57you want to read their intentions of like, OK, here was the technique you were trying to employ. And of course, with Method, they don\'t really want you to critique anything.\\r\\n- 1:06I mean, I I critique it anyway in the CLIR because it\'s always good to have your critical hat on. But when you\'re in the answer choices itself, you\'re not like coming for their wig or anything.\\r\\n- 1:17You\'re not like really trying to kill anybody. You\'re just describing what the author was doing, what their intention was, what they meant to do, a strategy they employed successful or not. But you\'re doing it without any sort of judgment or anything like that in the answer choices.\\r\\n- 1:34And you\'re doing a very similar operation to what you do in Argument Part. And so all of the advice that I gave you on the Argument Part strategy video, it really pertains here.\\r\\n- 1:42So if you haven\'t watched that one yet, definitely go back and watch the Argument Part strategy video because we\'re going to be building on it here.\\r\\n- 1:47But the difference is it pertains to the Method pertains to the entire stimulus as opposed to one single Argument Part.\\r\\n- 1:55Now, one thing I often see people have trouble with in Method is first off, the vocabulary of the answer choices, how they\'re phrased and being able to think in your Mission and your Method Mission in language like those answer choices.\\r\\n- 2:09This tends to be really hard for people, which is why I do have a thing called the Method vocab list in the Loophole.\\r\\n- 2:15And there\'s an uncut version as well online. And so I\'ll link to that here where you can actually go through and look at the uncut Method vocab list for more information.\\r\\n- 2:22Of course, that\'s also relevant to Argument Part, but it\'s named after Method for a reason. You know, it was literally sourced from Method answer choices and so super, super helpful to use.\\r\\n- 2:30Now, just one extra piece of advice about how to use the uncut Method vocab list and the Method vocab list that\'s in the Loophole.\\r\\n- 2:38When you go through those those words, I want you to use the standard of could I easily use this word in a casual sentence?\\r\\n- 2:48And if the answer to that question is no, you need to study that word and elevate it to that level\\r\\n- 2:55because I think very often people don\'t like studying vocab and like don\'t want to have to do it.\\r\\n- 2:59And so they\'re like, yeah, basically. You\'re probably expecting it to be like some super technical, mathy sounding thing of like, uh, the consequence. It\'s like really not that.\\r\\n- 0:13The worst conditional habit everyone has is that they are very all or nothing about like an exclusive relationship between diagramming and understanding.\\r\\n- 0:23Very few people will actually combine a conceptual understanding of the stimulus\'s Translation with a diagramming level understanding. Typically if someone is going to diagram, they will want to skip straight to diagram and diagramming and not really think about what the sentence means in any kind\\r\\n- 0:41of conceptual way that they will kind of let the diagramming do that step for them. But here\'s the problem with that.\\r\\n- 0:47If you let the diagramming do the understanding step for you, then your diagram might end up wrong. Um, your diagram might not actually represent the real meaning of the sentence or phrase that you\'re trying to diagram.\\r\\n- 1:00And so then Xing out that moment of conceptual Translation before you diagram, it really just creates a very faulty picture on all but the most simple conditionals.\\r\\n- 1:13And very often, I think the reason why this happens is, you know, time, but beyond the, the time panic aspect of it is people will not know their conditional indicators typically well enough to be able to easily rephrase them into a conceptual Translation\\r\\n- 1:30that translating a sentence that contains the word only if into a personalized if then feels harder in the moment than just going and jumping to a diagram or they aren\'t sure of their indicators at all, which is really a separate problem. You know, maybe that\'s the second biggest\\r\\n- 1:46diagramming habit people have that\'s bad is not really knowing their indicators. But if you let\'s say you do, you still might be not quite comfortable enough with translating them into the real meaning of the phrase\\r\\n- 2:00rather than just like beep beep boop robot. I put thing after arrow, you know, like that, that is like a typically a lesser amount of understanding than actually being able to frame the conceptual relationship between the two variables yourself in a personalized if then before you put the\\r\\n- 2:19diagram down because you really want to understand what the statement that they wrote, what it really means to then know that your conditional is right.\\r\\n- 2:28Very often we have a lot of anxiety about like, what if my conditional is wrong? I don\'t want a diagram because what if I put it down wrong\\r\\n- 2:34and all of that different kind of stuff? And honestly, this whole conceptual Translation step of really feeling out what they mean by the statement they give you, that\'s before you diagram. That\'s the way to know that your conditional is right. Because if you actually translated into a personalized if then\\r\\n- 2:51before you throw the diagram down, then you\'ll know that, OK, did what I write match my personalized if then because I generated my personalized if then Translation on the basis of whatever it is that they wrote.\\r\\n- 3:05And so then you kind of have that intermediary checkpoint phase. All right.\\r\\n- 3:47Hope this helped and I\'ll see you soon.\\r\\n\\r\\n##### In This Training Block\\r\\n\\r\\n## Video Course Review\\r\\n\\r\\n[\\r\\n\\r\\nConcept Review\\r\\n\\r\\n](https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/concept-review)\\r\\n\\\\nFILE training-camp\\\\learn\\\\Weaken Question Strategy • LO.md\\\\n---\\r\\ntitle: \\"Weaken Question Strategy • LO\\"\\r\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/learn/video/weaken-question-strategy?source=timeline&plan=foundation-plan\\"\\r\\nauthor:\\r\\npublished:\\r\\ncreated: 2026-08-30\\r\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\r\\ntags:\\r\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\r\\n---\\r\\n## Weaken Question Strategy\\r\\n\\r\\n4:45\\r\\n\\r\\n/\\r\\n\\r\\n9:13\\r\\n\\r\\n- 0:00Why is Weaken hard? I would say that it\'s one of the top question types that people tend to bring to me as like I just have a problem with X question type.\\r\\n- 0:06And so I did a bit of thinking of what the reasons really are for why people have those problems. And we\'re going to go over each of them and we\'re going to go over their fixes as well.\\r\\n- 0:15And so the first one is that people don\'t understand highest card question types and what their burdens are. People don\'t make Missions, at least not Missions that are well translated and detailed enough to actually function well on a Weaken question.\\r\\n- 0:26And they don\'t read the whole answer before they judge it slash they use cheap metrics and the answer is not the backup plan.\\r\\n- 0:32And so let\'s go through each one of these reasons and we\'re going to talk about how to overcome, you know, the problem that this reason poses and how we can make Weaken not a problem for you.\\r\\n- 0:42So first people don\'t understand highest card question types. So there\'s a previous video that goes into depth on what is a highest card question type.\\r\\n- 0:49I\'m going to assume you know some of that, but I\'ll give a brief, you know, brief primer just in case you\'ve skipped ahead.\\r\\n- 0:55So highest card is a one subset of the question types and it\'s either highest card questions or aces or nothing.\\r\\n- 1:03Basically I split the question types similar to the bifurcation of Powerful approval with different questions on each side. And aces or nothing questions, which Weaken is not one of those,\\r\\n- 1:13they are very binary. You\'re either right or you\'re wrong. Like Sufficient Assumption, you either prove the Conclusion or you don\'t.\\r\\n- 1:19And then we have highest card questions of which Weaken is one, which strength it is one, Resolution is another in which you are doing something but you don\'t have to hit 100 to be correct.\\r\\n- 1:30And so what I mean by that in this context is a correct Weaken answer choice can just make the Conclusion like 13% less likely to be true.\\r\\n- 1:38And that is full on enough to be correct. And so this is what they\'re actually referring to with the most in the question stem on some Weaken questions is not that there\'s more than one answer that\'s going to Weaken, but like you Weaken the most as in there\'s a gradation, there\'s a spectrum and we just\\r\\n- 1:55have to push it. We don\'t have to go all the way over the line into disproving to be a correct Weaken answer\\r\\n- 2:00choice. And so the way to think about this correctly is there is one answer choice in there that is doing anything that is making the Conclusion less likely to be true at all, like greater than 1%. And there are four answer choices that are doing zero. And if you think that there\'s more than one\\r\\n- 2:17answer that is impacting the Conclusion in a Weakening sense, you can know you\'re wrong from the very beginning because that does not exist.\\r\\n- 2:27And so if you are thinking that is true, you know that you have to remedy your misunderstanding somewhere because that\'s not how we can works,\\r\\n- 2:34which I actually find to be a very comforting dynamic, right? Because I would much rather know I\'m wrong than persistent and incorrect understanding\\r\\n- 2:42that if I\'m like, Oh no, both of these do Weaken. It\'s just that one Weaken more. I know. No, no, no, no, no.\\r\\n- 2:47I misunderstood something somewhere. I\'ve got to go fix it rather than being like, okay, that one Weaken more. So that one\'s right.\\r\\n- 2:54That\'s not a real thing because there will only be one answer choice in the set that Weaken at all. It\'s just that that Weakening, it\'s kind of a move, don\'t disprove sort of thing.\\r\\n- 3:05You just want to move the Conclusion into the less likely to be true territory, less supported, less reasonable territory. You don\'t need to literally say, Oh, this can never be true.\\r\\n- 3:17One way that I can tell people don\'t really understand this aspect of Weakening questions when I talk about them with them is they\'ll say, yeah, but you know, if they is true, the Conclusion could still be true.\\r\\n- 3:27And I\'m like, yeah, it doesn\'t fully disprove the Conclusion. That\'s okay. It just makes it less likely. And that sort of gradation, it\'s a, it\'s a bit more of a, I don\'t know, a nuanced complex space than simple disproving.\\r\\n- 3:45And I think that\'s why it gets in people\'s craw a little bit to be like, but you could still have the Conclusion.\\r\\n- 3:50It\'s like, yeah, but the probability that the Conclusion is true has decreased. And that\'s what makes this answer right. And so next on our list, the reason why people don\'t like Weakening questions or have trouble with them is that they don\'t make Missions.\\r\\n- 4:03And so why are Missions so crucial on Weaken questions? Well, it\'s because the Mission on Weaken and Strengthen, but we\'ll focus on we can hear is a little bit special.\\r\\n- 4:13We want it to be a goal for the answer choice to hit. And so let\'s say my Conclusion is Jenna is a great runner and I want to Weaken that.\\r\\n- 4:24So I am now going to say, okay, I want an answer that makes it more likely that Jenna isn\'t a great runner or Jenna\'s not necessarily a great runner.\\r\\n- 4:33And notice how I\'ve just translated into the question stem or into the Conclusion, the question stem energy, right? That my Conclusion is Jen is a great runner.\\r\\n- 4:41I want to Weaken that. We, I want a way. It doesn\'t have to be true. And so I say, Jenna isn\'t necessarily a great runner.\\r\\n- 4:47That\'s what I want my answer to gesture toward. That\'s my Mission. And so that little moment there where I\'m saying exactly, exactly in a very concrete way, what I want my answer to gesture toward, what I want my answer to make more likely that Jenna isn\'t necessarily a great runner, that little mental\\r\\n- 5:05step of making it super concrete, what my answer is supposed to do is actually really, really, really helpful. And I\'ll tell you the thing that people tend to do that isn\'t this is I\'ll say, okay, so I want something that Weakens the Conclusion.\\r\\n- 5:19How much harder is it to vet answers against I want something that Weakens the Conclusion when you don\'t even have in your conscious working memory, what the Conclusion actually is on the surface.\\r\\n- 5:29And you now Weaken the Conclusion. I have to know I have to apply a negation to whatever my referential idea of the Conclusion is like\\r\\n- 5:36it\'s way easier to make a mistake. If I\'m asking an answer, do you Weaken the Conclusion versus do you make it less likely that Jenna is a great runner?\\r\\n- 5:44Do you make it more likely that Jenna isn\'t necessarily a great runner? You could say it in multiple ways. Um, that specificity in the Mission is so, so, so helpful for eliminating wrong answers.\\r\\n- 5:56And so our last bit is people not reading the entire answer slash using sheet metrics slash not the backup plan.\\r\\n- 6:02So why is this such a problem on week in questions? Well, if you judge the answer before you\'ve read the entire answer, you\'re very susceptible to what I\'ll call relevancE Bias. Um,\\r\\n- 6:13I in tutoring this week, I had somebody miss a week in question, and I believe it started with, I think tech breakthroughs or technological breakthroughs or something like that.\\r\\n- 6:22And there is no mention of technological breakthroughs in the stimulus. And I believe that this answer did Weaken. Um,\\r\\n- 6:29and they read tech breakthroughs and they were like, Oh, well, I mean, I know that tech breakthroughs aren\'t relevant to the stimulus. And so I knew this was wrong immediately.\\r\\n- 6:38And I understand that it\'s really nice to feel these kind of instantaneous reactions to answers. And it makes you feel like an expert and you\'re very in control, but it\'s actually like very Counterproductive to accuracy.\\r\\n- 6:47And so when you\'re in the moment there and you\'ve read tech breakthroughs, you want to delay judgment until after you\'ve read and translated the entire answer choice.\\r\\n- 6:56I know this sucks. Like literally the next couple of words had a bunch of words from the stimulus in there.\\r\\n- 7:29And so what that answer is doing is playing on people who tend to jump the gun, right? And it doesn\'t matter to me. How are you going to translate? It\'s fine.
