See you soon.\\r\\n\\r\\n##### Day 10\\r\\n\\r\\n### Take Notes\\r\\n\\r\\n#\\r\\n\\\\nFILE training-camp\\\\learn\\\\What Is a Necessary Assumption • LO.md\\\\n---\\r\\ntitle: \\"What Is a Necessary Assumption • LO\\"\\r\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/learn/video/what-is-a-necessary-assumption?source=timeline&plan=foundation-plan\\"\\r\\nauthor:\\r\\npublished:\\r\\ncreated: 2026-08-30\\r\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\r\\ntags:\\r\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\r\\n---\\r\\n### Ellen\'s Notes\\r\\n\\r\\n**Example**: Dr Shaah\'s research claims that UFOs are real.New evidence of alien bones has recently come to light and carbon testing shows the bones to be of unknown origin. No remains of earthly origin indicate an unknown origin in carbon testing.\\r\\n\\r\\n***Example Necessary Assumptions***\\r\\n\\r\\n1. The presence of alien bones on earth indicates the possibility of UFOs.\\r\\n2. The bones that were recently found qualify as remains.\\r\\n3. Remains of non-earthy origin were not transported to earth by natural cosmological processes.\\r\\n\\r\\n***Ways to Think About a Necessary Assumption (Pick your fav!)***\\r\\n\\r\\n1. If the conclusion is true, the necessary assumption has to be true.\\r\\n2. The author has to believe the necessary assumption.\\r\\n3. The author has to think the necessary assumption is true.\\r\\n4. If the conclusion is proven, the necessary assumption has to be true.\\r\\n5. If the premises worked to prove the conclusion, the necessary assumption is required.\\r\\n6. If these premises proved this conclusion, the author must assume the necessary assumption.\\r\\n7. The argument relies on the necessary assumption.\\r\\n8. For the argument to make any sense, the necessary assumption has to be its foundation.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Take Notes\\r\\n\\r\\n#\\r\\n"}\n{"chunk_id":"89640b","wall_time_seconds":0.6033049,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":8529,"output":"<stdin>:6: FutureWarning: Possible nested set at position 4\\r\\n\\\\nFILE training-camp\\\\translation\\\\Micro Quips and Stream of Consciousness Magic • LO.md\\\\n---\\r\\ntitle: \\"Micro Quips and Stream of Consciousness Magic • LO\\"\\r\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/translation/video/micro-quips-and-stream-of-consciousness-magic?source=timeline&plan=foundation-plan\\"\\r\\nauthor:\\r\\npublished:\\r\\ncreated: 2026-08-27\\r\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\r\\ntags:\\r\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\r\\n---\\r\\n## Micro-Quips and Stream of Consciousness Magic\\r\\n\\r\\n0:24\\r\\n\\r\\n/\\r\\n\\r\\n7:59\\r\\n\\r\\n- 0:00Ah, microquips and stream of consciousness magic. Yeah, microquips are so fun. Microquips are basically where you are at any point in the RTQ stage,\\r\\n- 0:09maybe not in the Q stage, because it would make no sense to have a microquip inside the stage, but at some point in the reading or translating stage. And you just have this like moment of like a burst of inspiration. And you just you have a thought and you need to say it\\r\\n- 0:22like that\'s a microquip. It\'s like a little kind of like bubble, like a little carbonated pop in the middle of another step.\\r\\n- 0:30If you really feel that reaction in that moment of like, OK, yeah, sure. You know, like whatever weird thing you want to say in that moment back at what they\'re saying. If you feel like it\'s natural and you can\'t hold it in, just say it right in the moment.\\r\\n- 0:43However, I really want to advise you not to let these microquips, these like tiny little baby quips that pop up in different parts of the process. They do not replace the Q stage of the RTQ process.\\r\\n- 0:54And so sometimes when you watch my examples, you are going to see these little microquip moments because I like honestly can\'t help it.\\r\\n- 1:00Sometimes when I\'m reading, I just need to say something. And sometimes you even when I was doing my one example, I was like, I just displayed very bad form because I quit right before my Translation.\\r\\n- 1:09That was a microquip. That\'s like these little things that they just come to mind and you just want to say them. But they are not a replacement for the quip stage of the RTQ process.\\r\\n- 1:18You can think of the quip stage of the RTQ process that occurs after the Translation stage of the RTQ,\\r\\n- 1:23the RT, I guess, that that\'s like when you\'re really processing a lot of raw information and you need to have that processing stage to just make sure that everything is really nicely incorporated.\\r\\n- 1:35And if you have these little microquips otherwise, that\'s totally OK. That\'s just you honoring your stream of consciousness technique where you aren\'t editing yourself, you aren\'t vetting. Oh, was this a good quip, a bad quip?\\r\\n- 1:46Was it OK? Was it useful? All that like not doing all that means sometimes you\'re going to have a little stream of consciousness magic moment where a quip pops up in the middle of something else and you just honor it and you just go with it.\\r\\n- 1:56And it\'s incorporated into the process and totally fine. But I don\'t want you to take that as this kind of license to not do a real quip at the end, because those real quips are really more substantive and they allow that kind of preCLIR motion. They allow you to just move forward into the next\\r\\n- 2:10steps of the process. So we don\'t want to cut those out just because we have microquips. But I want to offer you one little thing to remember in terms of why quipping should be stream of consciousness.\\r\\n- 2:20And what do I mean by stream of consciousness? I mean, it\'s whatever thoughts come into your mind in the moment. You just say them, you just think them.\\r\\n- 2:28We don\'t want you like I\'ve talked about before, prethinking your reactions as like, are they good enough reactions? If not, I say something else.\\r\\n- 2:35No, no, no, no. The reaction is the reaction. And you just move on and you keep on, you know, coming up with better ones. But you don\'t opt out.\\r\\n- 2:41A good rule of thumb for this is an adaptation of an Ernest Hemingway quote. Ernest Hemingway has a quote called, not called, I guess he said, write drunk, edit sober. And I used to have this as a little illustrated postcard behind my desk when I was writing the Loophole. And no, I was\\r\\n- 2:58not literally drunk when I wrote the Loophole. But the the message behind write drunk, edit sober is that we are in that writing idea generation stage. You cannot be going and like vetting what you did, trying to make the perfect sentence, rewriting one sentence over and over and\\r\\n- 3:14over again, trying to make it, you know, ideal. Instead, you need to just get those ideas out. You need to write drunk, you know, just go do it.\\r\\n- 3:22And you know what? You\'ll fix it later. And edit sober, meaning that sober stage where you\'re editing, you\'re like, OK, here\'s where I clean up my own mess.\\r\\n- 3:29Like that was kind of a disaster in certain ways. And I enforce this for myself with the Loophole. And like I could literally show you, I mean, it\'s not this notebook, but it\'s a notebook that kind of look like this one\\r\\n- 3:38that I I wrote the whole Loophole book out longhand in notebooks handwritten. Yes, the Loophole is four hundred sixty two pages.\\r\\n- 3:49And yes, I did actually handwrite the entire book. The first draft of every page of the book outside of like the real else, that drills. I didn\'t rewrite all the stimuli or anything,\\r\\n- 3:59although I did. I think I rewrote the examples. But all of those were originally handwritten in my notebook.\\r\\n- 4:05And you want to know why? It was because it was only in my notebook without any of the electronic garbage to distract you that I could truly write drunk,\\r\\n- 4:13that I could truly actually just keep on going. And you can\'t really edit when you\'re writing, you know, in a big pen and a paper notebook,\\r\\n- 4:19you could, but it would be pretty non-ideal. And I I loved the flow state that I would get into where I would just write and write and write.\\r\\n- 4:26And I knew when I put it into the computer, I\'m going to edit it. I\'m going to fix whatever is broken.\\r\\n- 4:31Now, my extended metaphor or analogy over to Hemingway, it has a purpose because the analogous case here is quip drunk, CLIR sober. And\\r\\n- 4:42when you\'re quipping, just get it out. Just have the idea, you know, just have the thought. And maybe it gets you somewhere.\\r\\n- 4:50Maybe it doesn\'t. Maybe it\'s a dead end. Maybe it isn\'t. But you know what? You\'re energized. You\'re engaged. You\'re doing something in the stimulus.\\r\\n- 4:56You\'re bringing your beautiful, unique analytical mind to bear on the information as you read and translate. And that\'s what I want.\\r\\n- 5:03That\'s what I care about. I have made a lot of, you know, explanation videos in my life. And I don\'t think I can ever think of a time where I was like, you know what?\\r\\n- 5:12I didn\'t like that quip. I don\'t want to share that. Like, no, because in a sense, as long as you are actively engaged in quick quipping specifically and like in in with the actual thoughts, then there really aren\'t bad quips or right quips.\\r\\n- 5:27Like, sure, we can offer you pro tips of like things that make quips even more optimized or even more better, all that different kind of stuff.\\r\\n- 5:33But there\'s nothing that\'s going to teach you that better than experience of feeling out how quipping can grow across the stimulus, how you can reference earlier parts of the stimulus in a later quip and really make connections or show where connections don\'t exist.\\r\\n- 5:45The best way for you to learn that is to go and get in the ring yourself, actually be in the arena.\\r\\n- 5:52As Teddy Roosevelt once said, I love to. I need to get a thing for back there. But there\'s nothing better than being in the arena and watching all the tips in the world\\r\\n- 6:01isn\'t going to do anything if you don\'t actually get in there and challenge yourself very, very directly. And so in the CLIR stage, though, that\'s when we become sober editors.\\r\\n- 6:10Because, yeah, you\'re CLIR as to like actually like be a Loophole or be an Inference or be a Resolution, be a Controversy, whatever. And of course, I do allow a lot of flexibility in the CLIR.\\r\\n- 6:20Like if you want to say a Necessary Assumption, it\'s devolvable. OK, cool. But there\'s a lot more success and failure on that.\\r\\n- 6:25Like, you know, there\'s a CLIR answer key. There\'s ways to gauge whether a CLIR is successful or not successful in more of a very directly palpable sense than you would have with a quip.\\r\\n- 6:35A quip is meant to be that creative exploration stage. You\'re putting ideas together and it\'s like there are no bad ideas and improv.\\r\\n- 6:41Maybe you watch that YouTube video where I show you there are bad ideas and improv, but there are no bad ideas and improv in an LSAT land.\\r\\n- 6:44And so I want to let you be free in that sense. And then when you get into the CLIR, that\'s when you\'ve got to, you know, batten down the hatches, button things up and actually start to edit and make sure that all of your analysis is going to a place.\\r\\n- 6:58And so I hope that that helped. I hope that I\'ve encouraged you to access your stream of consciousness magic because stream of consciousness magic is truly great.\\r\\n- 7:06I mean, you might have guessed this. So forgive me if I\'m telling you something you already know. But all the videos you see in this whole loop online land are improvised.\\r\\n- 7:17And that\'s like, you know, tens of hours a video or all. No, no script, no bullets. It\'s all improvised because that stream of consciousness magic,\\r\\n- 7:29like that\'s what I that\'s the way I can do the best I can for you is by actually being present with you in this moment and talking to you, you know, by myself in my office, but talking to you as much as I can.\\r\\n- 7:40That\'s the energy, that awakeness, that kind of alertness and engaged feeling. That\'s how I want you to be when you\'re with a stimulus, not like accessing some pre-programmed strategy to quip or something like that.\\r\\n- 7:51I want you to take in information and I want you to react to it in the moment, just like I\'m reacting to you right now without you being even here.\\r\\n- 7:55All right. I love you. And I miss you, even though I don\'t know you. All right. See you soon. (you are not broken)\\r\\n\\r\\nor they will just not have any sub vocalization at all.\\r\\n\\r\\n1:16\\r\\n\\r\\n/\\r\\n\\r\\n6:29\\r\\n\\r\\n- 0:00What is reading and what should it be? I know I\'m probably asking a very annoying reductive question right now But I swear I have a more novel than average answer\\r\\n- 0:08When I ask what is reading what I\'m asking is what is it for you when you read? What are you really doing?\\r\\n- 0:18And what are your intentions when you are actually doing that act of reading? Like what do you really want to have happen?\\r\\n- 0:25Because very often and when people read they are not necessarily reading to get all the nuances of every single thing that is on the page right\\r\\n- 0:35because there are lots of different intentions that somebody might have when they\'re reading But very very often what you will find is what people call reading is more like some of the following\\r\\n- 0:46I listed out just a couple of habits that I have observed over the years that people do when they read\\r\\n- 0:50they will take on Multiple sentences all at once that they\'re just kind of like looking at them overall. They\'ll scan for proper nouns.\\r\\n- 0:57They\'ll omit connector words They\'ll get the gist. They\'ll omit the details. They\'ll combine the lists into those things They\'ll say okay, you know, and this is something about coins or this is something about boats.\\r\\n- 1:08This is something about light bulbs they\'ll Kind of say the words in their head But it will be in a monotone or they will just not have any sub vocalization at all Vocalization\\r\\n- 1:18means the voice in your head and they will often skip words that they don\'t want to pronounce And so if a word is long, they will just skip it.\\r\\n- 1:27They won\'t actually even really read it They\'ll skip names. They\'ll skip dates. They\'ll skip numbers. They\'ll skip anything that makes them uncomfortable\\r\\n- 1:36they just won\'t actually read it and This problem I call it a problem because I don\'t really consider this to be a reading for depth Sort of protocol that we could follow follows us into the LSAT\\r\\n- 1:50and it causes a lot of Heartache because all of those things that are you know safe ish to do in real life are Super not safe to do on the LSAT when you actually have to recall all the idiosyncrasies of what you\'re reading\\r\\n- 2:06And you actually do have to recall the idiosyncrasies while you\'re doing the LSAT The issue is the LSAT requires so much more of us than what these reading habits are designed to give us that There\'s just a misalignment there.\\r\\n- 2:20And so just for the purposes of our conversation because this is a conversation to me. It\'s a conversation Let\'s call all those strategies, you know of like it\'s about coins and skipping the stuff that\'s confusing to me Let\'s call that ghost reading\\r\\n- 2:34ghost reading is how I like to think of it in my in my head and It\'s a reading strategy that\'s designed for like a Verizon bill You just got in the mail where it\'s like I don\'t really want to read this thing\\r\\n- 2:46I\'m just trying to figure out about whatever, you know new fee you\'re putting out on my bill and like do I have to cancel\\r\\n- 2:52and it\'s a style of reading that is designed for really one thing which is speed to being done and To get done with the act of reading faster\\r\\n- 3:03And so when you think about that think about it for a second that like our our goal when we\'re reading is to not read\\r\\n- 3:10That isn\'t exactly a goal that is you know aligned for Retaining all the nuances of the details of the complexity of the information and all that\\r\\n- 3:22you were literally telling your brain I want to not do what I\'m doing while I\'m doing it I mean, that\'s that\'s some silliness\\r\\n- 3:28and for those of you out there who are like, oh no, Ellen I love to read I want to read\\r\\n- 3:32I want to you know, like marinate in the Tea of information that is going on. I love you\\r\\n- 3:40and thank you and you\'re in a world where this happens But one thing I really want to tell you is if you\'re doing any of the things that I just described and we all do Them when we are reading Verizon bills,\\r\\n- 3:51so this is no moral judgment on my behalf think of me like a researcher I\'m someone who\'s trying to understand the situation here\\r\\n- 3:57But if you are doing those things, which I think you do at least sometimes, you know When you\'re looking at a Verizon bill, I just want to tell you The fact that sometimes when you\'re doing the LSAT you have trouble remembering what you read trouble understanding what you read\\r\\n- 4:11It doesn\'t mean you\'re broken You\'re not broken. You\'re just bringing a bad tool to the wrong fight You\'ve been socialized and trained to look at words a certain way and do certain activities.\\r\\n- 4:24That\'s fine Like I said, no moral judgment here But those tools that you\'ve been socialized to use, you know what\\r\\n- 4:31they just don\'t work that well over here and That doesn\'t mean that you\'re stupid. It doesn\'t mean that you\'re dumb\\r\\n- 4:39you\'re not dumb and This is something that\'s just really really important to me to let you know, you do not have any kind of disorder Necessarily,\\r\\n- 4:49you don\'t have a learning disability necessarily and for those of our friends who do you know more power to you?\\r\\n- 4:56Love you, too But just the fact that you are having trouble remembering what you read it doesn\'t necessarily mean that there is any sort of significant Biological issue going on.\\r\\n- 5:08It\'s just that you know, you\'re you\'re trying to I don\'t know Speed through a residential street when you know That\'s not really what the signs are built for and you might get a ticket\\r\\n- 5:18Like that is just a habit that you have been built to have but it does not mean that you can\'t make another habit\\r\\n- 5:25Right now when one is ghost reading essentially you\'re just treating a skill that is like one of those lifelong mastery sorts of skills like it\'s a utility like it\'s something Disposable\\r\\n- 5:37but we can treat reading like Japanese masters treat the making of you know knives and swords and things like they treat Making sushi like any artisan treats their craft the thing that they love to do day in and day out And they get better and better and better at it over time Reading thrives with\\r\\n- 5:56that sort of treatment and we can treat it like that We don\'t have to treat it as something that our only intention while we do it is to be done with it Faster\\r\\n- 6:06and when you switch this mindset and you start wanting to marinate in the tea of the words or maybe that tisane A tisane might be better.\\r\\n- 6:13Um, I think you\'ll see a big big difference But as you get into these later videos I really want to remind you take your time and let yourself marinate in the tisane\\r\\n- 6:24speed is not the only thing that matters All right. I\'ll see you in a minute\\r\\n\\r\\n##### Day 2\\r\\n\\r\\n### Take Notes\\r\\n\\r\\n## Video Course Review\\r\\n\\r\\nConcept Review\\r\\n\\\\nFILE training-camp\\\\translation\\\\Whats Quipping Lets Try It Together • LO.md\\\\n---\\r\\ntitle: \\"Whats Quipping Lets Try It Together • LO\\"\\r\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/translation/video/whats-quipping-lets-try-it-together?source=timeline&plan=foundation-plan\\"\\r\\nauthor:\\r\\npublished:\\r\\ncreated: 2026-08-27\\r\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\r\\ntags:\\r\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\r\\n---\\r\\n## What\'s Quipping? Let\'s Try It Together\\r\\n\\r\\n0:07\\r\\n\\r\\n/\\r\\n\\r\\n9:34\\r\\n\\r\\n- 0:00Alright, pause the video and just read the thing that\'s on the screen because I know you\'re gonna want to read it and I want you to read it.\\r\\n- 0:05Alright, did you pause? Did you pause? Okay, good. I\'m assuming you paused and you\'ve read what\'s on the screen. You\'re like, what the heck is this?\\r\\n- 0:15Well, this is AI Ellen. I have been training an AI doppelganger version of me and so you can see that I\'ve recruited her to the enterprise.\\r\\n- 0:22The reason I\'ve recruited AI Ellen to the enterprise today is because I want to actually have real real-time Improv\'d quipping that you know I didn\'t prepare for because I didn\'t write the example.\\r\\n- 0:34AI Ellen wrote the example. And so I am going to be going through it and doing a bunch of quickfire quipping demos with you right now that you can do along with me.\\r\\n- 0:41But one thing before we get started is make sure before you watch this video you have watched the Translation 2.0 module\\r\\n- 0:48and especially the video on quipping in the Translation 2.0 module because there\'s a lot of intro and material that I\'m not gonna go over in this video.\\r\\n- 0:54Instead, we\'re gonna work from that base and do a bunch of quickfire examples together. And so go watch a video if you haven\'t already.\\r\\n- 1:01And as I get example sentences here from AI Ellen, I want you to as soon as it gets generated also pause the video and try and translate and quipp that sentence yourself before you listen to me do it.\\r\\n- 1:12That way it\'ll be more fun. You\'ll be able to be more engaged and interactive with what we\'re gonna be doing together. Oh, I heart.\\r\\n\\r\\n##### Day 2\\r\\n\\r\\n### Take Notes\\r\\n\\r\\n#\\r\\n\\\\nFILE training-camp\\\\translation\\\\Whats Rtq • LO.md\\\\n---\\r\\ntitle: \\"Whats Rtq • LO\\"\\r\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/translation/video/whats-rtq?source=timeline&plan=foundation-plan\\"\\r\\nauthor:\\r\\npublished:\\r\\ncreated: 2026-08-24\\r\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\r\\ntags:\\r\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\r\\n---\\r\\n## What\'s RTQ?\\r\\n\\r\\nIt is a process, an operation\\r\\n\\r\\n0:32\\r\\n\\r\\n/\\r\\n\\r\\n4:31\\r\\n\\r\\n- 0:00What is RTQ? I mean, I have been waiting so long to be able to tell you this. I\'m so excited. OK,\\r\\n- 0:07so RTQ stands for read, translate, quip. And it\'s actually an acronym that one of my students came up with that I now look back and I\'m like, you know, they were right.\\r\\n- 0:17That\'s a that\'s a good way to encapsulate it, because RTQ is basically a unit. You read and you translate and you quip on every piece of text that you enCounter throughout a stimulus, an answer choice, a question to them, a passage, whatever it might be. It is a process, an operation that you perform\\r\\n- 0:34on chunks of text as you go. And so this is something you would be doing instead of writing down the conceptual information in your Translation like we have in the Loophole book.\\r\\n- 0:43It\'s totally fine to start with that writing down of conceptual information as a practice. You begin when you\'re reading a Loophole book.\\r\\n- 0:49It\'s not harmful. It\'s good. It\'s kind of a good like warm up to be able to get us into the RTQ process.\\r\\n- 0:56And the RTQ process is just a little bit more actively engaging throughout the stimulus as opposed to saving it all until the end, which is, you know, interesting because it allows us to do more things.\\r\\n- 1:05And so let me explain just the basics of how it works, and then we\'ll have in-depth videos that go through each step for you next.\\r\\n- 1:11And so RTQ, essentially, let\'s say a sentence is a nice full idea chunk. And that that doesn\'t necessarily mean that you would stop to RTQ at the end of a sentence only. Maybe you have like a comma and you want to have a sentence.\\r\\n- 1:25Maybe it\'s actually, you know, more like breaking it into thirds of a sentence. Typically, I tell people don\'t RTQ combining sentences unless you\'re, you know, very advanced and you feel very, very confident that you absolutely will retain all the conceptual details of everything you\'ve read.\\r\\n- 1:42Because usually when someone combines two sentences, especially when they\'re in the beginner stages, the first sentence\'s details will often be lost. And we don\'t want that to happen.\\r\\n- 1:51And so I often say, try and figure out where you want to break in your read to RTQ. By thinking of texts like this coffee cup, noticing\\r\\n- 1:57all my videos, I always have a coffee cup as a prop. It\'s because they\'re like five on this desk.\\r\\n- 2:02So as you\'re reading, you want to think of words as filling the coffee cup. And the words should go until about here, not here. When you stop to translate,\\r\\n- 2:13meaning you don\'t want to be retaking on so much text at a time that the words are starting to get lost and overflow.\\r\\n- 2:19You want to be a little bit more conservative in your break strategy of like I read, read, read, read, read until I\'m to about here in terms of the amount of words I\'ve taken it. And I\'m like, OK, I know I\'ve got this. I know it\'s not going to spill. I can stop to RTQ.\\r\\n- 2:33And so that would be when you stop, like I said, rounding down instead of rounding up, because if you round up, the chances of lost information are much higher than if you round down.\\r\\n- 2:42Some exceptions being, you know, if you really don\'t have a full idea of what\'s happened yet, you know, that you don\'t have any complete thought. OK, you can take on a little more text. But like I said, be really careful about going beyond one sentence at a time, especially to start.\\r\\n- 2:56But that read stage of the RTQ process, that\'s the first stage. You want to make sure that you\'re fully focusing on your read while you\'re doing it, that you\'re not mixing in other steps, especially in the beginning.\\r\\n- 3:08You want to have full focus on just taking in kind of like a one way street on a road that\'s like we\'re just bringing information into the brain in that read stage. And then the Translation stage is when we say back to ourselves the conceptual information, what we just read. And we\'re going\\r\\n- 3:24to say it in our own words, like we\'re telling a story to a friend. And then in our quip stage, this is the new stuff. This is when we react to what we just read.\\r\\n- 3:34We show curiosity, we show skepticism, we have an independent thought about what we just read, bringing ourselves into an active alignment with the author of the stimulus, having a little bit of a back and forth.\\r\\n- 3:45And that quip stage is super, super, super important to do after every every read read and translate stage, because it keeps you really conceptually alive throughout the stimulus, really active so that you\'re taking the question apart. And\\r\\n- 4:01essentially the quips are where you\'re getting the question right. And so that\'s why it\'s really important because you\'re actively taking the stimulus apart.\\r\\n- 4:08This is when you\'re essentially doing your analytical thinking to enable you to do the work to eventually arrive at the correct answer to the question.\\r\\n- 4:15It\'s kind of like your margin notes on the stimulus as you are going. And so that\'s the basics of RTQ.\\r\\n- 4:22I hope that that helped. We\'re just going to go into a lot more of the details on every individual stage in these next couple of videos.\\r\\n- 4:28So definitely stay tuned. All right. I\'ll see you soon.\\r\\n\\r\\n##### Day 2\\r\\n\\r\\n### Ellen\'s Notes\\r\\n\\r\\nAnd this one is for those who justly complain on Reddit that I already have too many acronyms in *The Loophole* 😘\\r\\n\\r\\n### Take Notes\\r\\n\\r\\n#\\r\\n\\\\nFILE training-camp\\\\translation\\\\Whats the Translation Standard Were Trying to Hit • LO.md\\\\n---\\r\\ntitle: \\"Whats the Translation Standard Were Trying to Hit • LO\\"\\r\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/translation/video/whats-the-translation-standard-were-trying-to-hit?source=timeline&plan=foundation-plan\\"\\r\\nauthor:\\r\\npublished:\\r\\ncreated: 2026-08-29\\r\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\r\\ntags:\\r\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\r\\n---\\r\\n## What\'s the Translation Standard We\'re Trying to Hit?\\r\\n\\r\\n1:23\\r\\n\\r\\n/\\r\\n\\r\\n2:36\\r\\n\\r\\n- 0:00What is the Translation standard we\'re trying to hit? You know, what does good look like? What is the Elysium at the end of the rainbow here?\\r\\n- 0:07It is that when you read and translate a stimulus, you have a 100% conceptual awareness, 100% conceptual retention of all of the information that is presented to you in that stimulus.\\r\\n- 0:19Now, that does not mean that you are literally reciting every word of the stimulus in the exact order it\'s given absolutely verbatim.\\r\\n- 0:27You\'re totally free to translate into your own words using your own language and also to take things out of order.\\r\\n- 0:35But when I say translate things in your own words, I don\'t mean take away the details. What I mean is save the details in your own words.\\r\\n- 0:44And maybe that\'s fewer words because you\'re able to conceptualize the information in a more efficient way, but it actually covers all of the information.\\r\\n- 0:52If that\'s so, that\'s totally fine. But I don\'t want you to take efficiency in your Translation as an excuse to leave information out\\r\\n- 0:59because you need all the concepts in the stimulus to be retained and presented within your Translation. And you wanna retain your entire Translation as you\'re going into the answer choice because you need that information,\\r\\n- 1:10that information interrelated with the information that\'s given to you in the answer choices is how you\'ll know what is correct and what is incorrect.\\r\\n- 1:17But the standard isn\'t just conceptual retention and conceptual awareness, it\'s also the way in which that conceptual awareness is gained.\\r\\n- 1:25Eventually what you want is for Translation and quipping to feel like breathing, feel like water, feel like something that you cannot turn off.\\r\\n- 1:33It\'s just when you read, you are naturally translating without having to volitionally tell yourself that you have to translate. And without having to say, okay, I need to remember this, look for this, remember that, you just do remember it. That is the goal of mastery that we\'re\\r\\n- 1:51hoping to achieve here. When I am going through and reading something, the reason why I didn\'t know Translation existed for so long is because I did not know that I was actually doing all of this.\\r\\n- 2:00It was just that at the end of reading a stimulus, I\'d of course be able to tell you everything that happened because I thought that was just the way people read.\\r\\n- 2:08That\'s what we want for you is that as you\'re going through, all of these things are naturally happening in a subconscious way that form a great process that at the end of the stimulus, you\'re like, yeah, of course I know what it said.\\r\\n- 2:18How could I not? Of course I know what it said. How could I not? Isn\'t that just such a gift of entitlement?\\r\\n- 2:24Of course I know what it said. How could I not? That\'s the standard that we\'re going for at the end of every stimulus in logical reasoning at the end of every passage as well.\\r\\n- 2:35All right, I\'ll see you soon.\\r\\n\\r\\n##### Day 5\\r\\n\\r\\n### Take Notes\\r\\n\\r\\n#\\r\\n"}\n{"chunk_id":"80c4c9","wall_time_seconds":0.6075592,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":7341,"output":"<stdin>:6: FutureWarning: Possible nested set at position 4\\r\\n\\\\nFILE training-camp\\\\translation\\\\Wont Quipping Slow Me Down • LO.md\\\\n---\\r\\ntitle: \\"Wont Quipping Slow Me Down • LO\\"\\r\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/translation/video/wont-quipping-slow-me-down?source=timeline&plan=foundation-plan\\"\\r\\nauthor:\\r\\npublished:\\r\\ncreated: 2026-08-27\\r\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\r\\ntags:\\r\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\r\\n---\\r\\n## Won\'t Quipping Slow Me Down?\\r\\n\\r\\n0:06\\r\\n\\r\\n/\\r\\n\\r\\n6:28\\r\\n\\r\\n- 0:00Won\'t quipping slow me down? No, no it will not. It actually speeds you up. And I know you probably think I\'m selling you a massive bill of goods right now that I am like, you know, some slimy person pounding the pavement for quipping.\\r\\n- 0:15But really, really, really, really, really, it actually does not slow you down. It actually does speed you up. And here\'s why.\\r\\n- 0:22I\'ll give you an analogy actually, illustrate it. So if you were, let\'s say, trying to get to an appointment on time and you\'ve got 10 minutes to navigate a mile and you are driving there, would you rather take the residential streets at the speed limit and have to go over\\r\\n- 0:41those speed bumps and stay at a measured pace? Or would you rather speed down the business highway and have a one in 10 chance of getting pulled over?\\r\\n- 0:49Because, and imagine you have to make this journey 25 times. Would you rather chance it where you have a one in 10 chance every time of getting pulled over?\\r\\n- 1:01Because I wouldn\'t. I would rather just drive on the residential streets at my nice measured pace, dealing with my speed bumps, and just generally, you know, enjoying my little mini roller coaster of the residential roads,\\r\\n- 1:12you know, the nice trees, whatever else, rather than risking getting pulled over because I\'m speeding on some kind of business highway.\\r\\n- 1:21And so my analogy means, you know, by Parallel Reasoning, we can assume that the nice residential street with the little speed bumps, that\'s doing your actual RTQ with real quipping.\\r\\n- 1:32Like we could say quipping is the speed bump because, oh, there\'s a cost there. Even when a quipp can take like literally one to two seconds, depending on how you quipp.\\r\\n- 1:40I don\'t want people sitting there and be like, well, it\'s like, that\'s not good quipping. If you\'re quipping like that, it means you need to work on your RTQ and get more intuitive.\\r\\n- 1:51But it can take seconds to quipp. So we\'ll say, you know, that\'s our speed bumps. But you know, the biggest time sink when you\'re doing the LSAT, the single, single biggest time sink, it is not knowing the answer.\\r\\n- 2:04Not knowing the answer costs all of us a lot more time than all the virtuous process in the world. And not knowing the answer is what I liken to getting pulled over.\\r\\n- 2:14Because if you adjust your process in such a way that it creates a lot of risk, for instance, you are, you know, just essentially moving through questions without really knowing what\'s going on.\\r\\n- 2:25And you\'re just like, well, I just got to go. I\'ve just got to pick all that different kind of stuff.\\r\\n- 2:29That\'s this kind of one in ten chance of getting pulled over. And the one in ten chance, you know, it could have resulted in the wrong answer.\\r\\n- 2:34That would be also a bad outcome. It doesn\'t fit my analogy quite as well. But the analogy really fits with the idea of I\'m down to B and D and I don\'t know which one\'s right.\\r\\n- 2:43I am now crossed off every answer and I don\'t know which one\'s right. They\'re all open. I don\'t know which one\'s right.\\r\\n- 2:48The penalty of time that you are going to have to pay or accuracy to get yourself out of that jam, even one or two times will outweigh an entire section\'s worth of quipping.\\r\\n- 3:01Granted, you\'ve got to be good at quipping for that math to be true. And you\'d probably be like, well, how much time have I spent?\\r\\n- 3:07You know, let\'s play with math all we want. But just go with me on the idea here that if you get pulled over, meaning not knowing the answer, even one or two more times because you didn\'t quip, you\'re actually in the time red from declaring to quip or declining to quip.\\r\\n- 3:23And, you know, why is that bad? And why should we pay this upfront cost instead? Well, you should pay this upfront cost because it makes you more likely to know the answer at later stages and less likely to be pulled over.\\r\\n- 3:35I\'ll give you a couple of examples just with answer choices and also with the CLIR. So let\'s hope you\'re actually CLIRing. You know, CLIRing is great.\\r\\n- 3:41But if you properly quip throughout the stimulus, your CLIR comes like butter so fast because you\'re actively engaging your mind in analytical operations on the text throughout your journey through the stimulus.\\r\\n- 3:55So that CLIR is there waiting for you right at the end of the stimulus. No delay, no pause, no wondering\\r\\n- 4:01or anything like that, because it\'s like essentially you\'ve been warming up the car the whole time that you\'ve been going through the stimulus.\\r\\n- 4:06It\'s there ready to go because you\'ve been analyzing everything you\'ve read the whole time. I often say the best quips or function as a sort of pre CLIR. And I really believe that,\\r\\n- 4:17like if you watch my equipping demo with A.I. Ellen, you saw that a lot of my quips were coming up as very skeptical. Right.\\r\\n- 4:23That\'s when quip is really functioning as a pre CLIR. And so it massively speeds up that CLIRing stage because essentially you\'re working on it all throughout the stimulus.\\r\\n- 4:30And so anytime you would have spent on the CLIR that would have been, you know, slow and sloggy and whatever else, you\'re saving by actually having quip throughout the stimulus.\\r\\n- 4:39And so when we get to the answer choices, though, if you have a better, more engaged memory of what happened in the stimulus and you\'re able to quip the answers, you\'re more likely to be able to actually pick an answer because you\'ll be able to deduce evidence of why it is right or wrong.\\r\\n- 4:53I know that this feels kind of like the eat your vegetables of LSAT prep, which, yes, is my specialty. But this kind of math really will work out and it\'ll also make you so much more confident that as you move forward through the question, you\'re acting based on real evidence and real knowledge,\\r\\n- 5:11because honestly, quipping is thinking. You know, we don\'t really want to just go and willy nilly make decisions impulsively without really thinking.\\r\\n- 5:20We want to actually take the time to think about what we\'re doing as we\'re doing it. And really, the direct content of the question is the thing we should be thinking about.\\r\\n- 5:32And so I hope that this showed you that even if you are making an initial investment in the time it takes to quip that time it takes to quip initially, it\'s actually really not that much time. And it can be saved through the avoidance\\r\\n- 5:48of these sorts of like time catastrophes that really do add up over the course of a section. Of course, you could retort to me that, oh, Ellen, like being down to two seconds, I\'m going to be able to do that. I would have come back at the end and all that\\r\\n- 6:02kind of stuff. And I would say, good for you for knowing the Loophole better than almost anyone I know. Thank you.\\r\\n- 6:08Thank you for that, Internet friend. And you know what? If you were hyper skipping, maybe that\'s true. Maybe you actually wouldn\'t burn all that time not knowing the answer. But you also probably risk getting it wrong and you would have to come back at the end.\\r\\n- 6:20So I still think I\'m right. But thank you for that imaginary retort, imaginary Internet friend. All right. I\'m going to go ahead and move on to the next question. I think I\'m right. But thank you for that imaginary retort, imaginary Internet friend. All right. I will see you\\r\\n- 6:25very soon on how we can do this in 35.\\r\\n\\r\\n##### Day 2\\r\\n\\r\\n### Take Notes\\r\\n\\r\\n## Video Course Review\\r\\n\\r\\nConcept Review\\r\\n\\\\nFILE training-camp\\\\translation\\\\Yes You Have to Know the Lists • LO.md\\\\n---\\r\\ntitle: \\"Yes You Have to Know the Lists • LO\\"\\r\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/translation/video/yes-you-have-to-know-the-lists?source=timeline&plan=foundation-plan\\"\\r\\nauthor:\\r\\npublished:\\r\\ncreated: 2026-08-29\\r\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\r\\ntags:\\r\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\r\\n---\\r\\n## Yes, You Have to Know the Lists\\r\\n\\r\\nIt could refer to those list items by name\\r\\n\\r\\n0:51\\r\\n\\r\\n/\\r\\n\\r\\n4:07\\r\\n\\r\\n- 0:00Yes, you have to know the lists. I know I\'m the Bearer [REDACTED] bad news here, but I don\'t know why everyone thinks you don\'t have to know the lists.\\r\\n- 0:08Because I rail on and on and on about how every word in the stimulus is the most important word in the stimulus,\\r\\n- 0:14all of this different kind of stuff, and yet when you\'re actually sitting there vetting someone\'s Translation live, they always get to a list.\\r\\n- 0:21And it could be as few as two items or something in a list, but especially if it gets to three, then all bets are off,\\r\\n- 0:29and they\'ll get there and they will not translate the individual list items. And when you\'ll say to them, hey, but you know, what about the, oh, did I have to do the list?\\r\\n- 0:38Like it somehow does not come across no matter what else I say as like, no, this is a responsibility that you have.\\r\\n- 0:45Like you do have to know the items in a list. And I guess I would wonder why does everyone think that the items in a list are meaningless simply because there\'s more than one item in a row?\\r\\n- 0:55You know, like it\'s really interesting because I think the brain wants to just categorize them out as like, this is a list of items or reasons or something as if they don\'t have like individual ideas to them.\\r\\n- 1:06And lists are just words too, right? There\'s like every other word of the stimulus. They are words, just the fact that there are multiple\\r\\n- 1:12items in a list that doesn\'t necessarily devalue any of the individual items. And so it\'s like, okay, well, but why? Why do you have to know these items in a list? Right?\\r\\n- 1:21Because the answer choices are going to expect you to know the items in this list. That the answer choices could touch upon any of those individual list items, either individually abstracting out commonalities in the list items. It could refer to those list items by name\\r\\n- 1:37or it could refer to them by a Translation of one of the names. And so if you don\'t really know what the list items say, well, you\'re going to just get yourself into some trouble in the answer choices, which obviously we don\'t want.\\r\\n- 1:47And so every individual list item that you don\'t know is basically an one more opportunity that the answer choices have to potentially exploit you and cause you to get the question wrong.\\r\\n- 1:56Obviously no one wants that. So we want you to remember the items in the list, but you might ask, okay, well, how do I remember these items that appear in lists?\\r\\n- 2:03And this of course is for either a passage or a stimulus or even in an answer choice, like anytime you see items in an ordered list.\\r\\n- 2:11Well, one way to remember them better is to individually intone each item with a different tone. Like for instance, if I wanted to remember apples, oranges and pears,\\r\\n- 2:23if I said it\'s apples, oranges and pears, I can\'t say oranges, apples, oranges and pears. And I don\'t really individually designate them in my head with a different tone.\\r\\n- 2:32It\'s a lot harder to remember apples, oranges and pears. Whereas instead, if I say apples, oranges and pears, notice that they all now sound more individualized.\\r\\n- 2:43They have like an individual meaning put onto them. And so it\'s like, oh, why are oranges in that list? Like you can kind of hear the curiosity in the way that I said oranges and the pears, the disappointment in pears.\\r\\n- 2:55I hate pears. I don\'t know why. I don\'t know why other people like pears, but it\'s fine. And so you can individually individually intone them and then you can individually quip list items as well.\\r\\n- 3:06You can have those little like microquip moments after each individual list item of like apples. You know, even that little sound, that little feeling is really good at locking in your memory as I hit my glasses of that apple list item and feeling that dislike for pears, feeling the surprise\\r\\n- 3:23with the oranges as you read it. All of those individual kind of microquips are going to do wonders for the memory to help you lock in that meaning.\\r\\n- 3:31So if you\'re engaging with them on an individual basis, you\'re intoning them on an individual basis and you translate them individually when you do the Translation step, it\'s going to be a lot, a lot easier to remember those list items. But honestly, the biggest thing that helps with remembering\\r\\n- 3:45a list is just accepting that like they are not less important. They are just as important as every other part of the stimulus. And so as soon as you elevate them to the level of like they\'re important, they are like they\'re they\'re good. I have to know them. These are not optional. Often\\r\\n- 4:01the issue goes away with them just to start. All right. I hope that that helps. It\'s a little micro video and I will see you soon.\\r\\n\\r\\n##### Day 5\\r\\n\\r\\n### Take Notes\\r\\n\\r\\n#\\r\\n\\\\nFILE workouts\\\\Accuracy Failsafe • LO.md\\\\n---\\r\\ntitle: \\"Accuracy Failsafe • LO\\"\\r\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/workouts-and-routines/workouts/accuracy-failsafe\\"\\r\\nauthor:\\r\\npublished:\\r\\ncreated: 2026-08-30\\r\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\r\\ntags:\\r\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\r\\n---\\r\\n## Quick Facts\\r\\n\\r\\n### Will This Help Me?\\r\\n\\r\\nThis will help you if\\r\\n\\r\\nYou need a systematic way to improve your accuracy that will automatically assign you workouts to improve your habits when needed.\\r\\n\\r\\nSkip for now if\\r\\n\\r\\nYou\'re nowhere near your accuracy goals.\\r\\n\\r\\nchallenge indexBuckle Up\\r\\n\\r\\nlevelIntermediate\\r\\n\\r\\nminimum time90+ minutes\\r\\n\\r\\n![clock](https://new.theloophole.com/_next/static/media/clock.f4c45ff9.svg)\\r\\n\\r\\ntiming compatible with35m, 53m, 70m, Marathon, Stopwatch, Natural Pace\\r\\n\\r\\n## Instructions\\r\\n\\r\\n**NOTE**: You can do an Accuracy Failsafe with any workout. Failsafes are time-training protocols that you can apply to any workout.\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Set your accuracy goal as a target number of wrong answers per section.\\r\\n\\t1. An accuracy goal could be -5, for example.\\r\\n2. Complete an LR or RC section\\\\* at your natural pace.\\r\\n3. Take a five-minute break and Camo the section you just completed.\\r\\n\\t1. If you have met your accuracy goal, proceed to a second section of the same type.\\r\\n\\t\\t1. This means if you just completed an RC section and hit your accuracy goal of -5, you would proceed to do another RC section with an accuracy goal of -5.\\r\\n\\t\\t2. If you have not met your accuracy goal on the first section, you have not beaten today\'s Failsafe.\\r\\n\\t\\t1. Do not decrease your target number of wrong answers tomorrow. Keep the same accuracy goal.\\r\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t2. Do not proceed to a second section of the same type today.\\r\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t3. Instead, Wrong Answer Journal and do one Recovery Workout (defined below) now.\\r\\n4. If you have proceeded to a second section, take a five-minute break and Camo it.\\r\\n5. If you have met your accuracy goal for the second time in a row, you have beaten today\'s Failsafe!\\r\\n\\t1. Wrong Answer Journal both of today\'s sections.\\r\\n\\t\\t2. Decrease your target number of wrong answers tomorrow.\\r\\n\\t\\t1. This means that if you beat today\'s Failsafe of -5 accuracy, try for -4 accuracy on tomorrow\'s Failsafe.\\r\\n6. If you do not meet the accuracy goal on the second section, you have not beaten today\'s Failsafe.\\r\\n\\t1. Wrong Answer Journal both of today\'s sections.\\r\\n\\t\\t2. Do not decrease your target number of wrong answers tomorrow. Keep the same accuracy goal.\\r\\n7. Return to Step 1 the following day.\\r\\n\\r\\nSections to use\\r\\n\\r\\nNew Sections Only\\r\\n\\r\\nhow to time\\r\\n\\r\\nCount-Up Stopwatch\\r\\n\\r\\nCountdown Timer\\r\\n\\r\\n## Ellen\'s Advice\\r\\n\\r\\n### If You Want to Win\\r\\n\\r\\n- Failsafes are a great Workout for the student who loves data and structured goals.\\r\\n- Don\'t be intimidated by all the steps you see in the Failsafe.\\r\\n\\t- Once you get into it, the flow of the Workout is relatively intuitive.\\r\\n\\t\\t- Essentially, if you meet your goals twice in a row, you move your goal. If you don\'t meet your goals twice in a row, you don\'t move your goal.\\r\\n- Which Recovery Workout should you choose?\\r\\n\\t- It depends on where you tend to feel difficulty throughout the section.\\r\\n\\t\\t- If you notice you miss questions from not translating the answer choices, going toward an Answer Choice Primer or MemAC could be a great move.\\r\\n\\t\\t- If you notice that you are making strategic mistakes, making a Highlight Reel is a safe bet.\\r\\n\\t\\t- You can be the judge of which Recovery Workout is best for you, but the Recovery Workouts are designed to not allow you to simply repeat the exact same mistakes that you made in the first section of the FailSafe.\\r\\n\\t\\t- Even if you\'re tempted to go outside the list of Recovery Workout, don\'t do one that involves timed question answering.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Real Examples\\r\\n\\r\\n***Recovery Workouts***\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\\\nFILE workouts\\\\Camo Review • LO.md\\\\n---\\r\\ntitle: \\"Camo Review • LO\\"\\r\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/workouts-and-routines/workouts/camo-review\\"\\r\\nauthor:\\r\\npublished:\\r\\ncreated: 2026-08-30\\r\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\r\\ntags:\\r\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\r\\n---\\r\\n## Quick Facts\\r\\n\\r\\n### Will This Help Me?\\r\\n\\r\\nThis will help you if\\r\\n\\r\\nYou are answering questions in any section and need to learn from your mistakes. (This is all of you 🕵🏻)\\r\\n\\r\\nSkip for now if\\r\\n\\r\\nYou have not beaten the Translation + CLIR time and accuracy goals.\\r\\n\\r\\nchallenge indexPretty Chill\\r\\n\\r\\nlevelBeginner\\r\\n\\r\\nminimum time15+ minutes\\r\\n\\r\\n![clock](https://new.theloophole.com/_next/static/media/clock.f4c45ff9.svg)\\r\\n\\r\\ntiming compatible withNot Timed\\r\\n\\r\\n## Instructions\\r\\n\\r\\n1. When you finish an LR or RC section in LawHub, do not press Complete Section. Instead, write down your answers.\\r\\n\\t1. Use Self-Paced Mode on all LR and RC sections. Never use Exam Mode.\\r\\n2. At least 24 hours later, enter your answers into Camo Review.\\r\\n\\t1. **Do not check your answers against the answer key before entering your answers into Camo Review.**\\r\\n3. Camo Review will give you a list of questions to return to.\\r\\n\\t1. This list includes all of your wrong answers and an unknown number of decoy correct answers.\\r\\n4. Reattempt all of the questions in your Camo Review from scratch. Attempt to do a better job on the question now than you did the first time.\\r\\n\\t1. Doing a better job means you should retranslate, requip, and re-CLIR the stimulus on LR or retranslate and requip the passage on RC. Retranslate every part of the question, consider every answer choice, and try to correct your process mistakes before choosing a new answer.\\r\\n5. Enter all your new answers into Camo Review.\\r\\n6. Camo Review will identify all your Misreads, Conceptual Gaps, Self-Doubt, and Self-Confidence answers.\\r\\n7. Your Misreads, Conceptual Gaps, and Self-Doubt answers have been automatically added to your Wrong Answer Journal. Go to your Wrong Answer Journal now to immediately begin the Wrong Answer Journal process.\\r\\n\\r\\nvideo walkthrough\\r\\n\\r\\n[How to Camo](https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/accuracy/video/how-to-camo)\\r\\n\\r\\nSections to use\\r\\n\\r\\nPreviously Seen Sections Only\\r\\n\\r\\nhow to time\\r\\n\\r\\nUntimed\\r\\n\\r\\n## Ellen\'s Advice\\r\\n\\r\\n### If You Want to Win\\r\\n\\r\\n- Never Camo before doing your sections for the day. Always Camo as the last LSAT thing you do for the day.\\r\\n\\t- If you Camo immediately before completing another section, you will be overly reactive to the result.\\r\\n\\t\\t- If the previous day\'s section went well, this can make you lazy.\\r\\n\\t\\t- If it went poorly, this can make you discouraged.\\r\\n\\t\\t- It\'s not worth taking the risk of either of these reactions affecting your performance on the next section.\\r\\n- The only point of Camo Review is to get a -0.\\r\\n\\t- Do not phone in the Camo Review or rechoose the answer you originally chose out of loyalty because you aren\'t sure it\'s wrong.\\r\\n\\t\\t- If you got more than two questions back in the Camo Review, some of the questions are definitely wrong, so you must change at least some of them.\\r\\n- Do not Camo a section immediately after completing it, unless you are directed explicitly to do so as part of another Workout or Routine.\\r\\n\\t- If you Camo a section immediately after completing it, the Camo score will be worse and you will lose a valuable opportunity to improve.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\\\nFILE workouts\\\\Decaf RC Translation Drill • LO.md\\\\n---\\r\\ntitle: \\"Decaf RC Translation Drill • LO\\"\\r\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/workouts-and-routines/workouts/decaf-rc-translation-drill\\"\\r\\nauthor:\\r\\npublished:\\r\\ncreated: 2026-08-27\\r\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\r\\ntags:\\r\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\r\\n---\\r\\n## Quick Facts\\r\\n\\r\\n### Will This Help Me?\\r\\n\\r\\nThis will help you if\\r\\n\\r\\nYou need to translate an RC passage. (Which is all of you? 😂)\\r\\n\\r\\nSkip for now if\\r\\n\\r\\nYou cannot RTQ a stimulus reliably with good process.\\r\\n\\r\\nchallenge indexBuckle Up\\r\\n\\r\\nlevelBeginner\\r\\n\\r\\nminimum section time32+ minutes\\r\\n\\r\\n![clock](https://new.theloophole.com/_next/static/media/clock.f4c45ff9.svg)\\r\\n\\r\\ntiming compatible withStopwatch\\r\\n\\r\\n## Instructions\\r\\n\\r\\n**NOTE**: Do your recaps out loud in the beginning to keep yourself honest about the quality and completeness of the recaps. Transition to silent recaps when you feel like you\'re ready.\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Read, translate, and quip paragraph 1\\\\*. Do it just like LR.\\r\\n\\t1. This means all the details are included just like they would be on LR.\\r\\n\\t\\t2. Quip after *every* Translation.\\r\\n\\t\\t3. This standard applies to every paragraph you ever translate on RC.\\r\\n2. Look away from paragraph 1 and recap the **FULL TRANSLATION** of paragraph 1 silently.\\r\\n\\t1. The recap is not a summary, gist, or main point.\\r\\n\\t\\t2. The recap should not omit names, dates, and lists.\\r\\n\\t\\t3. The recap should not be a shopping list of information. It should be a story in your own words.\\r\\n\\t\\t4. It is encouraged to mix up the order of the sentences and ideas.\\r\\n\\t\\t5. This standard applies to every paragraph (or collection of paragraphs) you ever recap on RC.\\r\\n3. Read, translate, and quip paragraph 2. Do it just like LR.\\r\\n4. Look away from paragraph 2 and recap the **FULL TRANSLATION** of paragraph 2 silently.\\r\\n5. Without looking back, recap the Translations of paragraphs 1 + 2 back to back silently.\\r\\n6. Read, translate, and quip paragraph 3. Do it just like LR.\\r\\n7. Look away from paragraph 3 and recap the **FULL TRANSLATION** of paragraph 3 silently.\\r\\n8. Without looking back, recap the Translations of paragraphs 1 + 2 + 3 back to back silently.\\r\\n9. Repeat this pattern for all remaining paragraphs.\\r\\n10. Complete this same process on all remaining passages in the section.\\r\\n\\r\\nvideo walkthrough\\r\\n\\r\\n[How and Why to Do Decaf RC Translation](https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/translation/video/how-and-why-to-do-decaf-rc-translation)\\r\\n\\r\\nSections to use\\r\\n\\r\\nMix in Previously Seen Sections\\r\\n\\r\\nhow to time\\r\\n\\r\\nCount-Up Stopwatch\\r\\n\\r\\nper passage time goal to move on\\r\\n\\r\\n12 min (outloud recaps), 8 min (silent recaps)\\r\\n\\r\\n## Ellen\'s Advice\\r\\n\\r\\n### If You Want to Win\\r\\n\\r\\n- If you have a 70-minute time accommodation, you can use Decaf RC Translation on test day. You do not have to master Cappuccino RC Translation and Caffeinated RC Translation for use on test day, if Decaf RC Translation offers superior Translation retention.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\\\nFILE workouts\\\\Method Flaw Argument Part Answer Choice Translation Spreadsheet • LO.md\\\\n---\\r\\ntitle: \\"Method Flaw Argument Part Answer Choice Translation Spreadsheet • LO\\"\\r\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/workouts-and-routines/workouts/method-flaw-argument-part-answer-choice-translation-spreadsheet\\"\\r\\nauthor:\\r\\npublished:\\r\\ncreated: 2026-08-30\\r\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\r\\ntags:\\r\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\r\\n---\\r\\n## Instructions\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Make a spreadsheet with three columns, or use [this template](https://docs.google.com/spreadsheets/d/1V7BS4BgrznJf_JQepzRK07dJw5yX0dZijLFSFYDqank/edit?usp=sharing):\\r\\n\\t1. **Column 1**: Vexing Answer Choice\\r\\n\\t\\t2. **Column 2**: Answer Choice Translation\\r\\n\\t\\t3. **Column 3**: Corresponding Stimulus\\r\\n2. Collect all the difficult answer choices to translate, focusing especially on Method/Flaw/Argument Part answer choices.\\r\\n\\t1. This could include harvesting answer choices from your sections immediately after you\'re done or going back over old sections to harvest answer choices.\\r\\n3. Write the difficult answers in the Vexing Answer Choice column of the spreadsheet, one answer choice per row.\\r\\n4. Translate the answers in the Answer Choice Translation column of the spreadsheet.\\r\\n5. In the Corresponding Stimulus column of the spreadsheet, write an example stimulus which would make the answer choice in that row correct.\\r\\n\\\\nFILE workouts\\\\Proto Stimulus Rebound • LO.md\\\\n---\\r\\ntitle: \\"Proto Stimulus Rebound • LO\\"\\r\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/workouts-and-routines/workouts/proto-stimulus-rebound\\"\\r\\nauthor:\\r\\npublished:\\r\\ncreated: 2026-08-30\\r\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\r\\ntags:\\r\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\r\\n---\\r\\n## Quick Facts\\r\\n\\r\\n### Will This Help Me?\\r\\n\\r\\nThis will help you if\\r\\n\\r\\nYou forget a significant amount of the stimulus by the time you finish the CLIR.\\r\\n\\r\\nSkip for now if\\r\\n\\r\\nYou retain the all information in the stimulus after you\'ve finished the CLIR.\\r\\n\\r\\nchallenge indexBuckle Up\\r\\n\\r\\nlevelBeginner\\r\\n\\r\\nminimum time25+ minutes\\r\\n\\r\\n![clock](https://new.theloophole.com/_next/static/media/clock.f4c45ff9.svg)\\r\\n\\r\\ntiming compatible withStopwatch\\r\\n\\r\\n## Instructions\\r\\n\\r\\n**NOTE**: Proto-Stimulus Rebounds may be done with or without answering the question.\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Translate, quip, and CLIR the stimulus\\\\*.\\r\\n2. Cover the stimulus and say the full Translation from memory\\r\\n\\t1. Try to reorder the argument parts in your retelling of the Translation.\\r\\n3. Relook at the stimulus and see if anything is missing, added, or changed.\\r\\n\\t1. Take a note of what was missing, added, or changed.\\r\\n4. Complete the rest of the question, if you choose to do so.\\r\\n\\r\\nSections to use\\r\\n\\r\\nMix in Previously Seen Sections\\r\\n\\r\\nhow to time\\r\\n\\r\\nCount-Up Stopwatch\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\\\nFILE workouts\\\\Translation Marathon • LO.md\\\\n---\\r\\ntitle: \\"Translation Marathon • LO\\"\\r\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/workouts-and-routines/workouts/translation-marathon\\"\\r\\nauthor:\\r\\npublished:\\r\\ncreated: 2026-08-30\\r\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\r\\ntags:\\r\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\r\\n---\\r\\n## Quick Facts\\r\\n\\r\\n### Will This Help Me?\\r\\n\\r\\nThis will help you if\\r\\n\\r\\nYou need to bring your Translation time down to meet the Translation time goals.\\r\\n\\r\\nSkip for now if\\r\\n\\r\\nYou are past the Translation stage of prep.\\r\\n\\r\\nchallenge indexEllen Whyyy\\r\\n\\r\\nlevelBeginner\\r\\n\\r\\nminimum time120+ minutes\\r\\n\\r\\n![clock](https://new.theloophole.com/_next/static/media/clock.f4c45ff9.svg)\\r\\n\\r\\ntiming compatible withStopwatch\\r\\n\\r\\n## Instructions\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Complete six to eight Translation + CLIR Drills\\\\* in one sitting with no breaks.\\r\\n\\r\\nSections to use\\r\\n\\r\\nNew Sections Only\\r\\n\\r\\nhow to time\\r\\n\\r\\nCount-Up Stopwatch\\r\\n\\r\\n## Ellen\'s Advice\\r\\n\\r\\n### If You Want to Win\\r\\n\\r\\n- **It is very important that you do not take breaks between sections or check your sections in the middle of the Marathon.**\\r\\n\\t- Marathons only work because your brain doesn’t get the chance to re-adapt to non-LSAT thinking.\\r\\n\\t\\t- Taking breaks guarantees that the Marathon will not work.\\r\\n\\t\\t- Do not check your phone.\\r\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t- Do not have conversations.\\r\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t- Do not take a lunch break.\\r\\n\\t\\t- Put snacks and drinks on the desk to eat while you Marathon, if needed.\\r\\n\\t\\t- The only breaks allowed during Translation Marathons are medically required or biologically required breaks.\\r\\n\\t\\t- Running to the bathroom is allowed between sections.\\r\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t- Accommodated breaks for medically required tasks (like insulin injections, etc) are obviously allowed.\\r\\n- Open all the sections you plan to complete in separate tabs before starting the Translation Marathon.\\r\\n- Keep track of the order of the sections in your Translation Marathon, so you can analyze the results.\\r\\n- If you feel yourself getting worse as the Translation Marathon progresses, you are likely becoming fatigued.\\r\\n\\t- This is not cause for alarm. Fatigue is part of the process; it indicates that you need to build stamina.\\r\\n- At the end of the Translation Marathon, take a break and then check all the sections against the CLIR Answer Key in a big batch.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Timing Tips\\r\\n\\r\\n- Set a section timer for 400 minutes in LawHub\'s Self-Paced Mode. **Do not use Exam Mode.**\\r\\n- Time each Translation + CLIR Drill with an external stopwatch. Record the time each drill takes in your tracker.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n"}'}]
