[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 0.5 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"9183fe","wall_time_seconds":0.2962143,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":7300,"output":"# Plan: Conditional Logic Cheat Sheet (LSAT U classroom note)\\r\\n_Round 0 — initial draft by Claude_\\r\\n\\r\\n## Goal\\r\\n\\r\\nA student-facing LSAT conditional-logic reference that renders inside the LSAT U\\r\\nclassroom as a `classroom_items` row (`content_html`, sanitized by DOMPurify\\r\\ndefault config, styled by `projects/lsatu/src/components/classroom/classroom.css`\\r\\nunder `.classroom-prose`). It must work as a *reference a student reaches for\\r\\nmid-drill*, not as a lesson. It is one of a pair; its sibling, The Breakdown\\r\\n(`corpus/concept-notes/conditionals-breakdown.lsatu.html`), teaches the same\\r\\nmaterial properly, one part at a time.\\r\\n\\r\\n**The artifact under review:** `corpus/concept-notes/conditionals-cheat-sheet.lsatu.html`\\r\\n\\r\\n## Current state (what is already built and live)\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **Grouped by what you do to the sentence**, using the class\'s own whiteboard\\r\\n   column names. The source class recording is\\r\\n   `lectures/conditional-logic/core/2026-05-28/script.md`; the instructor draws\\r\\n   four columns `LEFT / RIGHT / NEGATE LEFT / NEGATE RIGHT` (script.md:366 and\\r\\n   :475) and at 00:20:05 (script.md:556) says students who dislike the terms\\r\\n   *sufficient* and *necessary* \\"can also just say left and right.\\"\\r\\n   Section headings therefore read:\\r\\n   - 1. Words that put it on the left side of the arrow\\r\\n   - 2. Words that put it on the right side of the arrow\\r\\n   - 3. Words that negate the left side of the arrow\\r\\n   - 4. Words that negate the right side of the arrow\\r\\n   Sections 5-10 cover the traps: words on no list, and/or and the flip,\\r\\n   biconditionals, non-conditionals, the two illegal moves, when to draw at all.\\r\\n\\r\\n2. **Collapsible**, modelled on 7Sage\'s conditionals cheat sheet\\r\\n   (https://7sage.com/pages/free-lsat-resources-conditionals-cheat-sheet).\\r\\n   13 top-level `<details>`, all closed by default. Closed page height ~1550px,\\r\\n   down from ~9575px when it was flat. The list starts ~620px down the page so\\r\\n   roughly seven section names are visible without scrolling.\\r\\n\\r\\n3. **An example for every indicator word.** 44 words across the four groups\\r\\n   (19 / 16 / 4 / 5), each a table row: the word, a sentence using it, and the\\r\\n   arrow it produces. Two columns, arrow beneath the sentence, so nothing clips\\r\\n   at 375px.\\r\\n\\r\\n4. **A pattern panel per group**, taken from 7Sage\'s internal structure:\\r\\n   \\"It shows up like this\\" (sentence shapes using A and B), \\"Ask yourself\\"\\r\\n   (a diagnostic question that finds the trigger), \\"Which gives you\\"\\r\\n   (the bracket template rendered as tinted `left` / neutral `right` chips,\\r\\n   plus one worked example under a hairline).\\r\\n\\r\\n5. **Practice affordance:** each group has a \\"Diagram this\\" prompt with a bare\\r\\n   text input and a `<details>` \\"Check it\\" answer. Nothing is persisted.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Key decisions & tradeoffs (contest these)\\r\\n\\r\\n- **D1. Left/right vocabulary over sufficient/necessary.** The four group\\r\\n  headings name positions, not logical roles. *sufficient* and *necessary*\\r\\n  survive in exactly three places: the trigger/requirement diagram at the top,\\r\\n  the group-2 word list where *necessary* is itself an indicator, and the flaw\\r\\n  bridge (\\"when a flaw answer says the author confuses a necessary condition\\r\\n  with a sufficient one\\"). Tradeoff: the test\'s own answer choices use the\\r\\n  Latinate terms, so under-teaching them could cost points on Flaw questions.\\r\\n- **D2. One concrete example per word rather than 7Sage\'s head-word grouping.**\\r\\n  7Sage collapses variations under a head word (EVERY covers everyone,\\r\\n  everything, every time, everywhere). We keep all 44 words visible with an\\r\\n  example each. Tradeoff: longer, more repetitive, but nothing is hidden behind\\r\\n  a representative.\\r\\n- **D3. Everything collapsed by default, including \\"The four moves\\".** Nothing\\r\\n  is open on load. Tradeoff: a first-time reader sees only headings.\\r\\n- **D4. Inline styles only, driven by classroom CSS custom properties.** No\\r\\n  `<style>` block, because it would leak into the classroom globally. Tradeoff:\\r\\n  no media queries, so every layout must be width-agnostic. This already forced\\r\\n  one rebuild when a three-column table clipped at 375px.\\r\\n- **D5. Group 3 is named \\"negate the left\\" though its rule sends the attached\\r\\n  idea right.** This matches the class board\'s `NEGATE LEFT` column, which is\\r\\n  about *unless* meaning \\"whatever you put on the left, negate it.\\" Tradeoff:\\r\\n  the heading and the mechanic can read as contradictory on first pass.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Risks / open questions\\r\\n\\r\\n- **R1. Wording.** Joshua\'s standing note is that drafts do not sound like his\\r\\n  teaching. Which sentences still read as written-by-a-machine rather than said\\r\\n  in class? Be specific and quote them.\\r\\n- **R2. Layout.** Is the per-group pattern panel plus a 19-row table too much\\r\\n  inside one accordion section? Is there a better division?\\r\\n- **R3. Are any of the 44 examples wrong, ambiguous, or badly chosen?**\\r\\n  Especially group 2 bare *only* (\\"Only members may vote\\"), group 1\\r\\n  *provided that*, and group 3 *except*.\\r\\n- **R4. Redundancy.** \\"The four moves\\" section, the per-group rule paragraph,\\r\\n  and \\"All ten, in four steps\\" arguably say the same thing three times.\\r\\n- **R5. Does anything contradict the source class?** The class recording is the\\r\\n  authority. `corpus/concept-notes/conditionals-breakdown.lsatu.html` is the\\r\\n  sibling and must not disagree with this file.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Voice rules (hard constraints, non-negotiable)\\r\\n\\r\\n- No em dashes anywhere.\\r\\n- No AI-slop vocabulary (delve, tapestry, testament, demystify, boasts,\\r\\n  \\"in the realm of\\").\\r\\n- No quantifying what an action costs (\\"costs you time\\", \\"worth the two\\r\\n  seconds\\"). Logged as rule L-11.\\r\\n- No anthropomorphising (\\"the rules start linking to each other on their own\\").\\r\\n- No grandiose absolutes (\\"works on every conditional ever written\\").\\r\\n- Must match the class\'s board vocabulary.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Out of scope\\r\\n\\r\\n- The Breakdown (sibling file), except where it contradicts this one.\\r\\n- Publishing to any student. This is on Joshua\'s own account only.\\r\\n- Changing `classroom.css` or any app code.\\r\\n- Dark mode: `html.dark` never matches in the running app.\\r\\n# Plan Review Log: Conditional Logic Cheat Sheet\\r\\nStarted 2026-08-23 session. MAX_ROUNDS=5. Model gpt-5.6-sol, reasoning effort forced high.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n## Round 1 — Codex (gpt-5.6-sol, reasoning high)\\r\\n\\r\\nThis needs revision. The artifact contains two outright logic errors, several unreliable indicator examples, and a layout that behaves like a compressed lesson rather than a mid-drill reference.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Source and logic conflicts\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **Indicator words are overstated.** “One word tells you which is which” conflicts with the transcript, which says some conditionals have no indicator and must be recognized from universality, requirements, or certainty. Rewrite:\\r\\n\\r\\n   > Usually, an indicator tells you where the two ideas go. When there is no indicator, ask what triggers the rule and what must follow.\\r\\n\\r\\n2. **“Read every rule as the left requires the right” is too absolute.** The instructor says students may read a rule as either “if left, then right” or “left requires right,” depending on which sounds natural. Rewrite:\\r\\n\\r\\n   > Read it as “if left, then right.” When it sounds natural, you can also read it as “left requires right.”\\r\\n\\r\\n3. **The page omits the instructor’s exact “the only” exception.** “Only members may vote” correctly gives `vote → member`, but the transcript explicitly teaches that **the only** goes left. Add:\\r\\n\\r\\n   - Only experts can solve it: `solve → expert`\\r\\n   - The only experts here are lawyers: `expert → lawyer`\\r\\n\\r\\n4. **The `unless` instruction can cause a double negation.** “Read the word as if not, then put that on the left and negate it” is garbled. Rewrite:\\r\\n\\r\\n   > Replace “unless B” with “if not B.” Put NOT B on the left and the other idea on the right.\\r\\n\\r\\n5. **The `no` section hides the symmetry taught in class.** The instructor taught that either term may go left, provided the other term is negated on the right. Rewrite:\\r\\n\\r\\n   > With “no,” either idea can go on the left. Put the other idea on the right and negate it. The two diagrams are contrapositives.\\r\\n\\r\\n6. **“Words that are on no list” contradicts both the transcript and the artifact.** `Prerequisite`, `depends on`, `will`, and `never` already appear in the tables. The instructor also explicitly lists `depends` and `prerequisite` as requirement language. Delete this section and retain only the fallback question about trigger and requirement.\\r\\n\\r\\n7. **`Otherwise` is taught incorrectly.** From `NOT deposit → released`, the contrapositive `NOT released → deposit` is the same rule, not a second direction. It does not prove “Either both things happen or neither does.” Delete `otherwise` from the biconditional section. The transcript instead teaches:\\r\\n\\r\\n   - if and only if\\r\\n   - all that is required\\r\\n   - all that is needed\\r\\n\\r\\n8. **The artifact avoids the word `contrapositive`.** The instructor repeatedly names contraposition as a core skill. Use “contrapositive, or flip” once, then either term afterward.\\r\\n\\r\\n9. **The condensed flip instructions are incomplete.** Several places say only “switch every and to an or.” The class teaches both directions. Every summary should say:\\r\\n\\r\\n   > Swap the sides, negate every term, and switch AND ↔ OR.\\r\\n\\r\\n10. **The necessary/sufficient flaw is narrowed incorrectly.** The HTML says it means claiming the left from the right. The instructor says the flaw family includes both illegal reversal and illegal negation. Rewrite:\\r\\n\\r\\n   > That flaw description can refer to either illegal move: claiming the left from the right, or negating the right because the left is false.\\r\\n\\r\\n11. **The drawing advice contradicts the class.** “One conditional in an ordinary argument. Do not draw” is too categorical. The instructor advises beginners to diagram and contrapose until the operation is automatic, and experienced students to draw when rules might connect, a fact might trigger a rule, or the notation protects them from a trap. Rewrite:\\r\\n\\r\\n   > If you are still learning, write the rule and its contrapositive. Later, draw when rules may connect, when a fact may trigger a rule, or when writing it down helps you reject a reversal or negation.\\r\\n\\r\\n12. **Applying a rule to a fact is missing from “All ten, in four steps.”** The transcript names this as the fifth core skill. Add:\\r\\n\\r\\n   > Check the facts. If a fact gives you the left side, apply the rule. If it denies the right side, apply the contrapositive.\\r\\n\\r\\n13. **“Find the hole and you have found the answer” overclaims.** `B → C` is one common bridge from `A → B` to `A → C`, not a universal answer formula. Qualify it as a common necessary-assumption pattern and require genuine term matching.\\r\\n\\r\\n14. **The causal-language rows fight the opening distinction in the class.** The transcript separates causation from conditional rules. Generic `leads to`, `produces`, and `signals` statements should not be treated as guarantees unless the example establishes a rule or closed system.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The 44 examples\\r\\n\\r\\nThe file contains exactly 44 rows. The following are wrong, ambiguous, factually unsafe, or badly chosen. The remaining rows diagram correctly as written.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Indicator | Problem | Replacement |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| `wherever` | “Wherever the trail floods, it closes” has unclear spatial scope and an ambiguous `it`. | “Wherever the river floods the road, that stretch closes.” `flooded stretch → closed stretch` |\\r\\n| `provided that` | Permission language makes waiting sound necessary, while the table wants the formal sufficient reading. | “Provided that the badge scans, the door opens.” `badge scans → door opens` |\\r\\n| `leads to` | “Skipping review leads to repeat errors” states a tendency, not a guarantee. | “Under the policy, submitting late leads to automatic rejection.” `late → rejected` |\\r\\n| `produces` | “Heat produces expansion” is not universally true without qualifications. | “In this machine, pressing START produces a receipt.” `press START → receipt` |\\r\\n| `guarantees` | A 175 does not generally guarantee an interview. This is especially bad misinformation for LSAT students. | “Under the contest rules, a perfect score guarantees a place in the final.” |\\r\\n| `ensures` | A seal does not logically ensure freshness. | “Under this rule, filing by noon ensures same-day review.” |\\r\\n| `always` | “Rushed questions always cost points” is a false absolute and violates the plan’s wording rule. | “Late applications are always rejected.” |\\r\\n| `only` | The direction is correct, but `may vote` becomes actual `vote`. | “Only members received ballots.” `received ballot → member` |\\r\\n| `prerequisite` | Calculus is not universally a prerequisite for physics. | “Course 101 is a prerequisite for Course 201.” |\\r\\n| `needs` | “The plant needs light to bloom” is vague about the relevant event. | “The engine needs fuel to start.” `starts → fuel` |\\r\\n| `essential` | “Trust is essential to a partnership” is rhetorical rather than rule-like. | “A signature is essential for a valid submission.” |\\r\\n| `depends on` | “The picnic depends on the weather” does not say “good weather”; the arrow invents that idea. | “The launch depends on a successful safety inspection.” |\\r\\n| `then` | `If`, not `then`, creates the conditional. `Then` is not a standalone right-side indicator. | Replace the row with `is contingent on`: “The launch is contingent on approval.” |\\r\\n| `implies` | Smoke does not guarantee fire. | “Being a square implies having four sides.” |\\r\\n| `signals` | Sirens can be tests or false alarms. | “On this map, a blue triangle signals a campsite.” |\\r\\n| `means` | A red tag has no fixed real-world meaning. | “In this filing system, a red tag means the file is closed.” |\\r\\n| `will` | Future tense does not independently create a conditional; the example works because of `if`. | Replace the row with `is conditional on`: “Publication is conditional on approval.” |\\r\\n| `unless` | The arrow is correct, but `cannot...unless` hides the basic operation behind a double negative. | “The store closes unless a manager is present.” `NOT manager → closes` |\\r\\n| `except` | Bare `except` is context-dependent, and the arrow changes permission to actual entry. | Label the form `no...except`: “No one entered except members.” `entered → member` |\\r\\n| `no` | “No freshman may apply” should produce `NOT permitted`, not necessarily `NOT apply`. | “No freshman is eligible to apply.” |\\r\\n| `never` | “Rushed work is never accurate” is another false, scolding absolute. | “Express trains never stop at Cedar Station.” |\\r\\n| `not` | Bare `not` negates; it does not create a conditional. The generic plural is doing the universal work. | Replace with `nobody`: “Nobody under 18 entered.” |\\r\\n\\r\\nAlso rewrite “Whenever she drills, she reviews it after” as:\\r\\n\\r\\n> Whenever she finishes a drill, she reviews every question she missed.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Machine-written wording\\r\\n\\r\\nThese are the clearest offenders.\\r\\n\\r\\n- “Every conditional is one sentence with a trigger and a requirement.”\\r\\n\\r\\n  > A conditional has two parts: a trigger and what that trigger requires.\\r\\n\\r\\n- “One word tells you which is which. Find it and you know where both halves go.”\\r\\n\\r\\n  > Find the indicator first. It usually tells you which idea triggers the rule and which idea is required.\\r\\n\\r\\n- “Read every rule as the left requires the right, and the ones that link get easy to spot.”\\r\\n\\r\\n  > Read the arrow as “if left, then right.” Then check whether one rule ends where another begins.\\r\\n\\r\\n- “There are only four moves. Every word on this page does one of them.”\\r\\n\\r\\n  > Each conditional indicator tells you to do one of four things.\\r\\n\\r\\n- “Nothing you type is saved, so guess freely when you are not sure.”\\r\\n\\r\\n  > Write your best diagram before you open the answer. This box does not save what you type.\\r\\n\\r\\n- “Same sentence as the one above, one word different, and the arrow points the other way.”\\r\\n\\r\\n  > Compare the two scholarship examples. Adding “only” reverses the arrow.\\r\\n\\r\\n- “The two routes always give you a rule and its flip, so if yours came out as the other one, you are still right.”\\r\\n\\r\\n  > `NOT review → NOT raise score` is correct too. It is the contrapositive.\\r\\n\\r\\n- “The test uses plenty of words nobody wrote down, and you do not need them memorised.”\\r\\n\\r\\n  > When there is no familiar indicator, ask what triggers the rule and what must follow.\\r\\n\\r\\n- “Flipping a rule is what the test is really checking. Three steps, and the third is where the points are.”\\r\\n\\r\\n  > To write the contrapositive, swap the sides, negate every term, and switch AND with OR.\\r\\n\\r\\n- “Some sentences hand you the rule going both ways. Two words do it, and they do not work the same way.”\\r\\n\\r\\n  > “If and only if” gives you both directions.\\r\\n\\r\\n- “Put those two beside each other and both directions are covered. Either both things happen or neither does.”\\r\\n\\r\\n  > Delete. The underlying logic is false.\\r\\n\\r\\n- “These sit next to no and behave nothing like it. They count.”\\r\\n\\r\\n  > “Few” and “not all” use a similar translation, but neither gives you a rule about every member of the group.\\r\\n\\r\\n- “Two of the four prove something. The other two prove nothing at all.”\\r\\n\\r\\n  > Cut. The panel directly above already says this.\\r\\n\\r\\n- “Find the hole and you have found the answer.”\\r\\n\\r\\n  > If the argument needs `B → C`, look for that bridge or its contrapositive.\\r\\n\\r\\n- “Say the answer out loud before you open each one, and you will know which group still needs work.”\\r\\n\\r\\n  > Answer each one before you open it. If one stalls you, return to that section.\\r\\n\\r\\n- “Nothing. Claiming the left from the right is reading the arrow backwards, and it is the most commonly tested flaw on the test.”\\r\\n\\r\\n  > Nothing follows. Knowing the right side does not prove the left.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Layout\\r\\n\\r\\nThe accordion division is wrong.\\r\\n\\r\\n- All 13 sections are closed, and the summaries do not contain the words students will search for. A student looking up `only` must already know it belongs in the right-side group.\\r\\n- The four content types are mixed as peers: indicator lookup, notation, traps, strategy, and practice.\\r\\n- Yes, a pattern panel plus a 19-row table plus an input plus a nested answer is far too much for one section. Group 1 is a lesson module.\\r\\n- Important reference information is rendered at `.78em`, `.85em`, and 10px while framing copy gets more visual weight.\\r\\n- Nested “Check it” accordions create unnecessary tapping.\\r\\n- The unsaved inputs consume space without giving meaningful practice feedback.\\r\\n- “All ten, in four steps” is buried and collapsed even though it is supposed to summarize the page.\\r\\n\\r\\nReorganize it as follows:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **Always-visible quick map**\\r\\n\\r\\n   Four compact rows containing the rule, core words, and one example:\\r\\n\\r\\n   - Left: if, when, all, every\\r\\n   - Right: only, only if, must, requires\\r\\n   - Negate left: unless, until, without\\r\\n   - Negate right: no, none, never, cannot\\r\\n\\r\\n2. **Tricky wording**\\r\\n\\r\\n   Give `only` versus `the only`, `provided that`, `unless`, and constrained `except` their own annotated examples.\\r\\n\\r\\n3. **Full indicator index**\\r\\n\\r\\n   Four accordions whose summaries include the actual words. Put the complete tables here under “See all examples.” Remove the pattern panels.\\r\\n\\r\\n4. **Contrapositive and inference**\\r\\n\\r\\n   Put swap/negate/AND↔OR, legal inferences, and the two illegal moves together.\\r\\n\\r\\n5. **When to diagram**\\r\\n\\r\\n   Include beginner versus experienced advice, fact-triggering, `few`, `not all`, and one qualified missing-link example.\\r\\n\\r\\nMove all practice inputs and “Test yourself” to The Breakdown, or collect one short practice block at the end.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Redundancy to cut\\r\\n\\r\\n- “The four moves,” four group introductions, four pattern panels, “All ten, in four steps,” and “Test yourself” repeat the same rules.\\r\\n- Rain, pass/attend, enter/pass, and freshman/apply each appear in both a pattern panel and the first table row.\\r\\n- Section 5 repeats `prerequisite`, `depends on`, `will`, and `never`.\\r\\n- Contraposition is explained in Group 3, Section 6, the recap, and the quiz.\\r\\n- Drawing advice appears in Section 10, the recap, and the quiz.\\r\\n- `few`, `only if`, `unless`, and the illegal moves are taught and then immediately retested.\\r\\n- Four “Diagram this” blocks plus the final quiz duplicate the teaching function assigned to The Breakdown.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe clean cut is: one visible quick map, one complete index, one tricky-words section, one contrapositive/inference section, and one qualified drawing section. No files were modified.\\r\\n\\r\\nVERDICT: REVISE\\r\\n\\r\\n### Claude\'s response — triage\\r\\n\\r\\n**VERIFIED TRUE, fixing (real defects):**\\r\\n- **`otherwise` presented as biconditional.** Section 7 says `NOT deposit -> released`\\r\\n  flipped to `NOT released -> deposit` means \\"both directions are covered. Either both\\r\\n  things happen or neither does.\\" That is FALSE: those two are the same rule, a\\r\\n  contrapositive, not a second direction. The file commits the exact reversal error it\\r\\n  warns against two sections later. Highest-severity finding of the round.\\r\\n- **Section 5 \\"Words that are on no list\\" contradicts the tables.** Verified by script:\\r\\n  `prerequisite`, `depends on`, `will`, `never` each appear BOTH in a group table and in\\r\\n  section 5. The section\'s premise is false as written.\\r\\n- **`depends on` arrow invents a term.** \\"The picnic depends on the weather\\" ->\\r\\n  `picnic -> good weather`. Claude found this independently before Codex returned;\\r\\n  independent agreement, so treated as confirmed.\\r\\n\\r\\n**VERIFIED FALSE, rejected:**\\r\\n- **\\"The transcript explicitly teaches `the only` goes left.\\"** Checked every occurrence\\r\\n  of \\"the only\\" in script.md (lines 671, 769, 1109, 1406, 1409, 1522, 1626, 1737). All are\\r\\n  incidental prose (\\"the only way for them to connect\\", \\"the only thing in LSAT that\\r\\n  you\'ll be memorizing\\") or answer-choice text in a practice question. The instructor\\r\\n  never teaches \\"the only\\" as an indicator. Codex fabricated the sourcing.\\r\\n  NOTE: the *pedagogical* point stands on its own merit (only X vs the only X is a real\\r\\n  and commonly tested LSAT distinction) and is worth adding, but on its own authority,\\r\\n  not on a false claim about the class.\\r\\n\\r\\n**DEFERRED TO JOSHUA (product/pedagogy, not Claude\'s call):**\\r\\n- The full restructure: always-visible quick map, drop the four pattern panels, collapse\\r\\n  13 sections to 5, move all practice inputs to The Breakdown.\\r\\n- Dropping `then`, `will`, and bare `not` as standalone indicator rows.\\r\\n- The ~20 example replacements for factual safety.\\r\\n# Chapter 5 — Assumptions & The Objection\\r\\n\\r\\n*Transcribed from the quiz screenshots in [[Untitled 3.md]]. \\"Loophole\\" is rendered throughout as \\"Objection\\"; everything else is verbatim. Questions 1, 2, and 6 were not captured.*\\r\\n\\r\\n## Question 3\\r\\n\\r\\n*Hoodoo and Keith are kitten best friends who routinely engage in mischievous conduct spanning everything from play-wrestling to napping on radiators. As a pair, they have already gnawed through half a cigar, gotten trapped under an area rug, and claimed the counter as their own. Therefore, Hoodoo and Keith knocked over a vase of daffodils.*\\r\\n\\r\\nWhich one of the following is not an Objection in the argument above?\\r\\n\\r\\n- A. What if kitten best friends aren\'t strong enough to knock over a vase?\\r\\n- **B. What if knocking over a vase of daffodils doesn\'t qualify as mischievous conduct?** ✅\\r\\n- C. What if Hoodoo and Keith have never had access to a vase of daffodils?\\r\\n\\r\\n## Question 4\\r\\n\\r\\nHow do you design an Objection?\\r\\n\\r\\n- A. What if the conclusion is not true?\\r\\n- B. What if one of the premises is not really true?\\r\\n- **C. What if [really bad thing for the argument]?** ✅\\r\\n\\r\\n## Question 5\\r\\n\\r\\nHow will you know if your Objection works?\\r\\n\\r\\n- **A. If the Objection is possible, the conclusion is no longer proven.** ✅\\r\\n- B. The Objection works as a valid prephrase.\\r\\n- C. The Objection addresses key concepts in the argument.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Question 7\\r\\n\\r\\n*Keith is afraid of new people and gets stressed in new environments. He is spending the weekend at his aunt\'s house for the first time, so he will hide all weekend.*\\r\\n\\r\\nWhich one of the following is an Objection for the argument above?\\r\\n\\r\\n- A. What if Keith won\'t hide?\\r\\n- **B. What if Keith doesn\'t hide when he\'s stressed?** ✅\\r\\n- C. What if Keith\'s aunt cancels their weekend plans?\\r\\n\\r\\n## Question 8 — explanation\\r\\n\\r\\n*Keith is afraid of new people and gets stressed in new environments. He is spending the weekend at his aunt\'s house for the first time, so he will hide all weekend.*\\r\\n\\r\\n*What if Keith\'s aunt cancels their weekend plans?*\\r\\nThe premises told us that Keith is spending the weekend at his aunt\'s house. We can\'t negate the premises.\\r\\n\\r\\n*What if Keith won\'t hide?*\\r\\nThe conclusion claims that Keith will hide. We can\'t just negate the conclusion and call it an Objection.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Question 9\\r\\n\\r\\n*Hoodoo and Keith love to watch reality TV and paw at the speaker when people speak. This proves that all felines do in fact respond to language, since Hoodoo and Keith are house cats.*\\r\\n\\r\\nWhich one of the following is an Objection for the argument above?\\r\\n\\r\\n- A. What if Hoodoo and Keith don\'t really understand language but instead are simply responding to the sounds of reality TV?\\r\\n- B. What if Hoodoo and Keith are becoming more like wild cats?\\r\\n- **C. What if Hoodoo and Keith are not representative of all felines?** ✅\\r\\n\\r\\n## Question 10 — explanation\\r\\n\\r\\n*Hoodoo and Keith love to watch reality TV and paw at the speaker when people speak. This proves that all felines do in fact respond to language, since Hoodoo and Keith are house cats.*\\r\\n\\r\\n*What if Hoodoo and Keith don\'t really understand language but instead are simply responding to the sounds of reality TV?*\\r\\nThe argument only assumes that Hoodoo and Keith \\"respond\\" to language, not that they understand language. Pointing out that Hoodoo and Keith may not understand language does not affect the validity of the argument.\\r\\n\\r\\n*What if Hoodoo and Keith are becoming more like wild cats?*\\r\\nHoodoo and Keith becoming more like wild cats does not affect whether or not we can generalize from their behaviors to those of all felines.\\r\\n# Objection — Mini-Quiz\\r\\n\\r\\n5 questions. Pick one answer each. Answer key at the bottom.\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n### 1\\r\\n\\r\\n*Hoodoo and Keith are kitten best friends who routinely engage in mischievous conduct spanning everything from play-wrestling to napping on radiators. As a pair, they have already gnawed through half a cigar, gotten trapped under an area rug, and claimed the counter as their own. Therefore, Hoodoo and Keith knocked over a vase of daffodils.*\\r\\n\\r\\nWhich one of the following is **not** an Objection in the argument above?\\r\\n\\r\\n- A. What if kitten best friends aren\'t strong enough to knock over a vase?\\r\\n- B. What if knocking over a vase of daffodils doesn\'t qualify as mischievous conduct?\\r\\n- C. What if Hoodoo and Keith have never had access to a vase of daffodils?\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n### 2\\r\\n\\r\\nHow do you design an Objection?\\r\\n\\r\\n- A. What if the conclusion is not true?\\r\\n- B. What if one of the premises is not really true?\\r\\n- C. What if [really bad thing for the argument]?\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n### 3\\r\\n\\r\\nHow will you know if your Objection works?\\r\\n\\r\\n- A. If the Objection is possible, the conclusion is no longer proven.\\r\\n- B. The Objection works as a valid prephrase.\\r\\n- C. The Objection addresses key concepts in the argument.\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n### 4\\r\\n\\r\\n*Keith is afraid of new people and gets stressed in new environments. He is spending the weekend at his aunt\'s house for the first time, so he will hide all weekend.*\\r\\n\\r\\nWhich one of the following is an Objection for the argument above?\\r\\n\\r\\n- A. What if Keith won\'t hide?\\r\\n- B. What if Keith doesn\'t hide when he\'s stressed?\\r\\n- C. What if Keith\'s aunt cancels their weekend plans?\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n### 5\\r\\n\\r\\n*Hoodoo and Keith love to watch reality TV and paw at the speaker when people speak. This proves that all felines do in fact respond to language, since Hoodoo and Keith are house cats.*\\r\\n\\r\\nWhich one of the following is an Objection for the argument above?\\r\\n\\r\\n- A. What if Hoodoo and Keith don\'t really understand language but instead are simply responding to the sounds of reality TV?\\r\\n- B. What if Hoodoo and Keith are becoming more like wild cats?\\r\\n- C. What if Hoodoo and Keith are not representative of all felines?\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n## Answer key\\r\\n\\r\\n**1. B** — the other two attack the argument; this one doesn\'t.\\r\\n\\r\\n**2. C** — you don\'t negate the conclusion or the premises. You ask what would wreck the argument.\\r\\n\\r\\n**3. A** — if the Objection is possible and the conclusion no longer follows, it works.\\r\\n\\r\\n**4. B** — A negates the conclusion, C negates a premise. B breaks the link between stress and hiding.\\r\\n\\r\\n**5. C** — A: the argument only claims they respond, not that they understand. B: turning into wild cats doesn\'t touch whether you can generalize. C: kills the generalization from two house cats to all felines.\\r\\n"}'}]

## Tool: exec

text(await tools.apply_patch("*** Begin Patch\n*** Add File: C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/corpus/loophole-online/method-library.md\n+---\n+type: synthesis\n+subject: Recovered Loophole protocols for instructor use\n+created: 2026-10-08\n+updated: 2026-10-08\n+aliases: [Loophole drill library]\n+tags: [llm-wiki, lsat, teacher-research]\n+sources:\n+  - ../../wiki/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07.md\n+---\n+\n+# Choose a practice protocol from the student's actual difficulty\n+\n+This private instructor library compiles the operational details that were missing from the earlier [overview](index.md). Methods are attributed to Ellen Cassidy's captured Loophole Online lessons, not automatically adopted as mandatory Joshua curriculum. Existing topic pages and [Joshua's selected teaching standard](../../references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md) govern student-facing explanations. The annotated lecture editions linked below preserve the captured explanations and examples for deeper retrieval; they do not claim recovery of unprovided videos or images.\n+\n+## Read, translate, react, then check the reasoning\n+\n+1. Read one sentence or a smaller complete conceptual chunk. Avoid collecting several sentences before translating while learning.\n+2. Look away and retell its full conceptual content in ordinary language. Reorder ideas when useful, but retain qualifiers, dates, scope, comparisons and every list item. Reading again while paraphrasing is not a memory check.\n+3. React to this exact content. Give an example, make a connection, notice a possible problem, or ask a question and answer it. A generic verdict such as “that's interesting” does not show what was understood.\n+4. Repeat with the next chunk. Spontaneous micro-reactions may supplement the formal reaction stage; they do not replace translating the chunk.\n+5. At the end, produce the relevant analytical output: controversy, loophole, inference or resolution (CLIR). A final reaction can already be the CLIR, so another ceremonial step is unnecessary.\n+6. For an answer choice, translate the entire choice before judging it. Explain its relationship to the actual stimulus and question task. Start with evidence, then reach a verdict, rather than deciding first and looking for a justification.\n+\n+For the source drill, speak the process aloud in one continuous recording. Do not pause recording between steps or stimuli. Cassidy prohibits skipping stimuli in this translation drill; that is not a general prohibition on test-day question triage. Verbalize exploratory thinking instead of silently editing it into a perfect reaction. Exploratory reactions may be wrong; an inference or final answer still requires logical checking.\n+\n+When a student freezes, ask a concrete question about what they just read and require an attempted answer. Distinguish lack of understanding from fear that the reaction is not impressive enough. Do not impose the source's coercive rhetoric as a teaching interaction. Optimize for reaching an accurate insight rather than merely finishing the text quickly. Silent performance is a later transition after the process is reliable, not a reason to omit learning steps now.\n+\n+Sources: [translation drill](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/translation/How and Why to Do Translation Plus CLIR Drills • LO.md>), [full translation](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/translation/Translate Prove You Were Listening • LO.md>), [micro-quips](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/translation/Micro Quips and Stream of Consciousness Magic • LO.md>), [answer-choice evidence](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/translation/How and Why to Quip the Answer Choices • LO.md>), and [complete reading lecture edition](reading-lectures.generated.md).\n+\n+## Repair missing words, qualifiers and list items\n+\n+For an unfamiliar word, finish the thought, return to it and propose a meaning from the surrounding text. Mark it and collect it after the section into a vocabulary tracker. Verify the meaning and practice related roots and usages. The guess remains provisional: revise it when later evidence conflicts, and never turn confidence in a guess into proof that an answer is correct. The source's “90-plus-percent” guess accuracy and advice to lock in even a wrong guess are not established rules.\n+\n+For lists, retain each member rather than substituting “several things.” Cassidy suggests distinct intonation or a brief reaction for each item. For qualifiers and dependent clauses, ask which group, time, degree or exception they constrain. Her example of missing “recent” illustrates accidentally applying an earlier time period to new findings. Her omitted dependent-clause example illustrates widening a conclusion beyond its stated group. A repair must name the dropped meaning, not merely say “pay closer attention.”\n+\n+Ordinary vocabulary and context enable comprehension. External specialist facts do not replace what the question supplies. A learner can connect an oyster passage with ordinary experience without importing an external species count into the answer evidence.\n+\n+Sources: [unknown words](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/translation/Take a Guess at Words You Dont Know • LO.md>), [scope sensitivity](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/translation/Why Its Good to Be Hypersensitive • LO.md>), [lists](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/translation/Yes You Have to Know the Lists • LO.md>), [outside knowledge](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/translation/Why Outside Knowledge Is Not the Devil • LO.md>).\n+\n+## Check whether the stimulus survives analysis in memory\n+\n+**Proto-Stimulus Rebound:** translate, react and produce the CLIR; cover the stimulus; retell it completely from memory, preferably with the argument parts in a different order; compare with the text and record each missing, added or changed concept. Answer the question afterward if that is part of the assignment. Previously seen stimuli can be mixed with fresh ones; use a count-up stopwatch if measuring the process. Skip the extra memory audit when the learner already reliably retains the stimulus.\n+\n+For RC, follow the existing [cumulative-translation protocol](<../Curriculum/Reading Comprehension/Notes/cumulative-translation-review.md>): translate/react to paragraph 1 and recall it; do the same for paragraph 2, then recall 1+2; repeat cumulatively through the passage. These are full conceptual retellings, not shopping lists or main-point summaries. The source permits aloud recaps before silent ones and previously seen passages. Reliable stimulus RTQ is its prerequisite. Source goals of 12 minutes per passage with aloud recaps and 8 with silent recaps remain attributed training targets, not general test-day prescriptions.\n+\n+Sources: [Rebound](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/workouts/Proto Stimulus Rebound • LO.md>), [Decaf workout](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/workouts/Decaf RC Translation Drill • LO.md>), and [Decaf concept notes](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/concept-review/How and Why to Do Decaf RC Translation • LO.md>).\n+\n+## Turn opaque descriptions into concrete reasoning\n+\n+Mark a difficult Method, Flaw or Argument Part choice during a section, without interrupting the section to do this drill. Afterward, make a three-column row: exact answer text; accurate own-words translation; an invented stimulus in which that description would be correct. Typing the full choice makes every word confrontable. Look up uncertain vocabulary or ask for help during review. Compare the invented case against every component of the description.\n+\n+Cassidy's flower example describes drawing a distinction: two blue garden flowers appear alike but have different petal counts, so the speaker distinguishes them. That demonstrates the intended reasoning action; it does not prove that differing petal counts establish different species. The possible within-species variation is itself an objection to the illustrative argument.\n+\n+For Method, identify the action the author performs across the whole argument. Do not substitute your judgment that the argument is bad. Practice unfamiliar descriptive vocabulary until it can be used accurately in a casual sentence. Cassidy's suggested five vocabulary usages per day is an optional source exercise. Distinguish **unjustified** from **false**: criticizing evidence can show that a conclusion has not been supported without proving its opposite. A counterexample must be a case that conflicts with the asserted general relationship.\n+\n+Sources: [spreadsheet instructions](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/workouts/Method Flaw Argument Part Answer Choice Translation Spreadsheet • LO.md>), [rationale and example](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/learn/Method Classic Flaw Argument Part Answer Choice Translation Spreadsheet • LO.md>), [Method strategy](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/learn/Method Question Strategy • LO.md>).\n+\n+## Practice conditional meaning without replacing the current standard\n+\n+Paraphrase the complete statement into an ordinary if-then relationship before using its indicator to position labels. Read a proposed diagram back against the full sentence and look for a possible counterexample. The [current conditional reference](<../Curriculum/Conditional Logic/Notes/_megadoc_reference_conditionals.md>) remains the topic authority.\n+\n+Cassidy's Unless routine rotates three of four forms: `~A, unless B`; `Unless B, A`; `Unless ~B, ~A`; `A, unless ~B`. Vary the letters and derive each relationship fresh. The captured answer-key heading contains no answers; this library does not invent a recovered key. Her proposed target is three diagrams in 15 seconds, then weekly maintenance, rather than a universal standard for every learner.\n+\n+For conditional principle matching, determine the required conclusion, read the entire proposed principle, and test whether the stated premises establish its trigger. Keep the principle's full meaning, including contrapositively equivalent forms. Do not blindly reject an answer because a conclusion word is written on the left. A supplied fact plus an equivalent contrapositive can establish the required result. Cassidy's shortcut is an attributed heuristic, not a logical theorem.\n+\n+Sources: [concept-first diagramming](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/learn/The Worst Conditional Habit Everyone Has • LO.md>), [Unless workout](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/workouts/Unless Practice Routine • LO.md>), [necessary-condition trick](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/learn/The Necessary Condition Trick • LO.md>).\n+\n+## Keep assumption and weakening tasks precise\n+\n+A necessary assumption is something the argument's claimed support requires. Negation tests the support, not whether the conclusion could independently be true. Some captured notes incorrectly equate conclusion truth with assumption truth; those formulations are excluded from the usable synthesis. The UFO/bones illustration in the source is available for comparing proposed bridges, not an approved answer-key exercise. Some alleged assumptions are broader than the sketch alone establishes. Use the current topic material and test each candidate independently.\n+\n+For Weaken, put the actual conclusion into the task: does this whole choice damage the support for this specific claim? Delay the verdict until the full choice has been read and translated. Avoid rejecting a choice merely because it contains a familiar trap word or is not a surface topic match. A flaw prephrase helps but does not excuse ignoring a different way of weakening. The source's claim that only one answer can weaken at all is not adopted; question-relative strength and what the actual choices establish still matter.\n+\n+For objection practice, hold the premises fixed and identify a possible missing connection rather than just declaring the conclusion false. The recovered kitten examples distinguish stress from hiding, mischief history from this specific act, and a two-cat sample from all felines. Their response-versus-understanding distinction prevents importing a stronger conclusion than the text asserts. Use the existing [Assumption versus Objection homework](<../Curriculum/Flaw and Famous Flaws/Homework/Pop Quiz - Assumption vs Objection.md>); the [recovered example edition](worked-examples.generated.md) preserves both the incomplete chapter and its five-question derivative without replacing that homework.\n+\n+Sources: [NA strategy](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/learn/Necessary Assumption Question Strategy • LO.md>), [NA definition examples](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/learn/What Is a Necessary Assumption • LO.md>), [Weaken strategy](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/learn/Weaken Question Strategy • LO.md>).\n+\n+## Review without knowing which questions were wrong\n+\n+**Camo:** preserve the initial answers without revealing the score or key. Reattempt a list containing misses and an unknown number of correct decoys. Restart each question completely: stimulus/passage, question task and every choice. Do not only compare the final two or preserve an initial answer out of loyalty. Then compare attempts and journal while the evidence is fresh.\n+\n+| First attempt | Review | Source label | What to investigate |\n+|---|---|---|---|\n+| Wrong | Wrong | Conceptual gap | What remains misunderstood, including a repeated process error? |\n+| Wrong | Right | Misread | What changed in the reading or reasoning? |\n+| Right | Wrong | Self-doubt | Why was a defensible answer abandoned? Was the first answer genuinely understood? |\n+| Right | Right | Self-confidence | Can the learner explain the evidence, rather than repeating a lucky guess? |\n+\n+These outcomes are clues, not proof of a single cause. Normally Cassidy waits at least 24 hours and puts Camo after new practice. The Failsafe explicitly uses immediate Camo after a five-minute break. Do not call this contradiction; it is a stated exception. Nor should a learner change an answer merely because the mixture must include misses: each change requires question-specific evidence.\n+\n+**Wrong Answer Journal:** record date, question and chosen answer. In one or two sentences, name what the learner did that led to the wrong acceptance and right rejection, then a directly responsive action beginning “I will.” “I misread” is insufficient without identifying the dropped or changed meaning. Establish why choices are right/wrong first; journal the transferable repair rather than another long answer explanation. If a backlog prevents current journaling, Cassidy suggests starting fresh with recent sections rather than postponing all review.\n+\n+**Island:** set aside questions that remain unclear after review. Revisit for five minutes per day. Move a question off only when its explanation genuinely makes sense, not merely because its answer letter is familiar. The Island is a queue for unresolved understanding, not a second answer key.\n+\n+Sources: [Camo lesson](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/accuracy/How to Camo • LO.md>), [Camo workout](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/workouts/Camo Review • LO.md>), [journal](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/workouts/Wrong Answer Journal • LO.md>), [Island](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/concept-review/How and Why to Use the Island • LO.md>).\n+\n+## Use a two-success accuracy progression\n+\n+The captured **Accuracy Failsafe** sets a permitted-misses goal for a new section at natural pace. After a five-minute break, do Camo. If the goal is met, attempt a second new section of the same type and repeat the break/review. Two consecutive successes tighten the permitted-misses goal the following day. A first-section failure ends that day's Failsafe: journal and choose a recovery exercise instead of another same-type section. A second-section failure means journal both and keep tomorrow's goal unchanged.\n+\n+Select recovery from the observed problem. The source names Answer Choice Primer or MemAC for answer-choice translation, and Highlight Reel for strategic mistakes. Their complete instructions were not captured here, so those names are pointers, not newly reconstructed drills. The source advises recovery without more timed question answering. A proposed target progression must be tailored to the actual student rather than automatically assigned.\n+\n+Source: [Accuracy Failsafe](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/workouts/Accuracy Failsafe • LO.md>).\n+\n+## Preserve the source's routine targets without making them requirements\n+\n+The Foundation routine orders consecutive translation/CLIR sections, extracurricular reading and vocabulary; it proposes at least three sections per day when possible, ideally six, plus a weekly eight-section marathon. Its described block is two to four hours. Supplemental suggestions are ScienceDaily for science, Aeon for general RC-like prose, Arts & Letters Daily for humanities, the NYT Guide to Essential Knowledge for background gaps, and selected Crash Course topics. These are historical source suggestions, not newly researched product recommendations.\n+\n+The source advances to Translation + Decaf at the midpoint between initial and target translation time. Its example is 52 minutes initially, 20-minute target, midpoint 36. Targets are 20 minutes normally, 25 for 53-minute accommodations and 30 for 70-minute accommodations in the routine. The separate drill notes use 30 accommodated with 25 as a stretch target. Preserve that distinction rather than silently reconciling it. A qualifying time requires an aloud translation and reaction to every stimulus and a legitimate CLIR for each; the source suggests approximately five CLIR misses or fewer, adjustable for goals.\n+\n+The Marathon uses six to eight fresh sections, records their order and individual count-up times, defers checking until after the batch, and permits medically or biologically required breaks. The captured 400-minute platform-timer setting is an old UI workaround, not verified current behavior. Claims that a single daily section cannot improve skill or that any other break guarantees failure are not accepted as established learning science. Fatigue is observable evidence to investigate, not an automatic instruction to push harder.\n+\n+Sources: [Foundation](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/routines/Translation Foundation Routine • LO.md>), [Marathon](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/workouts/Translation Marathon • LO.md>).\n+\n+## Capture CLIRs and preserve blind review\n+\n+Dictate the question number and CLIR into an existing document when producing the CLIR, then format the numbered entries without altering the student's words. For a full-session transcript, marking each CLIR aloud makes extraction easier. Check against the key after the drill, without inventing missing speech. The source's Whisper Flow/Superwhisper/Docs/Loom claims about pricing, privacy and capabilities are historical; use verified local settings before choosing tools.\n+\n+Keep initial answers and timing data separate from revealed results when blind review is the purpose. The captured LawHub workaround uses self-paced tabs, avoids completion/show-answer controls and score-revealing hovers, and uses an external timer for custom durations. These exact buttons and persistence claims need a current interface check before operational use. This is not a blanket ban on exam-mode practice.\n+\n+Sources: [transcription method](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/translation/How to Use AI to Transcribe Your Cli Rs in Translation Plus Cli Rs • LO.md>), [LawHub lesson](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/learn/How to Use Law Hub in a Way That Doesnt Hurt Your Prep • LO.md>).\n+\n+## Check a teaching reference before publishing it\n+\n+Recovered conditional-plan review adds a useful artifact checklist: distinguish a quick reference from a complete lesson; make lookup labels contain the words a learner searches for; check mobile-width examples; avoid hiding every essential rule in collapsed panels; keep practice separate where it duplicates teaching; verify every diagram and its complete scope; distinguish contraposition from a second independent direction; preserve permission versus actual action; avoid inventing terms such as “good” weather; check causal tendencies before rendering them as guarantees; and distinguish beginner training requirements from test-day advice.\n+\n+This checklist does not approve the historical artifact, old app path, left/right-only pedagogy or reviewer examples. The review's claim that a class explicitly taught “the only goes left” was rejected in its own triage; do not repeat the fabricated attribution. Current approved conditional sequence and voice standard control any implementation.\n+\n+Sources: [historical plan](../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/PLAN.md), [review and triage](../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/PLAN-REVIEW-LOG.md).\n+\n+## Retrieve the actual explanations and examples\n+\n+- [Reading and translation lectures](reading-lectures.generated.md): full captured instructional transcript and notes, with claim boundaries.\n+- [Question-strategy lectures](question-strategies.generated.md): Method, conditionals, assumption and Weaken material with explicit doctrinal corrections.\n+- [Workouts and routines](review-workouts.generated.md): captured ordered protocols, prerequisites, source targets and review advice.\n+- [Worked examples](worked-examples.generated.md): freeway sampling, peat analogy, antilock-brake resolution, conditional forecast and kitten objection examples.\n+- [Every teaching-file coverage record](../../research/obsidian-vault-comparison/2026-10-07/teaching-active-coverage.md): active destination and exclusions for each original teaching file.\n*** End Patch"));


## Tool: custom_tool_call_output

Preserve the source-specific Objection label; use the current homework as the teaching authority.'\n+  if name.startswith('Objection Mini'):warning='This is a five-item derivative of the chapter capture. It is retained as an alternate practice presentation, not a second authoritative answer key.'\n+  # Missing wikilinks are explicit unavailable evidence, not dangling active links.\n+  content=re.sub(r'\\[\\[([^\\]]+)\\]\\]',r'`\\1` (unavailable source attachment)',content)\n+  lines+=['',f'## {name}','',f'[Captured source](<{source}>) · SHA-256 `{hashlib.sha256(p.read_bytes()).hexdigest()}`','',f'**Use boundary:** {warning}','',content]\n+  records[p.relative_to(ARC).as_posix()]={'destination':f'corpus/loophole-online/{dest.name}#{anchor(name)}','boundary':warning,'chars':len(content)}\n+ dest.write_text('\\n'.join(lines)+'\\n',encoding='utf-8')\n+\n+review=(ROOT/'research/obsidian-vault-comparison/2026-10-07/teaching-review.md').read_text(encoding='utf-8-sig')\n+rows=[]\n+for line in review.splitlines():\n+ if not line.startswith('| ['):continue\n+ m=re.search(r'\\]\\(<?([^)>]+)>?\\)\\s*\\|\\s*([^|]+)\\|\\s*(.*)\\|$',line)\n+ if not m:continue\n+ rel=m.group(1).split('old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/')[-1]\n+ rec=records.get(rel)\n+ if rec:out=f'[{rec[\"destination\"].split(\"#\")[0].split(\"/\")[-1]}](../../../{rec[\"destination\"]})';reason=rec['boundary']+' Noninstructional navigation/player/thumbnail/promotion material omitted.'\n+ elif '7Sage' in rel:out='[Tutoring diagnosis](../../../corpus/tutoring-diagnosis.md)';reason='Diagnosis retained; logic-games-era and universal timing claims are not current teaching rules.'\n+ else:out='Not made active';reason=m.group(3).strip()\n+ rows.append((rel,out,reason))\n+coverage=ROOT/'research/obsidian-vault-comparison/2026-10-07/teaching-active-coverage.md'\n+if coverage.exists():raise RuntimeError('Refusing to overwrite coverage')\n+lines=['# Every recovered teaching file has an explicit disposition','', 'Reviewed against the retained local archive on 2026-10-08. This follow-up distinguishes active teacher reference from raw preservation. The [method library](../../../corpus/loophole-online/method-library.md) compiles actionable steps; the four annotated editions preserve substantive captured lecture and note text for retrieval without treating vendor claims as approved doctrine.','',f'Annotated editions cover {len(records)} source files. This original-file matrix has {len(rows)} rows, including removed duplicate/empty captures. It does not claim the unprovided videos/images were recovered.','', '| Original file | Active destination | Exclusions or boundaries |','|---|---|---|']\n+for rel,out,reason in rows:lines.append(f'| `{rel}` | {out} | {reason.replace(\"|\",\"/\")} |')\n+lines+=['','## Additional historical planning sources','','`PLAN.md` and `PLAN-REVIEW-LOG.md` contribute only the artifact-check checklist in [the method library](../../../corpus/loophole-online/method-library.md#check-a-teaching-reference-before-publishing-it). A source edition is not a newly published lecture or assigned student homework.']\n+coverage.write_text('\\n'.join(lines)+'\\n',encoding='utf-8')\n+print(json.dumps({'edition_sources':len(records),'matrix_rows':len(rows),'outputs':[str(OUT/(g+'.generated.md')) for g in groups]+[str(coverage)]},indent=2))\n*** End Patch"));

Blind-review purpose does not forbid all exam-mode practice.\\r\\n\\r\\n#### How to Use LawHub in a Way that Doesn\'t Hurt Your Prep\\r\\n\\r\\n- 0:00how to use LawHub in a way that doesn\'t hurt your prep. Um, and, you know, LawHub has a lot of positives in that, you know, this is going to be the actual test-taking platform that you use on the real day of the exam, so of course you want to be really familiar with the interface.\\r\\n- 0:13However, it has a lot of negatives. Especially if you like to Camo. If you like to Camo, you basically have to fight LawHub in order to have the privilege of doing so.\\r\\n- 0:24Because LawHub wants to reveal to you your score and the correct answer seemingly at every turn. And so I\'m going to outline to you how you can use LawHub in a way that does not hurt your prep here forthwith.\\r\\n- 0:37First thing is, never press complete section. When you finish up a section, you can press complete section and that will then take you to a screen that would show you all of the correct answers\\r\\n- 0:48and all of the answers you chose. Of course, if you want to Camo or if you are doing something else and you want to blind you\'d ever want to do that because you want to Camo. That if you ever want to be able to look at the question without knowing what the right answer is, you\\r\\n- 1:06don\'t ever want to press complete section. And in addition, you only ever want to use self-paste mode, not exam\\r\\n- 1:12mode, even when you are actually taking PTs. Because if you use exam mode, it is going to automatically take you to the next section\\r\\n- 1:21And then at the end of the sections of the PT, it is going to automatically show your score along with all of the correct answers and all of that different kind of stuff.\\r\\n- 1:30Again, ruining your ability to Camo or productively review the questions that you struggled with without having the right answer already spoiled for you.\\r\\n- 1:39And it gets even worse in that when you then go to the screen that has the question on it, you know, it literally circles the correct answer.\\r\\n- 1:47So you can\'t even look at the question without having the right answer like really pointed out to you. And so just review these first two things. Only use self-paste mode. Don\'t use exam mode and never press complete section.\\r\\n- 2:00If you follow these two vague directives, I guess they\'re not vague. These two very specific directives, you at least will be able to Camo kind of at the onset.\\r\\n- 2:10Now, what else? So the other way that they reveal to you your score and impact your ability to Camo is on the selection menu screen where you\'re like picking which PT in which section to do.\\r\\n- 2:22If you like mouse over the section, it then shows you your score. It\'s weird. It feels like it\'s booby trapped, right?\\r\\n- 2:31Like it feels like it\'s booby trapped to not allow you to Camo. And so one way you can avert this, because sometimes people have challenges in that they\'re like, oh, I wanted to go in and open the next section, but I moused over it and I saw the score and then I was upset and blah, blah, blah.\\r\\n- 2:45Is you can just open in different tabs, all of the sections that you plan to do that day or even that week\\r\\n- 2:52and just let them be open tabs. And then you don\'t have to go back to the menu screen and potentially accidentally mouse over something and have your score revealed to you.\\r\\n- 3:02So the other thing is, and this may seem obvious, but never press the show answer button. The show answer button shouldn\'t be there.\\r\\n- 3:10The show answer button is really evil. Don\'t use the show answer button because the the Camo process requires you to not know the answer.\\r\\n- 3:18And that feeling of not knowing the answer is actually the stuff of actually taking the exam for real on test day.\\r\\n- 3:23It is not bad to not know the answer yet. That\'s completely OK. And so to move on from the ways it sabotages you in Camo, let\'s talk about the ways it sabotages you with time.\\r\\n- 3:34So one key tip, you actually can hide the law hub timer. You just have to click where the timer is and it disappears.\\r\\n- 3:44Now, when you hit the five minute warning, the timer, you\'ll get a pop up that\'s like, you have five minutes left and that cannot be overcome.\\r\\n- 3:51That will always happen. And it\'s not a bad thing necessarily that it happens because on test day you\'ll have that pop up and you do want to get used to it.\\r\\n- 3:58But after the five minute warning happens, the time will no longer be hideable. And so that\'s just a thing to know that you are going to have to get used to that five minute countdown timer being visible to you.\\r\\n- 4:07But if you are in the stage of prep where you\'re doing untimed sections or stopwatch sections or let\'s say you\'re gradually bringing your time down, maybe you\'re fail saving and you\'re like, you know what I want to do? I don\'t know, 42 minute sections or whatever it is, something that law hub is\\r\\n- 4:23not as friendly with and which will, you know, maybe get into a bit of a mix with the timer. If you want to do custom timing, just set the law hub timer for something way above what you are going to actually be working with.\\r\\n- 4:37Set it at like 400 minutes a section or something like that. And if you\'re then doing a stopwatch section where you just want to start the stopwatch, cover it and see what your natural pace is, you\'ll be then enabled to do that\\r\\n- 4:49without the five minute timer showing up, without like the section automatically ending, all that kind of stuff. Not having to deal with the law hub interface timer business while you\'re doing stopwatch timing and more unconventional timing drills is a major help\\r\\n- 5:04just because it\'s not super flexible, the law hub timing interface. And so we don\'t want you to accidentally get yourself in a whole situation.\\r\\n- 5:13But I mean, most of these issues with law hub, they really do stem from law hub wanting to show you the answer all the time.\\r\\n- 5:21And so I hope that these hacks have helped you find ways to overcome that, because it really can be a great test taking platform if you know the trouble spots to step over.\\r\\n- 5:32So hopefully this helped and I will see you very soon.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Method Classic Flaw Argument Part Answer Choice Translation Spreadsheet\\r\\n\\r\\n[Captured source](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/learn/Method Classic Flaw Argument Part Answer Choice Translation Spreadsheet • LO.md>) · SHA-256 `f4cef45e7acb4a07b0d665858dcf05f9024d385d9eca4cc16b8773f1c0a604ba`\\r\\n\\r\\n**Use boundary:** This is an attributed source explanation. Verify logical inferences independently and use current Joshua curriculum for student-facing delivery; source anecdotes and mechanism claims are not independently established.\\r\\n\\r\\n#### Method/Classic Flaw/Argument Part Answer Choice Translation Spreadsheet\\r\\n\\r\\n- 0:00Hey, hey, hey. So, welcome to the Method, Flaw, Argument, Part, Answer, Choice, Translation spreadsheet. Not the best name for a drill I\'ve ever come up with.\\r\\n- 0:09Really, I miss mini-cycles now. But, one of the more useful spreadsheet-based drills though, and this one has been in circulation for five-ish years, it\'s resulted in a lot of improvement, so I\'m really excited to get you spun up on it, because it\'s something that actually pertains throughout\\r\\n- 0:26Your whole prep journey, essentially, until it makes itself irrelevant by being successful, and it will be relevant in the for as long as there are answer choices on method, flaw, and argument part questions that you don\'t fully understand, which can cause, you know, a lot of inaccuracy, as we\\r\\n- 0:41Talked about, and so this drill kind of goes on top of all your question-answering drills, and it\'s just a part of your review that you do after this.\\r\\n- 0:48And so, I\'m not going to read all the instructions to you, because obviously, like, you can read them, but I will add some flavor to them.\\r\\n- 0:55And so, I\'ll describe, you know, how, how you would actually perform this drill. See you soon.\r\n\r\n## What Is a Necessary Assumption\r\n\r\n[Captured source](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/learn/What Is a Necessary Assumption • LO.md>) · SHA-256 `3a026a6d7c44f2ba0bc2a8c493d60fbcb440d7535711d2e3d32d66ec18975808`\r\n\r\n**Use boundary:** The UFO illustration and proposed assumptions are source claims, not an approved answer key. Conclusion truth does not establish what this support requires.\r\n\r\n##### Ellen's Notes\r\n\r\n**Example**: Dr Shaah's research claims that UFOs are real.New evidence of alien bones has recently come to light and carbon testing shows the bones to be of unknown origin. No remains of earthly origin indicate an unknown origin in carbon testing.\r\n\r\n***Example Necessary Assumptions***\r\n\r\n1. The presence of alien bones on earth indicates the possibility of UFOs.\r\n2. The bones that were recently found qualify as remains.\r\n3. Remains of non-earthy origin were not transported to earth by natural cosmological processes.\r\n\r\n***Ways to Think About a Necessary Assumption (Pick your fav!)***\r\n\r\n1. If the conclusion is true, the necessary assumption has to be true.\r\n2. The author has to believe the necessary assumption.\r\n3. The author has to think the necessary assumption is true.\r\n4. If the conclusion is proven, the necessary assumption has to be true.\r\n5. If the premises worked to prove the conclusion, the necessary assumption is required.\r\n6. If these premises proved this conclusion, the author must assume the necessary assumption.\r\n7. The argument relies on the necessary assumption.\r\n8. For the argument to make any sense, the necessary assumption has to be its foundation.\r\n\r\n"}]

Single-section ineffectiveness claims are not verified learning science. Drill notes elsewhere use a different accommodated-target formulation.\r\n\r\n#### Quick Facts\r\n\r\n##### Will This Help Me?\r\n\r\nThis will help you if\r\n\r\nYou\'re just starting out in your Translation skill building.\r\n\r\nSkip for now if\r\n\r\nDon\'t skip this Routine.\r\n\r\nchallenge indexPretty Chill\r\n\r\nlevelBeginner\r\n\r\nfrequencyDaily Foundation\r\n\r\nstudy block length2-4 hours\r\n\r\n#### Schedule (4 workouts)\r\n\r\nEnsure you complete the workouts **in order** to benefit the most from this routine.\r\n\r\n1\r\n\r\n##### Translation + CLIR Drill\r\n\r\n≥ 3 sections\r\n\r\n2\r\n\r\n##### Extracurricular Reading\r\n\r\n###### Instructions\r\n\r\n1. Every day, translate elevated reading material from outside the LSAT, using the following sources:\r\n\t1. **[ScienceDaily.com](https://www.sciencedaily.com/)**\r\n\t\t1. Ideal daily reading for those who feel alienated by scientific language or fear science passages in RC\r\n\t\t\t\t2. Be sure to choose the articles that challenge you the most, not articles that look familiar or interesting\r\n\t\t2. **[Aeon.co](https://aeon.co/)**\r\n\t\t1. The most similar articles to LSAT RC on the internet\r\n\t\t\t\t2. Reading an Aeon.co article every day is a great habit if you have no specific topic-based weaknesses, but would like to make RC-style reading a habit.\r\n\t\t3. **[ALDaily.com](https://www.aldaily.com/)**\r\n\t\t1. Ideal daily reading for those who feel alienated by abstract language, art, humanities, and pretension.\r\n\t\t\t\t2. Be sure to choose the articles that look the most unfamiliar, not articles that speak to your pre-existing interests.\r\n\t\t4. **[NYT Guide to Essential Knowledge](https://www.amazon.com/York-Times-Guide-Essential-Knowledge-ebook/dp/B005EXS7E4/ref=tmm_kin_swatch_0)**\r\n\t\t1. The single best way to fix gaps in common knowledge\r\n\t\t\t\t2. Here is a list of sections in the Guide that are relevant to LSAT. If any of these topics would trouble you as an RC passage, read over that section of the guide.\r\n\t\t\t\t3. **LSAT Relevant Guide Sections**: Architecture, Zoology, Botany, History, Art, Photography, Literature, Music (the serious parts about jazz and blues, especially), Film, Dance, Drama, Climate & Weather and Glossary of Weather Words (In Geography), Government, Law, Philosophy, Biology, Chemistry, Physics, Astronomy, Geology\r\n\t\t5. **[Crash Course](https://www.youtube.com/@crashcourse/playlists)**\r\n\t\t1. Not a written source, but Crash Course is too useful for solving common knowledge gaps to not include\r\n\t\t\t\t2. Watch Crash Course while you\'re in line, waiting for someone, on hold, brushing your teeth, etc. Any time you could be scrolling on your phone, watch Crash Course instead.\r\n\t\t\t\t3. **Top Five LSAT Relevant Crash Course Sections**: Intellectual Property (Ellen\'s favorite for LSAT students), Economics, US Government & Politics, Art History, and Philosophy\r\n\r\n3\r\n\r\n##### Vocab Training\r\n\r\n##### Once A Week, Do This Instead\r\n\r\n1\r\n\r\n##### Translation Marathon\r\n\r\n8 sections\r\n\r\n≥ 1x per week\r\n\r\n#### Ellen\'s Advice\r\n\r\n##### Why Are We Doing This?\r\n\r\nThe Translation + CLIR Drills in this Routine are the foundation of your entire LSAT prep journey. This is the most important homework you will do during your LSAT prep training, even compared to PTs.\r\n\r\nThis Routine builds a foundation in LR Translation before we port your Translation skills over to RC in the next Routine.\r\n\r\n##### How This Should Be Done\r\n\r\nYour progression toward the Translation time and accuracy goals is primarily determined by two things: **volume** and **following the instructions.**\r\n\r\nTo maximize your progress in this Routine, do all your Translation + CLIR Drills consecutively; do not take breaks between Translation + CLIR Drills. Do not review your Translation + CLIR Drills in between sections. All review should be done in a separate sitting.\r\n\r\n**Do at least 3 consecutive Translation + CLIR Drills per day, if possible.** Ideally, you would complete 6 Translation + CLIR drills consecutively per day. Doing 1 Translation + CLIR Drill per day will not improve your skills; it will keep your skills the same. You will only start improving on your second consecutive Translation + CLIR Drill; this is why it is so useful to do *at least* 3 consecutive Translation + CLIR Drills every day.\r\n\r\nAdd a Translation Marathon to your Daily Practice at least once per week. If you can manage to do Translation + CLIR Marathons more often, you will progress to the time goal faster.\r\n\r\nExtracurricular Reading and Vocab should always be done at the end of your Routine to gradually expand your common sense in the subject areas where you\'re weakest. If you struggle with science passages, emphasize daily science engagement in the Extracurricular Reading. If you struggle with abstract language, emphasize daily humanities engagement in the Extracurricular Reading.\r\n\r\n##### How to Move On\r\n\r\n**Once you are halfway to your Translation + CLIR Drill time goal, move to the next Routine: Translation + Decaf.**\r\n\r\n**The time goal for Translation + CLIR Drills is 20 minutes.** The accommodated time goals for Translation + CLIR Drills are 25 minutes for 53-minute accommodations and 30 minutes for 70-minute accommodations. Meeting this Translation + CLIR Drill time goal is only valid under two conditions: you have a CLIR for every stimulus and you have translated and quipped every stimulus in the section out loud. Ideally, you should hit -5 accuracy against the CLIR Answer Key consistently in order to beat the time goal, but this accuracy goal may be adjusted according to your score goals.\r\n\r\n**Example of When to Move On**: You started at 52 minutes per out loud Translation + CLIR Drill section and you don\'t have accommodations, so your time goal is 20 minutes. The midpoint of 52 and 20 is 36, so you would move on when your time hits 36 minutes.\r\n\r\n## How to Camo\r\n\r\n[Captured source](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/accuracy/How to Camo • LO.md>) · SHA-256 `126b76a944f1e3e957f3c25709914ee921ac3f2b9d76cfaee0777b3ebf43087b`\r\n\r\n**Use boundary:** Outcome labels are diagnostic clues.
