I hope that tells you how to Camo and I can\'t wait to see how you do\r\n\r\n###### In This Training Block\r\n\r\n##### Ellen\'s Notes\r\n\r\n***Camo Tips***\r\n\r\nNever check the answer key on any section before Camoing\r\n\r\nWait 24 hours before Camoing\r\n\r\nNever press Complete Section in LawHub\r\n\r\nDo your Wrong Answer Journal when you Camo\r\n\r\n## How and Why to Do Decaf RC Translation\r\n\r\n[Captured source](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/concept-review/How and Why to Do Decaf RC Translation • LO.md>) · SHA-256 `6abec7c63ea70c65cfdecbde9922d21b49a953a1ea9c78cfb7c92828b5dbcab7`\r\n\r\n**Use boundary:** This is an attributed source explanation. Verify logical inferences independently and use current Joshua curriculum for student-facing delivery; source anecdotes and mechanism claims are not independently established.\r\n\r\n##### Ellen\'s Notes\r\n\r\n***Decaf RC Translation Drill***\r\n\r\n**NOTE**: If you want to do your recaps out loud in the beginning to keep yourself honest about the quality and completeness of the recaps, that\'s totally fine. Transition to silent recaps when you feel like you\'re ready.\r\n\r\n1. Read, translate, and quip paragraph 1. Do it just like LR.\r\n\t1. This means all the details are included just like they would be on LR.\r\n\t\t2. Quip after *every* Translation.\r\n\t\t3. This standard applies to every paragraph you ever translate on RC.\r\n2. Look away from paragraph 1 and recap the **FULL TRANSLATION** of paragraph 1 silently.\r\n\t1. The recap is not a summary, gist, or main point.\r\n\t\t2. The recap should not omit names, dates, and lists.\r\n\t\t3. The recap should not be a shopping list of information. It should be a story in your own words.\r\n\t\t4. It is encouraged to mix up the order of the sentences and ideas.\r\n\t\t5. This standard applies to every paragraph (or collection of paragraphs) you ever recap on RC.\r\n3. Read, translate, and quip paragraph 2. Do it just like LR.\r\n4. Look away from paragraph 2 and recap the **FULL TRANSLATION** of paragraph 2 silently.\r\n5. Without looking back, recap the Translations of paragraphs 1 + 2 back to back silently.\r\n6. Read, translate, and quip paragraph 3. Do it just like LR.\r\n7. Look away from paragraph 3 and recap the **FULL TRANSLATION** of paragraph 3 silently.\r\n8. Without looking back, recap the Translations of paragraphs 1 + 2 + 3 back to back silently.\r\n9. Repeat this pattern for all remaining paragraphs.\r\n\r\n## How and Why to Use the Island\r\n\r\n[Captured source](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/concept-review/How and Why to Use the Island • LO.md>) · SHA-256 `06ce12ef8cd1c1f9dfa8f013ffbcf96db30428d81114e9d6a53d8786bdbcee13`\r\n\r\n**Use boundary:** This is an attributed source explanation. Verify logical inferences independently and use current Joshua curriculum for student-facing delivery; source anecdotes and mechanism claims are not independently established.\r\n\r\n##### Ellen\'s Notes\r\n\r\n***The Island***\r\n\r\n**NOTE**: The Island is a spreadsheet you make yourself with one column called Island and another column called Canoed Away.\r\n\r\n1. If a question is still mystifying after Camo, just add the citation of the question to the Island\r\n\t1. This means at least one of these three things is true:\r\n\t\t1. I don\'t understand why the right answer is right.\r\n\t\t\t\t2. I don\'t understand why the wrong answer is wrong.\r\n\t\t\t\t3. I have no idea how I could get this right in the future.\r\n2. At the start of prep each day, spend 5 minutes on the Island retrying a question or two.\r\n3. If the question clicks, move it to Canoed Away. Otherwise, keep it on the Island.\r\n\r\n## Accuracy Failsafe\r\n\r\n[Captured source](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/workouts/Accuracy Failsafe • LO.md>) · SHA-256 `fbf73e13421835cd21de79ebff00e15b681a33d19b3b24639af6cfbaf29ef7be`\r\n\r\n**Use boundary:** Immediate Camo after a five-minute break is an explicit exception to general delayed Camo. Related recovery drill names are pointers; full instructions were not captured.\r\n\r\n#### Quick Facts\r\n\r\n##### Will This Help Me?\r\n\r\nThis will help you if\r\n\r\nYou need a systematic way to improve your accuracy that will automatically assign you workouts to improve your habits when needed.\r\n\r\nSkip for now if\r\n\r\nYou\'re nowhere near your accuracy goals.\r\n\r\nchallenge indexBuckle Up\r\n\r\nlevelIntermediate\r\n\r\nminimum time90+ minutes\r\n\r\ntiming compatible with35m, 53m, 70m, Marathon, Stopwatch, Natural Pace\r\n\r\n#### Instructions\r\n\r\n**NOTE**: You can do an Accuracy Failsafe with any workout. Failsafes are time-training protocols that you can apply to any workout.\r\n\r\n1. Set your accuracy goal as a target number of wrong answers per section.\r\n\t1. An accuracy goal could be -5, for example.\r\n2. Complete an LR or RC section\\* at your natural pace.\r\n3. Take a five-minute break and Camo the section you just completed.\r\n\t1. If you have met your accuracy goal, proceed to a second section of the same type.\r\n\t\t1. This means if you just completed an RC section and hit your accuracy goal of -5, you would proceed to do another RC section with an accuracy goal of -5.\r\n\t\t2. If you have not met your accuracy goal on the first section, you have not beaten today\'s Failsafe.\r\n\t\t1. Do not decrease your target number of wrong answers tomorrow. Keep the same accuracy goal.\r\n\t\t\t\t2. Do not proceed to a second section of the same type today.\r\n\t\t\t\t3. Instead, Wrong Answer Journal and do one Recovery Workout (defined below) now.\r\n4. If you have proceeded to a second section, take a five-minute break and Camo it.\r\n5. If you have met your accuracy goal for the second time in a row, you have beaten today\'s Failsafe!\r\n\t1. Wrong Answer Journal both of today\'s sections.\r\n\t\t2. Decrease your target number of wrong answers tomorrow.\r\n\t\t1. This means that if you beat today\'s Failsafe of -5 accuracy, try for -4 accuracy on tomorrow\'s Failsafe.\r\n6. If you do not meet the accuracy goal on the second section, you have not beaten today\'s Failsafe.\r\n\t1. Wrong Answer Journal both of today\'s sections.\r\n\t\t2. Do not decrease your target number of wrong answers tomorrow. Keep the same accuracy goal.\r\n7. Return to Step 1 the following day.\r\n\r\nSections to use\r\n\r\nNew Sections Only\r\n\r\nhow to time\r\n\r\nCount-Up Stopwatch\r\n\r\nCountdown Timer\r\n\r\n#### Ellen\'s Advice\r\n\r\n##### If You Want to Win\r\n\r\n- Failsafes are a great Workout for the student who loves data and structured goals.\r\n- Don\'t be intimidated by all the steps you see in the Failsafe.\r\n\t- Once you get into it, the flow of the Workout is relatively intuitive.\r\n\t\t- Essentially, if you meet your goals twice in a row, you move your goal. If you don\'t meet your goals twice in a row, you don\'t move your goal.\r\n- Which Recovery Workout should you choose?\r\n\t- It depends on where you tend to feel difficulty throughout the section.\r\n\t\t- If you notice you miss questions from not translating the answer choices, going toward an Answer Choice Primer or MemAC could be a great move.\r\n\t\t- If you notice that you are making strategic mistakes, making a Highlight Reel is a safe bet.\r\n\t\t- You can be the judge of which Recovery Workout is best for you, but the Recovery Workouts are designed to not allow you to simply repeat the exact same mistakes that you made in the first section of the FailSafe.\r\n\t\t- Even if you\'re tempted to go outside the list of Recovery Workout, don\'t do one that involves timed question answering.\r\n\r\n#### Real Examples\r\n\r\n***Recovery Workouts***\r\n\r\n## Camo Review\r\n\r\n[Captured source](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/workouts/Camo Review • LO.md>) · SHA-256 `fba42f6d9ca9d2fe7fce08c4be41ad96c7eb907f781eee2ad9622d7d6e612829`\r\n\r\n**Use boundary:** Outcome labels are diagnostic clues. Never change an answer solely to game the mixture of misses/decoys. Platform UI claims are dated.\r\n\r\n#### Quick Facts\r\n\r\n##### Will This Help Me?\r\n\r\nThis will help you if\r\n\r\nYou are answering questions in any section and need to learn from your mistakes. (This is all of you 🕵🏻)\r\n\r\nSkip for now if\r\n\r\nYou have not beaten the Translation + CLIR time and accuracy goals.\r\n\r\nchallenge indexPretty Chill\r\n\r\nlevelBeginner\r\n\r\nminimum time15+ minutes\r\n\r\ntiming compatible withNot Timed\r\n\r\n#### Instructions\r\n\r\n1. When you finish an LR or RC section in LawHub, do not press Complete Section. Instead, write down your answers.\r\n\t1. Use Self-Paced Mode on all LR and RC sections. Never use Exam Mode.\r\n2. At least 24 hours later, enter your answers into Camo Review.\r\n\t1. **Do not check your answers against the answer key before entering your answers into Camo Review.**\r\n3. Camo Review will give you a list of questions to return to.\r\n\t1. This list includes all of your wrong answers and an unknown number of decoy correct answers.\r\n4. Reattempt all of the questions in your Camo Review from scratch. Attempt to do a better job on the question now than you did the first time.\r\n\t1. Doing a better job means you should retranslate, requip, and re-CLIR the stimulus on LR or retranslate and requip the passage on RC. Retranslate every part of the question, consider every answer choice, and try to correct your process mistakes before choosing a new answer.\r\n5. Enter all your new answers into Camo Review.\r\n6. Camo Review will identify all your Misreads, Conceptual Gaps, Self-Doubt, and Self-Confidence answers.\r\n7. Your Misreads, Conceptual Gaps, and Self-Doubt answers have been automatically added to your Wrong Answer Journal. Go to your Wrong Answer Journal now to immediately begin the Wrong Answer Journal process.\r\n\r\nvideo walkthrough\r\n\r\n[How to Camo](https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/accuracy/video/how-to-camo)\r\n\r\nSections to use\r\n\r\nPreviously Seen Sections Only\r\n\r\nhow to time\r\n\r\nUntimed\r\n\r\n#### Ellen\'s Advice\r\n\r\n##### If You Want to Win\r\n\r\n- Never Camo before doing your sections for the day. Always Camo as the last LSAT thing you do for the day.\r\n\t- If you Camo immediately before completing another section, you will be overly reactive to the result.\r\n\t\t- If the previous day\'s section went well, this can make you lazy.\r\n\t\t- If it went poorly, this can make you discouraged.\r\n\t\t- It\'s not worth taking the risk of either of these reactions affecting your performance on the next section.\r\n- The only point of Camo Review is to get a -0.\r\n\t- Do not phone in the Camo Review or rechoose the answer you originally chose out of loyalty because you aren\'t sure it\'s wrong.\r\n\t\t- If you got more than two questions back in the Camo Review, some of the questions are definitely wrong, so you must change at least some of them.\r\n- Do not Camo a section immediately after completing it, unless you are directed explicitly to do so as part of another Workout or Routine.\r\n\t- If you Camo a section immediately after completing it, the Camo score will be worse and you will lose a valuable opportunity to improve.\r\n\r\n## Decaf RC Translation Drill\r\n\r\n[Captured source](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/workouts/Decaf RC Translation Drill • LO.md>) · SHA-256 `0284b985986bae5ab5cfb7b7b06c1103d54d9e6c1ddf85d221d911bce10a4e24`\r\n\r\n**Use boundary:** Prerequisite and 12/8-minute goals are source prescriptions, not universal requirements or test-day claims for every learner.\r\n\r\n#### Quick Facts\r\n\r\n##### Will This Help Me?\r\n\r\nThis will help you if\r\n\r\nYou need to translate an RC passage. (Which is all of you? 😂)\r\n\r\nSkip for now if\r\n\r\nYou cannot RTQ a stimulus reliably with good process.\r\n\r\nchallenge indexBuckle Up\r\n\r\nlevelBeginner\r\n\r\nminimum section time32+ minutes\r\n\r\ntiming compatible withStopwatch\r\n\r\n#### Instructions\r\n\r\n**NOTE**: Do your recaps out loud in the beginning to keep yourself honest about the quality and completeness of the recaps. Transition to silent recaps when you feel like you\'re ready.\r\n\r\n1. Read, translate, and quip paragraph 1\\*. Do it just like LR.\r\n\t1. This means all the details are included just like they would be on LR.\r\n\t\t2. Quip after *every* Translation.\r\n\t\t3. This standard applies to every paragraph you ever translate on RC.\r\n2. Look away from paragraph 1 and recap the **FULL TRANSLATION** of paragraph 1 silently.\r\n\t1. The recap is not a summary, gist, or main point.\r\n\t\t2. The recap should not omit names, dates, and lists.\r\n\t\t3. The recap should not be a shopping list of information. It should be a story in your own words.\r\n\t\t4. It is encouraged to mix up the order of the sentences and ideas.\r\n\t\t5. This standard applies to every paragraph (or collection of paragraphs) you ever recap on RC.\r\n3. Read, translate, and quip paragraph 2. Do it just like LR.\r\n4. Look away from paragraph 2 and recap the **FULL TRANSLATION** of paragraph 2 silently.\r\n5. Without looking back, recap the Translations of paragraphs 1 + 2 back to back silently.\r\n6. Read, translate, and quip paragraph 3. Do it just like LR.\r\n7. Look away from paragraph 3 and recap the **FULL TRANSLATION** of paragraph 3 silently.\r\n8. Without looking back, recap the Translations of paragraphs 1 + 2 + 3 back to back silently.\r\n9. Repeat this pattern for all remaining paragraphs.\r\n10. Complete this same process on all remaining passages in the section.\r\n\r\nvideo walkthrough\r\n\r\n[How and Why to Do Decaf RC Translation](https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/translation/video/how-and-why-to-do-decaf-rc-translation)\r\n\r\nSections to use\r\n\r\nMix in Previously Seen Sections\r\n\r\nhow to time\r\n\r\nCount-Up Stopwatch\r\n\r\nper passage time goal to move on\r\n\r\n12 min (outloud recaps), 8 min (silent recaps)\r\n\r\n#### Ellen\'s Advice\r\n\r\n##### If You Want to Win\r\n\r\n- If you have a 70-minute time accommodation, you can use Decaf RC Translation on test day. You do not have to master Cappuccino RC Translation and Caffeinated RC Translation for use on test day, if Decaf RC Translation offers superior Translation retention.\r\n\r\n## Method Flaw Argument Part Answer Choice Translation Spreadsheet\r\n\r\n[Captured source](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/workouts/Method Flaw Argument Part Answer Choice Translation Spreadsheet • LO.md>) · SHA-256 `562b0c3a81b0b50c10aa442b1e78e57c594c7061eead3b6e0570e877e692da86`\r\n\r\n**Use boundary:** This is an attributed source explanation. Verify logical inferences independently and use current Joshua curriculum for student-facing delivery; source anecdotes and mechanism claims are not independently established.\r\n\r\n#### Instructions\r\n\r\n1. Make a spreadsheet with three columns, or use [this template](https://docs.google.com/spreadsheets/d/1V7BS4BgrznJf_JQepzRK07dJw5yX0dZijLFSFYDqank/edit?usp=sharing):\r\n\t1. **Column 1**: Vexing Answer Choice\r\n\t\t2. **Column 2**: Answer Choice Translation\r\n\t\t3. **Column 3**: Corresponding Stimulus\r\n2. Collect all the difficult answer choices to translate, focusing especially on Method/Flaw/Argument Part answer choices.\r\n\t1. This could include harvesting answer choices from your sections immediately after you\'re done or going back over old sections to harvest answer choices.\r\n3. Write the difficult answers in the Vexing Answer Choice column of the spreadsheet, one answer choice per row.\r\n4. Translate the answers in the Answer Choice Translation column of the spreadsheet.\r\n5. In the Corresponding Stimulus column of the spreadsheet, write an example stimulus which would make the answer choice in that row correct.\r\n\r\n## Proto Stimulus Rebound\r\n\r\n[Captured source](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/workouts/Proto Stimulus Rebound • LO.md>) · SHA-256 `109f737e41d770e3a91b62915243510b3c4db6b926da41110a62591ddeb5ec68`\r\n\r\n**Use boundary:** This is an attributed source explanation. Verify logical inferences independently and use current Joshua curriculum for student-facing delivery; source anecdotes and mechanism claims are not independently established.\r\n\r\n#### Quick Facts\r\n\r\n##### Will This Help Me?\r\n\r\nThis will help you if\r\n\r\nYou forget a significant amount of the stimulus by the time you finish the CLIR.\r\n\r\nSkip for now if\r\n\r\nYou retain the all information in the stimulus after you\'ve finished the CLIR.\r\n\r\nchallenge indexBuckle Up\r\n\r\nlevelBeginner\r\n\r\nminimum time25+ minutes\r\n\r\ntiming compatible withStopwatch\r\n\r\n#### Instructions\r\n\r\n**NOTE**: Proto-Stimulus Rebounds may be done with or without answering the question.\r\n\r\n1. Translate, quip, and CLIR the stimulus\\*.\r\n2. Cover the stimulus and say the full Translation from memory\r\n\t1. Try to reorder the argument parts in your retelling of the Translation.\r\n3. Relook at the stimulus and see if anything is missing, added, or changed.\r\n\t1. Take a note of what was missing, added, or changed.\r\n4. Complete the rest of the question, if you choose to do so.\r\n\r\nSections to use\r\n\r\nMix in Previously Seen Sections\r\n\r\nhow to time\r\n\r\nCount-Up Stopwatch\r\n\r\n## Translation Marathon\r\n\r\n[Captured source](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/workouts/Translation Marathon • LO.md>) · SHA-256 `323f24075094302d735e8bfac3969b1eb7b205a89aff6f69f3a583d45ad57d87`\r\n\r\n**Use boundary:** Source workloads and no-break efficacy assertions are not mandatory curriculum. Medically/biologically required breaks are explicitly permitted; fatigue is not automatic evidence to push harder.\r\n\r\n#### Quick Facts\r\n\r\n##### Will This Help Me?\r\n\r\nThis will help you if\r\n\r\nYou need to bring your Translation time down to meet the Translation time goals.\r\n\r\nSkip for now if\r\n\r\nYou are past the Translation stage of prep.\r\n\r\nchallenge indexEllen Whyyy\r\n\r\nlevelBeginner\r\n\r\nminimum time120+ minutes\r\n\r\ntiming compatible withStopwatch\r\n\r\n#### Instructions\r\n\r\n1. Complete six to eight Translation + CLIR Drills\\* in one sitting with no breaks.\r\n\r\nSections to use\r\n\r\nNew Sections Only\r\n\r\nhow to time\r\n\r\nCount-Up Stopwatch\r\n\r\n#### Ellen\'s Advice\r\n\r\n##### If You Want to Win\r\n\r\n- **It is very important that you do not take breaks between sections or check your sections in the middle of the Marathon.**\r\n\t- Marathons only work because your brain doesn’t get the chance to re-adapt to non-LSAT thinking.\r\n\t\t- Taking breaks guarantees that the Marathon will not work.\r\n\t\t- Do not check your phone.\r\n\t\t\t\t- Do not have conversations.\r\n\t\t\t\t- Do not take a lunch break.\r\n\t\t- Put snacks and drinks on the desk to eat while you Marathon, if needed.\r\n\t\t- The only breaks allowed during Translation Marathons are medically required or biologically required breaks.\r\n\t\t- Running to the bathroom is allowed between sections.\r\n\t\t\t\t- Accommodated breaks for medically required tasks (like insulin injections, etc) are obviously allowed.\r\n- Open all the sections you plan to complete in separate tabs before starting the Translation Marathon.\r\n- Keep track of the order of the sections in your Translation Marathon, so you can analyze the results.\r\n- If you feel yourself getting worse as the Translation Marathon progresses, you are likely becoming fatigued.\r\n\t- This is not cause for alarm. Fatigue is part of the process; it indicates that you need to build stamina.\r\n- At the end of the Translation Marathon, take a break and then check all the sections against the CLIR Answer Key in a big batch.\r\n\r\n##### Timing Tips\r\n\r\n- Set a section timer for 400 minutes in LawHub\'s Self-Paced Mode. **Do not use Exam Mode.**\r\n- Time each Translation + CLIR Drill with an external stopwatch. Record the time each drill takes in your tracker.\r\n\r\n## Unless Practice Routine\r\n\r\n[Captured source](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/workouts/Unless Practice Routine • LO.md>) · SHA-256 `12901587ba1a0e6c6f5eceff3a4c8d6436fd28146825842f1e99454c27a6a7ad`\r\n\r\n**Use boundary:** The four statements and timing advice were captured; the answer-key heading has no captured answers. Do not invent a recovered key.\r\n\r\n#### Quick Facts\r\n\r\n##### Will This Help Me?\r\n\r\nThis will help you if\r\n\r\nYou take longer than 5 seconds to diagram Unless or you make mistakes diagramming Unless greater than 1% of the time\r\n\r\nSkip for now if\r\n\r\nYou diagram Unless automatically in less than 5 seconds 100% of the time\r\n\r\nchallenge indexPretty Chill\r\n\r\nlevelBeginner\r\n\r\nminimum time3+ minutes\r\n\r\ntiming compatible withStopwatch\r\n\r\n#### Instructions\r\n\r\n1. Diagram three out of these four Unless statements:\r\n\t1. ~A, unless B.\r\n\t\t2. Unless B, A.\r\n\t\t3. Unless ~B, ~A.\r\n\t\t4. A, unless ~B.\r\n2. Switch up which three statements you diagram every time.\r\n3. Start every LSAT study session with this quick workout until you can diagram Unless accurately in under 5 seconds.\r\n\r\nSections to use\r\n\r\nDoesn\'t Use a Section\r\n\r\nhow to time\r\n\r\nCount-Up Stopwatch\r\n\r\ntime goal to move on\r\n\r\n15 sec\r\n\r\n#### Ellen\'s Advice\r\n\r\n##### If You Want to Win\r\n\r\n- Don\'t simply memorize the correct diagrams. Diagram each statement fresh every time.\r\n- Change the letters in the statements.\r\n- You\'re working too hard to lose points on your real LSAT because you didn\'t really get Unless.\r\n\r\n##### Timing Tips\r\n\r\n- It shouldn\'t take you more than five seconds to diagram an individual Unless statement once you really understand how Unless works.\r\n- The entire Routine shouldn\'t take you more than 15 seconds total once you\'re trained\r\n\t- I just diagrammed three Unless statements in 9 seconds to prove it can be done.\r\n- If the Unless Practice Routine takes you much longer than 15 seconds, it just shows how much you need to do it\r\n- Once you hit the point where you can diagram three Unless statements in 15 seconds, you can lower your Unless Practice Routine frequency to once per week.\r\n\r\n#### Real Examples\r\n\r\n***Unless Practice Routine Answer Key***\r\n\r\n## Wrong Answer Journal\r\n\r\n[Captured source](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/workouts/Wrong Answer Journal • LO.md>) · SHA-256 `30513ab4adc6a393a6a5cfa24f759d1189f5ce173e590e5749966bd611e6afba`\r\n\r\n**Use boundary:** This is an attributed source explanation. Verify logical inferences independently and use current Joshua curriculum for student-facing delivery; source anecdotes and mechanism claims are not independently established.\r\n\r\n#### Quick Facts\r\n\r\n##### Will This Help Me?\r\n\r\nThis will help you if\r\n\r\nYou need to improve your accuracy and stop making the same mistakes over and over again.\r\n\r\nSkip for now if\r\n\r\nWrong Answer Journals are harmless.\r\n\r\nchallenge indexPretty Chill\r\n\r\nlevelBeginner\r\n\r\nminimum time15+ minutes\r\n\r\ntiming compatible withNot Timed\r\n\r\n#### Instructions\r\n\r\n1. After you\'ve Camoed your LR or RC section, begin the Wrong Answer Journaling process.\r\n2. For each wrong answer, fill in the date you did the section, the question number that was wrong, and the answer you chose.\r\n\t1. This information will already be filled in for you if you Camo using Loophole Online.\r\n3. Fill in your Reason Missed column\r\n\t1. In this column, write down **what you did wrong that caused you to miss this question**.\r\n\t\t1. Ask yourself, "Why did I think that this wrong answer was right? Why did I think that this right answer was wrong?" Focus on changing the actions that enabled these mistaken judgments.\r\n\t\t2. The first word of every entry in this column should be "I."\r\n\t\t1. Example: "I misidentified the question stem."\r\n\t\t\t\t2. This will force you to reflect on **what you did** that caused you to choose the wrong answer and overlook the right answer.\r\n4. Fill in your Solution column\r\n\t1. In this column, write down **what you will do differently to ensure you don\'t make this same mistake again**.\r\n\t\t1. Ask yourself, "What could I have done that would have made me realize in the moment that the wrong answer is wrong? What could I have done that would have made me realize in the moment that the right answer is right?"\r\n\t\t2. The first words of every entry in this column should be "I will..."\r\n\t\t1. Example: "I will do Question Stem Training to improve my ability to correctly identify question types."\r\n\t\t3. Frame your Solution Column as a direct response to the Reason Missed column.\r\n\r\nClick [here](https://new.theloophole.com/review-and-camo/wrong-answer-journal) to go to your Wrong Answer Journal\r\n\r\nvideo walkthrough\r\n\r\n[How to Use the Wrong Answer Journal](https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/accuracy/video/how-to-use-the-wrong-answer-journal)\r\n\r\nSections to use\r\n\r\nPreviously Seen Sections Only\r\n\r\nhow to time\r\n\r\nUntimed\r\n\r\n#### Ellen\'s Advice\r\n\r\n##### If You Want to Win\r\n\r\n- Keep your Wrong Answer Journal entries short and sweet. One to two sentences is enough for each column.\r\n- **Do not waste time in your Wrong Answer Journal writing down detailed explanations of why the answer you chose was wrong, and why the right answer is right in the exact context of the question.**\r\n\t- If you\'re writing anything that cannot be ported directly to subsequent sections, you are wasting your Wrong Answer Journal time.\r\n- The Wrong Answer Journal gives you a chance to identify the exact **actions you took** that made you make the wrong decision. It then gives you the opportunity to decide which actions you will take in the future that will enable you to make correct decisions. This is the essence of improvement on the LSAT.\r\n\t- I want you to know why an answer is right and another is wrong, but I don\'t want you to stop there. I want you to start there.\r\n\t\t- After you know why the correct answer is correct and why the wrong answer is wrong, look more deeply at your actions and mindset in relation to the question.\r\n\t\t- What did you do that caused this result? And what will you do to ensure a different result next time?\r\n- Do not rely on cheap explanations in your Reason Missed column like "silly mistake," "misread," or "rushing."\r\n\t- Dive into why you made this silly mistake, why you misread, and why you were rushing.\r\n\t\t- This is how you can identify the problematic actions that lead to problematic judgments and change them for the better.\r\n\r\n'}]

Verify logical inferences independently and use current Joshua curriculum for student-facing delivery; source anecdotes and mechanism claims are not independently established.\r\n\r\n#### PT135.S1.Q5 Read-Translate-Quip Example\r\n\r\n- 0:00All right, let\'s jump into a nice little RTQ demo together now. And so I\'m just going to run to a stimulus and I will show you exactly what it looks like to RTQ individual pieces of text.\r\n- 0:11And so each sentence that I read, or if I wanted to cut it sooner and just do a portion of a sentence, that would also be okay.\r\n- 0:17I will read it, I will translate it, and I will quip it, and then I will repeat that process on the next chunk of text that I get to.\r\n- 0:23And you\'ll see here that it can actually turn into a nice fluid process. And so I\'m just going to go through it and do it in a nice, slowed down way, the way I normally would.\r\n- 0:31And then after I\'m done with the stimulus, we will talk through what each step was, why it was there, how it worked, all that kind of good stuff.\r\n- 0:37And so let\'s give it a try. All right. So in its coverage of a controversy regarding a proposal to build a new freeway, a television news program showed interviews with several people who would be affected by the proposed freeway.\r\n- 0:48Okay. So we have this TV news program and it\'s covering this controversy, around a proposed new freeway in a given community.\r\n- 0:55And they\'re interviewing people who would be affected by this new freeway. And it\'s like, oh, you know, a bunch of people probably complaining about like noise, pollution and stuff.\r\n- 1:01Right. I\'m like, oh, damn with the freeway, my property value. Don\'t worry about the question stem or answer choices.\r\n\r\n## Assumptions & The Objection\r\n\r\n[Captured source](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Assumptions & The Objection.md>) · SHA-256 `382be95b9a3d949ec1b7a69753fa58b8d3f0c954f61fdfe6c900f51c29670cfa`\r\n\r\n**Use boundary:** Chapter questions 1, 2 and 6 were not captured. The old local image link is unavailable. Preserve the source-specific Objection label; use the current homework as the teaching authority.\r\n\r\n### Chapter 5 — Assumptions & The Objection\r\n\r\n*Transcribed from the quiz screenshots in `Untitled 3.md` (unavailable source attachment). "Loophole" is rendered throughout as "Objection"; everything else is verbatim. Questions 1, 2, and 6 were not captured.*\r\n\r\n#### Question 3\r\n\r\n*Hoodoo and Keith are kitten best friends who routinely engage in mischievous conduct spanning everything from play-wrestling to napping on radiators. As a pair, they have already gnawed through half a cigar, gotten trapped under an area rug, and claimed the counter as their own. Therefore, Hoodoo and Keith knocked over a vase of daffodils.*\r\n\r\nWhich one of the following is not an Objection in the argument above?\r\n\r\n- A. What if kitten best friends aren\'t strong enough to knock over a vase?\r\n- **B. What if knocking over a vase of daffodils doesn\'t qualify as mischievous conduct?** ✅\r\n- C. What if Hoodoo and Keith have never had access to a vase of daffodils?\r\n\r\n#### Question 4\r\n\r\nHow do you design an Objection?\r\n\r\n- A. What if the conclusion is not true?\r\n- B. What if one of the premises is not really true?\r\n- **C. What if [really bad thing for the argument]?** ✅\r\n\r\n#### Question 5\r\n\r\nHow will you know if your Objection works?\r\n\r\n- **A. If the Objection is possible, the conclusion is no longer proven.** ✅\r\n- B. The Objection works as a valid prephrase.\r\n- C. The Objection addresses key concepts in the argument.\r\n\r\n#### Question 7\r\n\r\n*Keith is afraid of new people and gets stressed in new environments. He is spending the weekend at his aunt\'s house for the first time, so he will hide all weekend.*\r\n\r\nWhich one of the following is an Objection for the argument above?\r\n\r\n- A. What if Keith won\'t hide?\r\n- **B. What if Keith doesn\'t hide when he\'s stressed?** ✅\r\n- C. What if Keith\'s aunt cancels their weekend plans?\r\n\r\n#### Question 8 — explanation\r\n\r\n*Keith is afraid of new people and gets stressed in new environments. He is spending the weekend at his aunt\'s house for the first time, so he will hide all weekend.*\r\n\r\n*What if Keith\'s aunt cancels their weekend plans?*\r\nThe premises told us that Keith is spending the weekend at his aunt\'s house. We can\'t negate the premises.\r\n\r\n*What if Keith won\'t hide?*\r\nThe conclusion claims that Keith will hide. We can\'t just negate the conclusion and call it an Objection.\r\n\r\n#### Question 9\r\n\r\n*Hoodoo and Keith love to watch reality TV and paw at the speaker when people speak. This proves that all felines do in fact respond to language, since Hoodoo and Keith are house cats.*\r\n\r\nWhich one of the following is an Objection for the argument above?\r\n\r\n- A. What if Hoodoo and Keith don\'t really understand language but instead are simply responding to the sounds of reality TV?\r\n- B. What if Hoodoo and Keith are becoming more like wild cats?\r\n- **C. What if Hoodoo and Keith are not representative of all felines?** ✅\r\n\r\n#### Question 10 — explanation\r\n\r\n*Hoodoo and Keith love to watch reality TV and paw at the speaker when people speak. This proves that all felines do in fact respond to language, since Hoodoo and Keith are house cats.*\r\n\r\n*What if Hoodoo and Keith don\'t really understand language but instead are simply responding to the sounds of reality TV?*\r\nThe argument only assumes that Hoodoo and Keith "respond" to language, not that they understand language. Pointing out that Hoodoo and Keith may not understand language does not affect the validity of the argument.\r\n\r\n*What if Hoodoo and Keith are becoming more like wild cats?*\r\nHoodoo and Keith becoming more like wild cats does not affect whether or not we can generalize from their behaviors to those of all felines.\r\n\r\n## Objection Mini-Quiz\r\n\r\n[Captured source](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Objection Mini-Quiz.md>) · SHA-256 `e3a49c41e5a30387f6e1c71b9a32139787bb09b8b889a77d0b2c1f6597deca0e`\r\n\r\n**Use boundary:** This is a five-item derivative of the chapter capture. It is retained as an alternate practice presentation, not a second authoritative answer key.\r\n\r\n### Objection — Mini-Quiz\r\n\r\n5 questions. Pick one answer each. Answer key at the bottom.\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n##### 1\r\n\r\n*Hoodoo and Keith are kitten best friends who routinely engage in mischievous conduct spanning everything from play-wrestling to napping on radiators. As a pair, they have already gnawed through half a cigar, gotten trapped under an area rug, and claimed the counter as their own. Therefore, Hoodoo and Keith knocked over a vase of daffodils.*\r\n\r\nWhich one of the following is **not** an Objection in the argument above?\r\n\r\n- A. What if kitten best friends aren\'t strong enough to knock over a vase?\r\n- B. What if knocking over a vase of daffodils doesn\'t qualify as mischievous conduct?\r\n- C. What if Hoodoo and Keith have never had access to a vase of daffodils?\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n##### 2\r\n\r\nHow do you design an Objection?\r\n\r\n- A. What if the conclusion is not true?\r\n- B. What if one of the premises is not really true?\r\n- C. What if [really bad thing for the argument]?\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n##### 3\r\n\r\nHow will you know if your Objection works?\r\n\r\n- A. If the Objection is possible, the conclusion is no longer proven.\r\n- B. The Objection works as a valid prephrase.\r\n- C. The Objection addresses key concepts in the argument.\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n##### 4\r\n\r\n*Keith is afraid of new people and gets stressed in new environments. He is spending the weekend at his aunt\'s house for the first time, so he will hide all weekend.*\r\n\r\nWhich one of the following is an Objection for the argument above?\r\n\r\n- A. What if Keith won\'t hide?\r\n- B. What if Keith doesn\'t hide when he\'s stressed?\r\n- C. What if Keith\'s aunt cancels their weekend plans?\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n##### 5\r\n\r\n*Hoodoo and Keith love to watch reality TV and paw at the speaker when people speak. This proves that all felines do in fact respond to language, since Hoodoo and Keith are house cats.*\r\n\r\nWhich one of the following is an Objection for the argument above?\r\n\r\n- A. What if Hoodoo and Keith don\'t really understand language but instead are simply responding to the sounds of reality TV?\r\n- B. What if Hoodoo and Keith are becoming more like wild cats?\r\n- C. What if Hoodoo and Keith are not representative of all felines?\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n#### Answer key\r\n\r\n**1. B** — the other two attack the argument; this one doesn\'t.\r\n\r\n**2. C** — you don\'t negate the conclusion or the premises. You ask what would wreck the argument.\r\n\r\n**3. A** — if the Objection is possible and the conclusion no longer follows, it works.\r\n\r\n**4. B** — A negates the conclusion, C negates a premise. B breaks the link between stress and hiding.\r\n\r\n**5. C** — A: the argument only claims they respond, not that they understand. B: turning into wild cats doesn\'t touch whether you can generalize. C: kills the generalization from two house cats to all felines.\r\n\r\n'}]

`c81991cde0977493316408f9bd138296a3415ead96517632fd5dbb5aaf4c8697`\r\n\r\n**Use boundary:** This is an attributed source explanation. Verify logical inferences independently and use current Joshua curriculum for student-facing delivery; source anecdotes and mechanism claims are not independently established.\r\n\r\n#### But I Have To Go Fast: Intention Controls Retention\r\n\r\n- 0:00Everybody loves a rhyme. Intention controls retention. And this is something I want you to keep in mind as you start marinating in the fact that you feel like you need to go faster through this process, faster through a sentence, faster through a stimulus, is that our intentions as we are going\r\n- 0:17through a stimulus, a question, a passage, an answer choice, whatever it might be, our intention controls our retention. If our intention is only to be fast, well, the brain will cooperate, right?\r\n- 0:29The brain will start just reading topic sentences, scanning for relevant parts, eliminating subvocalization, knowing only that it\'s about coins, things like that.\r\n- 0:37It\'s gonna skip over anything that seems like a speed bump, even if that thing is actually quite necessary to knowing what happened in the text that you\'re reading.\r\n- 0:46And this sort of process we call ghost reading, right? It\'s what happens when you\'re reading only to be done rather than reading to actually retain information.\r\n- 0:55But what I want you to think about right now is a replacement metric of what our intention could be that isn\'t just going faster.\r\n- 1:03A pitch that I will make to you is that we want to replace the obsession with going faster and just to be done with going faster toward insight. We want to replace speed to done with a speed to insight metric.\r\n- 1:18And so what is insight? Insight is having a kind of epiphany moment of putting information together in a way that analyzes what is going on, concretizes it in some way, works with it in a way that will be helpful to you as you build the world of the stimulus analytically and you move yourself\r\n- 1:37toward the kinds of aha, eureka moments that are going to be intrinsic to choosing the correct answer. And that is what the RTQ process is designed to do.\r\n- 1:45It\'s designed to optimize mean time from text to insight. And so as you go through your RTQ process, you\'re going to have to get used to optimizing for insight as you go,\r\n- 1:57right? And well, that\'s what we all want out of this process anyway. We want it to help us get the right answer.\r\n- 2:02So it only makes sense to try and get the right answer, meaning have insights regarding the text that you\'re reading as quickly as possible.\r\n- 2:12And of course, in the beginning, like we\'ve talked about in our clipping videos, it can be a little bit difficult to have those insights as you\'re going through text.\r\n- 2:21It can be difficult to just translate quickly at all. And you might be asking, okay, but how do I get faster?\r\n- 2:29All this stuff about insights, it sounds great, Ellen, to be running toward the correct answer as I\'m in the stimulus, but how do I actually do that fast? Because\r\n- 2:36for me, I\'m just there, I read a text, and then, I mean, maybe I can translate it, but that whole like quipping insight moment, I\'m just like, I don\'t know what to say.\r\n- 2:45And so the first thing I would tell you is, don\'t skip it. Actually say something. But I would pose an analogous question, which is why do some people run faster than others? Right?\r\n- 2:58It\'s because they\'re in better shape. And how do you get in better shape? You run more. I mean, that\'s pretty much the only way.\r\n- 3:05Maybe you\'re doing the stair master or an elliptical, or you\'re at the gym doing some other kind of fitness activity. Maybe you\'re a swimmer, who knows? But you\'re doing some kind of fitness activity.\r\n- 3:15When people do an RTQ drill with only the purpose of being done and essentially like pantomiming through the drill, but not really actually translating like to the fullest extent of the law, actually quipping to the fullest extent of the law. What they\'re doing is essentially driving to the end\r\n- 3:32of the race and hopping over the finish line and saying, look, I did it, I\'m done. And that\'s cool and everything, but I get that you want to be done. And don\'t we all maybe now, I don\'t really wanna be done. I\'m sorry, I wanna empathize with you right now, but I really don\'t wanna\r\n- 3:49be done. So that\'s fine to feel, but it\'s not really going to make you better in terms of coming to right answer your eureka moments faster, easier, more intuitively, more effortlessly,\r\n- 4:01because here\'s the dirty secret about speed. The worst you are, the more obsessive you are about getting faster. That the more of a conscious thought it is, if I need to go fast,\r\n- 4:13I need to go fast, I need to go fast. And I\'m really sorry to say this, but when you\'re a toddler, you need to toddle.\r\n- 4:20You need to let yourself have that moment of, oh, I don\'t know what to say next in this Translation. I don\'t know what to say next in this quip.\r\n- 4:26And you need to force your brain to build those neural connections, to actually come up with the quip instead of just moving on because you don\'t have time.\r\n- 4:35No, this is the part of your prep where you do have time and you\'ve got to force your brain to do it.\r\n- 4:41And this is just like, why do some people run faster? Because when they were on their last mile and it was hot and they had a blister, you know what?\r\n- 4:48They continued to run. And experts who, you know, they\'re fast and natural at this, they don\'t actually care that much about going fast.\r\n- 4:58And you know why? Because they can finish in time naturally. They just go at the pace that they go and everything is fine.\r\n- 5:03And so they don\'t actually think that much about the time. I mean, I have later videos about how you know, thinking about time as a way to go slower.\r\n- 5:10So like, don\'t allow that talking by your conscious process. But I also, I just want to reiterate one thing for you, which is if you\'re obsessed with this kind of mean time to done metric, then you\'re optimizing yourself to do as little as possible, as quickly as possible, which no wonder we don\'t retain\r\n- 5:26much of what we read, right? Engaging in this quest to push you faster and faster in the vain hope you\'re going to get more for less.\r\n- 5:34You won\'t. Like we can only get so good at not reading while we\'re reading, you know, there\'s kind of a cap there.\r\n- 5:43And so I hope that this convinces you that as you\'re doing these RTQ drills, that you want to optimize for your ability to have takeaways during the stimulus that lead you toward that quest for the correct answer.\r\n- 5:57And I understand we\'re doing this all out of order. You know, you don\'t know what the correct answer is, obviously, when you\'re reading the first sentence of the stimulus, but I will tell you that the quiping and Translation process is optimized to lead you on a quicker mean time to insight metric.\r\n- 6:13And when you are reading those words, that is the moment where you need to have that quip, have that takeaway, have that engagement with the text, because wisdom is really ready to die on the vine.\r\n- 6:25You are going to have the easiest time getting wisdom, insights, actionable takeaways out of what you\'ve read when you have first read that thing.\r\n- 6:32Like I recently skimmed, I skimmed, this book, it wasn\'t that great, to be honest.\r\n- 6:39I skimmed this book called, some business book, I forget what it\'s called actually, but it\'s the author, the thing that really stood out to me was he stopped the\r\n- 6:49chapter and just literally begged people to stop what they were doing right now and open a bank account. And he was so insistent because he was like, you\'re going to get so much more out of this book\r\n- 6:59if you actually stop what you are doing right now and do not read anymore, go and do it. And then you go to the next sentence, he\'d be like, did you do it?\r\n- 7:08Did you do it? He knows that that action step, and this is what I\'m likening to that quip stage of the RTQ process, the takeaway stage, the actionable insight stage, that that part is so essential that without that, the book just isn\'t going to be that effective.\r\n- 7:25And so when you are tempted as you\'re going through your drills to just move on and be like, oh, this is annoying,\r\n- 7:33I don\'t really care or want to do this, whatever, I want you to think about what in that moment are you optimizing for?\r\n- 7:40What is your intention? Is your intention just to be done, but you don\'t really care about your LSAT score? Or is your intention to actually uplift these skills so that you can get a better LSAT score, more questions right, eventually quicker, but you got to toddle while you toddle.\r\n- 7:55And so if you\'re like, listen, girl, I just want to be done and I\'m cool. All right, cool, I respect you.\r\n- 8:01Everybody can have their own journey here. But if your goal is to really get questions right, faster, easier, and you want it to be that intuitive analytical process that I\'m talking about, give yourself the grace to breathe while you\'re doing this and don\'t give up just because things don\'t come easy\r\n- 8:18in the moment. I believe in you, I know you will grow and change. You just got to be a little bit stubborn with yourself.\r\n- 8:23All right, I\'ll see you soon.\r\n\r\n## Dont Hesitate Between the Steps\r\n\r\n[Captured source](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/translation/Dont Hesitate Between the Steps • LO.md>) · SHA-256 `9653d5950aec7f136c5b1ae2b2d3d7ebd6a5f0856591d39d800b6dd4daedf89f`\r\n\r\n**Use boundary:** This is an attributed source explanation. Verify logical inferences independently and use current Joshua curriculum for student-facing delivery; source anecdotes and mechanism claims are not independently established.\r\n\r\n#### Don\'t Hesitate Between the Steps\r\n\r\n- 0:00Don\'t hesitate between the steps. This can be a huge source of Translation, uh, and CLIR time lag where you are unable to hit your time goal for much longer because you\'re hesitating between the steps.\r\n- 0:13But, hesitating between the steps also means you aren\'t really doing the process as prescribed. And so, in, you know, the ideal world, the Translation CLIR process is your thinking process.\r\n- 0:24There is no not, like, another governing process on top of the Translation CLIR process. And so, we want everything that\'s coming out of your mouth as you\'re translating and CLIRing to be your raw unfiltered thoughts.\r\n- 0:37You shouldn\'t have a pause where you\'re like, oh, let me come up with a good CLIR. Oh, what about this?\r\n- 0:42What about this all silently? Because all of that processing should be your out loud- well. Like, quipping, and things like that.\r\n- 0:50If you\'re thinking before you come up with a good quip- that thinking actually is your quip. You know? But the issue is, if that thinking is not actually directly engaging with your Translation, engaging with the content of the stimulus, it\'s not very good quipping.\r\n- 1:03It\'s not very useful quipping. I know you can do it, and I\'ll see you soon.\r\n\r\n## How and Why to Do Translation Plus CLIR Drills\r\n\r\n[Captured source](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/translation/How and Why to Do Translation Plus CLIR Drills • LO.md>) · SHA-256 `28551070f63aea60181ca2eadf1163e07d65665fd720e5e483e6f713e5340503`\r\n\r\n**Use boundary:** This is an attributed source explanation. Verify logical inferences independently and use current Joshua curriculum for student-facing delivery; source anecdotes and mechanism claims are not independently established.\r\n\r\n##### Ellen\'s Notes\r\n\r\n***Translation + CLIR Drills***\r\n\r\n1. Read and translate the stimulus in chunks.\r\n2. Quip each chunk of the stimulus after you translate.\r\n3. Continue this process on every stimulus chunk until you reach the end of stimulus.\r\n4. Immediately proceed to the CLIR after your final quip of the stimulus (or the final quip is the CLIR).\r\n\r\n***Translation + CLIR Drill Tips***\r\n\r\n- Perform all steps of the drill out loud, and record your drills with a voice recorder.\r\n\t- You will eventually transition this process to silent, but the time goals are built around an outloud process.\r\n- Press "record" at the very beginning, and do not end the recording until you have finished with the entire drill.\r\n- Do not pause or stop the recording between steps or stimuli.\r\n- No hyperskipping on Translation + CLIR Drills.\r\n\r\n***Translation + CLIR Goals***\r\n\r\n- The time goal for Translation + CLIR is 20 minutes.\r\n- The accommodated time goal for Translation + CLIR is 30 minutes (or 25 minutes as an ideal stretch goal).\r\n- Meeting this time goal is only valid under two conditions:\r\n\t- You have a legitimate CLIR for every stimulus.\r\n\t\t- You have translated and quipped every stimulus in the section.\r\n\r\n## How and Why to Quip the Answer Choices\r\n\r\n[Captured source](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/translation/How and Why to Quip the Answer Choices • LO.md>) · SHA-256 `abdeee9e23f799e2478ca461041666076b9040a42d3a2748aa8f0f3c0f648ce1`\r\n\r\n**Use boundary:** This is an attributed source explanation. Verify logical inferences independently and use current Joshua curriculum for student-facing delivery; source anecdotes and mechanism claims are not independently established.\r\n\r\n#### How and Why to Quip the Answer Choices\r\n\r\n- 0:00how and why to quip the answer choices. This is one of those things that essentially no one wants to do.\r\n- 0:06Nobody wants to quip the answer choices, but the quipping of the answer choices to me is actually the highlight slash most important moment in the doing of any question,\r\n- 0:18because the quipping of the answer choices is when you get the chance to figure out whether that answer is right with actual, you know, real data as opposed to just kind of skipping to the end.\r\n- 0:30And so let me explain. Let me explain. I want people to quip the answers in a certain way.\r\n- 0:35Obviously, you read the answer, you translate the answer, you get to that quipping stage. In that quipping stage, ideally, you\'re going to quip that answer through melding together the information from the stimulus with the answer choice\r\n- 0:48and having some sort of commentary that\'s very much informed by the stimulus. You\'re bringing to bear a lot of the concepts, even the phrases that are included in the\r\n- 0:58you bring them down into the answer choices for that little roundtable discussion that just involves you and no one else at a roundtable.\r\n- 1:04And in that moment, you are conceptually engaging with the answer choice according to what the question is really asking,\r\n- 1:12but using evidence from the stimulus to have that interesting discussion. And so when you\'re having that interesting discussion, you\'re actually forming the basis of evidence for a judgment.\r\n- 1:23And it\'s really important. As we would kind of know from just the LSAT and like circular reasoning and things, it\'s really important to have evidence for a judgment or a claim or a Conclusion before you go to that judgment, claim or Conclusion.\r\n- 1:39And this presumption, which I feel like we can all agree on, right? You would rather start with evidence and then move toward a claim rather than starting with a claim and moving toward evidence.\r\n- 1:50You\'re just more likely to get a valid claim or Conclusion out of that sort of arrangement, right? When you start with evidence rather than starting with the claim.\r\n- 1:59However, when most people engage with LSAT answer choices, they do not want to start with evidence. In fact, they want to read it very read the answer choice very quickly, silently, and they want to immediately have a right wrong judgment.\r\n- 2:12And that right wrong judgment has to be, I guess, informed by like magic or intuition or liking or disliking or all kinds of things that are not actual metrics or actual evidence.\r\n- 2:24They will see when people do demos and things like that on video, they\'ll be like, oh, you have to know the fate of the answer immediately.\r\n- 2:31You can\'t have any sort of processing of the answer. You have to just read it and immediately and intuitively know if it\'s right or wrong.\r\n- 2:37And while that is a wonderful goal and perhaps it is something that could happen on some answer choices in the future.\r\n- 2:43And it\'s something like, I know what that feels like. It\'s not like I\'m saying it doesn\'t exist. If you were right now trying to remedy a big score gap, doing an impression of somebody who\'s at like expert level, who made a video and instantly and intuitively saw why an answer is right or wrong, doing\r\n- 3:00an impression of them where you read an answer and you\'re like, yeah, so it\'s wrong. That\'s not going to get you anywhere\r\n- 3:06because you\'re right there forcing knowledge that you don\'t actually have. You\'re forcing a judgment that you don\'t actually really have data for. The\r\n- 3:15people feel so much pressure to make that judgment really quickly that they skip the information analysis stage. And that\'s what really the quipping of the answer choices is.\r\n- 3:26It\'s that information analysis stage in which like this is the meat of doing the question, right? I mean, this is when you take the answer choice and you\'re like, hey, does this actually function\r\n- 3:36according to the question stem in regards to the stimulus? And in a way, what I\'m asking there of like the level of engagement I\'m asking for, it is asking, is this right?\r\n- 3:45But it\'s not asking, is this right? By literally saying the words, is this right? Because the words, is this right? Is like way too referential, way too generalized.\r\n- 3:54It\'s going to lead to very kind of cheap answer choices of like, I don\'t like this. This one\'s good. This one could be it.\r\n- 4:00Notice that there\'s like no content to those answers. They\'re like mass market generalized quips. Like I talked about in my last video where you could just pick them up and put them on a different stimulus or not a different stimulus, a different answer choice. And they would make\r\n- 4:15just as much sense. You want to stay away from that sort of quipping because it means you aren\'t really engaging with what you read and you don\'t have real evidence that\'s informed by specificity.\r\n- 4:24And you might be saying to yourself like, oh, you know, how do I have time for this? Verify logical inferences independently and use current Joshua curriculum for student-facing delivery; source anecdotes and mechanism claims are not independently established.\r\n\r\n#### Quip: Time to Clap Back 💅🏽\r\n\r\n- 0:00Ah, quipping. This is the time you clap back. You actually say something back to the author inside the stimulus and tell them what you think.\r\n- 0:13You know, you just read a piece of text, you translated it, now you're in the third part of the RQTQ process, which you're going to run on every chunk of text you enCounter on the L-SAT\r\n- 0:22and it is now your job to get actively engaged and say something back to the author about what they just read.\r\n- 0:29And so this part of the process, it's a bit open-ended, right? Because saying something back, having an independent thought about what you just read, having a reaction, there are lots of ways to have reactions, right?\r\n- 0:44But there are going to be some kinds of reactions that are more useful than other kinds of reactions.\r\n- 0:50And so I will outline for you here just some quick tips of things to avoid, things to lean on as we get into our quipping journey\r\n- 0:58and then there are going to be lots more videos just devoted to quipping very soon. And so if you get all of your questions not quite answered here, don't worry,\r\n- 1:04there is more content where this came from. And so in this quipping stage, you know, I have now just internalized what I read.\r\n- 1:09You know, I've set it back to myself of like, I understand it, I'm able to retain it. They're necessarily skeptical, but they're more like, hmm, did that add up, everyone?\r\n- 1:43Because they investigate relationships between pieces of text. And you might be objecting to me, well, if I only have one piece of text that I just have to be able to get to the bottom of it. How will I ever quip it?\r\n- 1:56Because it's only one thing. It doesn't have a relationship to anything else yet. Well, for that one, you could just have a reaction to the information.\r\n- 2:02You can make some kind of joke. You could come up with an example. You could laugh about it. And like, honestly, like an emotion is a quip because an emotion is a reaction.\r\n- 2:13You only have emotions about things that you actually understand, which leads me to what the genesis of quipping was.\r\n- 2:20I think it's kind of useful for you to understand why quipping exists so that you can do it most effectively.\r\n- 2:26Quipping exists because I was watching someone translate in 2019 and I heard him. I heard the words as he intoned his Translation. There was just no music there.\r\n- 2:34You know, it was I likened it to an empty cardboard box. Like every word was an empty cardboard box. And you knew that he was saying the word, but he had no idea what was inside that cardboard box.\r\n- 2:45He just didn't really understand the significance of any of the words he was saying. Even if he was saying the right words, you could hear that they were empty.\r\n- 2:52And so I stopped him and I told him to go back and I was just like, OK, yeah, yeah, this time, you know, you're saying everything right.\r\n- 2:58Just do one more thing for me of I want you to read and I want you to translate. And this time I want you to say something about what you just read after you've read it.\r\n- 3:06And he was like, well, what do you want me to say? I was like, well, it could be anything. Just something about what you read.\r\n- 3:09Just have a feeling, have a thought, have a critique, whatever you want to say. And he did that. And the magic thing that happened was not that, you know, his quip was so genius.\r\n- 3:19It was that the empty cardboard box feeling of the Translation went away because by having to know what the Translation actually meant in order to quip, then he was forced to understand what the Translation really meant on a deep level,\r\n- 3:37because essentially the brain is going to optimize away anything that isn't immediately used in a process as superfluous, not really needed. And it's going to find the lowest common denominator, the least effort version of everything.\r\n- 3:48And so it'll be like, oh, I'm saying the words of this Translation, but unless I'm actually going to do something with those words very, very quickly, again, die on the vine sort of thing where I will technically pantomime the correct words, but they won't get us where we're trying to go\r\n- 4:03by making you come up with an independent thought about what you just read. We ensure that that Translation is moving towards the right direction, which is really understanding real nuanced information.\r\n- 4:14And so why are these quips so useful, though, just in and of themselves beyond the impact they have on the quality of the Translation stage?\r\n- 4:22It's because our memory indexes on relationships like I'll give you one example to show you what I mean here.\r\n- 4:30I want you to memorize four, four, four, four, four, four, four, four. That's when your subconscious gets to come into play.\r\n- 8:32And when you're in that quip stage, you get used to consistently asking your subconscious for output. Source percentages and advice to lock in a wrong guess are not established facts or adopted doctrine.\r\n\r\n#### Take a Guess at Words You Don't Know\r\n\r\n- 0:00Take a guess at words you don't know. This is so important because I'm going to outline for you a problem that I see a lot when people go through translating and they get to a word that they do not know.\r\n- 0:11When people are reading and they see a word they don't know, very often what they will do is just glaze over that word and keep on going. And what they want to do is keep on reading, just blowing past this word they don't know, and hoping that later on the rest of the text will help me understand what that word meant.\r\n- 0:53So let me tell you why this doesn't work and why it's harmful. Because when you allow that word you don't know to permeate and become this big black hole that like, oh, I didn't know that word, so now I don't know anything really surrounding it either, there tends to be an avoidant mentality\r\n- 1:09that accompanies that. That people generally feel uncomfortable acknowledging that there is a word they don't know in the moment and so they don't really want to sit in that kind of icky, uncomfortable feeling and so they want to move forward. And the later paragraphs or whatever it is, they do not actually tell you what the word meant and you shouldn't pretend that actually going on is really in service of knowing what the word meant because it isn't\r\n- 1:54actually true. OK, you're allowed to read like to the end of the sentence, the end of the thought for sure and then but when you get to the end of that sentence, I want you to go back and I want you to take an educated guess on what that\r\n- 2:37word means. text(await tools.exec_command({cmd:"@'\nfrom pathlib import Path\nimport re,hashlib\nbase=Path('corpus/loophole-online')\nfor p in base.glob('*.generated.md'):\n expected=hashlib.sha256(p.read_bytes()).hexdigest();t=p.read_text(encoding='utf-8')\n t=re.sub(r'(?m)^subject: (.+)$',lambda m:'subject: \"'+m.group(1).replace('\"','\\\\\"')+'\"',t)\n t=re.sub(r'\\]\\((/[^)]+)\\)',r'](https://new.theloophole.com\\1)',t)\n t=re.sub(r'(?m)^###### In This Training Block\\s*\\n','',t)\n t=re.sub(r'(?m)^##### Captured instructor notes\\s*\\n(?=## |\\Z)','',t)\n if p.name=='worked-examples.generated.md':\n  start=t.index('## Objection Mini-Quiz\\n',t.index('## Assumptions & The Objection\\n'))\n  before=t[:start];tail=t[start:];head=tail[:tail.index('### Objection')]\n  t=before+head+'The derivative renumbers chapter questions 3, 4, 5, 7 and 9 as 1 through 5. Its corresponding answer key is B, C, A, B, C. The repeated question bodies are retained once in the preceding chapter section; no unique explanation is lost. Its short key reinforces: do not negate a premise or merely assert the conclusion false; break stress-to-hiding support; test representativeness without replacing “respond” with “understand.”\\n'\n  marker='## PT 111 S 3 Q 3 Translation Plus CLIR'\n  stem='### Separate unidentified stimulus captured on the same page\\n\\nMost doctors recommend that pregnant women eat a nutritious diet to promote the health of their babies. However, most babies who are born to women who ate nutritious diets while pregnant still develop at least one medical problem in their first year.\\n\\nThis text is not the brake question. No question stem, choices or explanation are captured. It remains a separate illustration to inspect: promoting health does not itself promise eliminating every medical problem. This observation is our limited synthesis, not a recovered answer key.\\n\\n'\n  t=t.replace(marker,stem+marker)\n # Explicit IDs make generated TOC anchors robust to punctuation.\n t=re.sub(r'(?m)^## (?!Find a source)(.+)$',lambda m:'<a id=\"'+re.sub('[^a-z0-9]+','-',m.group(1).lower()).strip('-')+'\"></a>\\n\\n'+m.group(0),t)\n if hashlib.sha256(p.read_bytes()).hexdigest()!=expected:raise RuntimeError('Concurrent edit '+str(p))\n p.write_text(t,encoding='utf-8')\nprint('Four generated editions repaired with compare-and-swap; duplicate quiz bodies merged and unrelated stimulus kept distinct.')\n'@ | python -",max_output_tokens:1000}));

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 0.4 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"62a259","wall_time_seconds":0.2121829,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":2654,"output":"\\r\\nFor objection practice, hold the premises fixed and identify a possible missing connection rather than just declaring the conclusion false. The recovered kitten examples distinguish stress from hiding, mischief history from this specific act, and a two-cat sample from all felines. Their response-versus-understanding distinction prevents importing a stronger conclusion than the text asserts. Use the existing [Assumption versus Objection homework](<../Curriculum/Flaw and Famous Flaws/Homework/Pop Quiz - Assumption vs Objection.md>); the [recovered example edition](worked-examples.generated.md) preserves both the incomplete chapter and its five-question derivative without replacing that homework.\\r\\n\\r\\nSources: [NA strategy](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/learn/Necessary Assumption Question Strategy • LO.md>), [NA definition examples](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/learn/What Is a Necessary Assumption • LO.md>), [Weaken strategy](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/learn/Weaken Question Strategy • LO.md>).\\r\\n\\r\\n## Review without knowing which questions were wrong\\r\\n\\r\\n**Camo:** preserve the initial answers without revealing the score or key. Reattempt a list containing misses and an unknown number of correct decoys. Restart each question completely: stimulus/passage, question task and every choice. Do not only compare the final two or preserve an initial answer out of loyalty. Then compare attempts and journal while the evidence is fresh.\\r\\n\\r\\n| First attempt | Review | Source label | What to investigate |\\r\\n|---|---|---|---|\\r\\n| Wrong | Wrong | Conceptual gap | What remains misunderstood, including a repeated process error? |\\r\\n| Wrong | Right | Misread | What changed in the reading or reasoning? |\\r\\n| Right | Wrong | Self-doubt | Why was a defensible answer abandoned? Was the first answer genuinely understood? |\\r\\n| Right | Right | Self-confidence | Can the learner explain the evidence, rather than repeating a lucky guess? |\\r\\n\\r\\nThese outcomes are clues, not proof of a single cause. Normally Cassidy waits at least 24 hours and puts Camo after new practice. The Failsafe explicitly uses immediate Camo after a five-minute break. Do not call this contradiction; it is a stated exception. Nor should a learner change an answer merely because the mixture must include misses: each change requires question-specific evidence.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Wrong Answer Journal:** record date, question and chosen answer. In one or two sentences, name what the learner did that led to the wrong acceptance and right rejection, then a directly responsive action beginning “I will.” “I misread” is insufficient without identifying the dropped or changed meaning. Establish why choices are right/wrong first; journal the transferable repair rather than another long answer explanation. If a backlog prevents current journaling, Cassidy suggests starting fresh with recent sections rather than postponing all review.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Island:** set aside questions that remain unclear after review. Revisit for five minutes per day. Move a question off only when its explanation genuinely makes sense, not merely because its answer letter is familiar. The Island is a queue for unresolved understanding, not a second answer key.\\r\\n\\r\\nSources: [Camo lesson](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/accuracy/How to Camo • LO.md>), [Camo workout](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/workouts/Camo Review • LO.md>), [journal](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/workouts/Wrong Answer Journal • LO.md>), [Island](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/concept-review/How and Why to Use the Island • LO.md>).\\r\\n\\r\\n## Use a two-success accuracy progression\\r\\n\\r\\nThe captured **Accuracy Failsafe** sets a permitted-misses goal for a new section at natural pace. After a five-minute break, do Camo. If the goal is met, attempt a second new section of the same type and repeat the break/review. Two consecutive successes tighten the permitted-misses goal the following day. A first-section failure ends that day\'s Failsafe: journal and choose a recovery exercise instead of another same-type section. A second-section failure means journal both and keep tomorrow\'s goal unchanged.\\r\\n\\r\\nSelect recovery from the observed problem. The source names Answer Choice Primer or MemAC for answer-choice translation, and Highlight Reel for strategic mistakes. Their complete instructions were not captured here, so those names are pointers, not newly reconstructed drills. The source advises recovery without more timed question answering. A proposed target progression must be tailored to the actual student rather than automatically assigned.\\r\\n\\r\\nSource: [Accuracy Failsafe](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/workouts/Accuracy Failsafe • LO.md>).\\r\\n\\r\\n## Preserve the source\'s routine targets without making them requirements\\r\\n\\r\\nThe Foundation routine orders consecutive translation/CLIR sections, extracurricular reading and vocabulary; it proposes at least three sections per day when possible, ideally six, plus a weekly eight-section marathon. Its described block is two to four hours. Supplemental suggestions are ScienceDaily for science, Aeon for general RC-like prose, Arts & Letters Daily for humanities, the NYT Guide to Essential Knowledge for background gaps, and selected Crash Course topics. These are historical source suggestions, not newly researched product recommendations.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source advances to Translation + Decaf at the midpoint between initial and target translation time. Its example is 52 minutes initially, 20-minute target, midpoint 36. Targets are 20 minutes normally, 25 for 53-minute accommodations and 30 for 70-minute accommodations in the routine. The separate drill notes use 30 accommodated with 25 as a stretch target. Preserve that distinction rather than silently reconciling it. A qualifying time requires an aloud translation and reaction to every stimulus and a legitimate CLIR for each; the source suggests approximately five CLIR misses or fewer, adjustable for goals.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe Marathon uses six to eight fresh sections, records their order and individual count-up times, defers checking until after the batch, and permits medically or biologically required breaks. The captured 400-minute platform-timer setting is an old UI workaround, not verified current behavior. Claims that a single daily section cannot improve skill or that any other break guarantees failure are not accepted as established learning science. Fatigue is observable evidence to investigate, not an automatic instruction to push harder.\\r\\n\\r\\nSources: [Foundation](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/routines/Translation Foundation Routine • LO.md>), [Marathon](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/workouts/Translation Marathon • LO.md>).\\r\\n\\r\\n## Capture CLIRs and preserve blind review\\r\\n\\r\\nDictate the question number and CLIR into an existing document when producing the CLIR, then format the numbered entries without altering the student\'s words. For a full-session transcript, marking each CLIR aloud makes extraction easier. Check against the key after the drill, without inventing missing speech. The source\'s Whisper Flow/Superwhisper/Docs/Loom claims about pricing, privacy and capabilities are historical; use verified local settings before choosing tools.\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep initial answers and timing data separate from revealed results when blind review is the purpose. The captured LawHub workaround uses self-paced tabs, avoids completion/show-answer controls and score-revealing hovers, and uses an external timer for custom durations. These exact buttons and persistence claims need a current interface check before operational use. This is not a blanket ban on exam-mode practice.\\r\\n\\r\\nSources: [transcription method](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/translation/How to Use AI to Transcribe Your Cli Rs in Translation Plus Cli Rs • LO.md>), [LawHub lesson](<../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/learn/How to Use Law Hub in a Way That Doesnt Hurt Your Prep • LO.md>).\\r\\n\\r\\n## Check a teaching reference before publishing it\\r\\n\\r\\nRecovered conditional-plan review adds a useful artifact checklist: distinguish a quick reference from a complete lesson; make lookup labels contain the words a learner searches for; check mobile-width examples; avoid hiding every essential rule in collapsed panels; keep practice separate where it duplicates teaching; verify every diagram and its complete scope; distinguish contraposition from a second independent direction; preserve permission versus actual action; avoid inventing terms such as “good” weather; check causal tendencies before rendering them as guarantees; and distinguish beginner training requirements from test-day advice.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis checklist does not approve the historical artifact, old app path, left/right-only pedagogy or reviewer examples. The review\'s claim that a class explicitly taught “the only goes left” was rejected in its own triage; do not repeat the fabricated attribution. Current approved conditional sequence and voice standard control any implementation.\\r\\n\\r\\nSources: [historical plan](../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/PLAN.md), [review and triage](../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/PLAN-REVIEW-LOG.md).\\r\\n\\r\\n## Retrieve the actual explanations and examples\\r\\n\\r\\n- [Reading and translation lectures](reading-lectures.generated.md): full captured instructional transcript and notes, with claim boundaries.\\r\\n- [Question-strategy lectures](question-strategies.generated.md): Method, conditionals, assumption and Weaken material with explicit doctrinal corrections.\\r\\n- [Workouts and routines](review-workouts.generated.md): captured ordered protocols, prerequisites, source targets and review advice.\\r\\n- [Worked examples](worked-examples.generated.md): freeway sampling, peat analogy, antilock-brake resolution, conditional forecast and kitten objection examples.\\r\\n- [Every teaching-file coverage record](../../research/obsidian-vault-comparison/2026-10-07/teaching-active-coverage.md): active destination and exclusions for each original teaching file.\\r\\n"}'}]

Missing answer keys, absent video demonstrations and image-only notes cannot be reconstructed. A source edition is not a newly published lecture or assigned student homework.\']\\r\\ncoverage.write_text(\'\\\\n\'.join(lines)+\'\\\\n\',encoding=\'utf-8\')\\r\\nprint(json.dumps({\'edition_sources\':len(records),\'matrix_rows\':len(rows),\'outputs\':[str(OUT/(g+\'.generated.md\')) for g in groups]+[str(coverage)]},indent=2))\\r\\n"}'}]

links=0\nfor p in outputs:\n t=p.read_text(encoding='utf-8')\n for rel,h in re.findall(r'\\[Captured source\\]\\(<([^>]+)>\\) · SHA-256 `([0-9a-f]{64})`',t):\n  s=(p.parent/rel).resolve(); actual=hashlib.sha256(s.read_bytes()).hexdigest()\n  if actual!=h:bad.append(str(s))\n  sources.append({'path':s.relative_to(root).as_posix(),'sha256':h,'edition':p.relative_to(root).as_posix()})\nfor p in outputs+[base/'method-library.md',base/'index.md',root/'research/obsidian-vault-comparison/2026-10-07/teaching-active-coverage.md']:\n for dest in re.findall(r'\\]\\(([^\\n]+?)\\)',p.read_text(encoding='utf-8')):\n  dest=dest.strip('<>'); filename=dest.split('#')[0]\n  if not filename or re.match(r'\\w+://',filename):continue\n  links+=1\n  if not (p.parent/filename).exists():bad.append(str(p)+': '+dest)\nreceipt={'kind':'reviewed transformation receipt','date':'2026-10-08','source_count':len(sources),'sources':sources,'outputs':[{'path':p.relative_to(root).as_posix(),'sha256':hashlib.sha256(p.read_bytes()).hexdigest()} for p in outputs], 'transformations':['Remove YAML frontmatter, video player counters, thumbnails, page navigation, related-page promotions, empty notes/training headings.','Retain instructional transcripts, captured Ellen notes, substantive question text and captured reasoning.','Normalize source heading depth and add stable explicit anchors; Changes to an edition require verifying every listed source SHA-256, checking the current output SHA-256 against this receipt before write (compare-and-swap), rerunning full content and link review, and replacing the output hash in a reviewed receipt. This receipt describes the reviewed final transformations; it is not a promise that a generic text extractor can safely reproduce editorial annotations.'}\nq=root/'research/obsidian-vault-comparison/2026-10-07/teaching-transformation-receipt.json'\nif q.exists():raise RuntimeError('Receipt already exists; review before replacing')\nq.write_text(json.dumps(receipt,indent=2,ensure_ascii=False)+'\\n',encoding='utf-8')\nprint(json.dumps({'sources':len(sources),'outputs':len(outputs),'links':links,'errors':bad}))\n'@ | python -",max_output_tokens:2000}));
